typography:
  meta:
    fontFamily: "'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif"
    fontSize: 11px
    fontWeight: 500
    lineHeight: 14px
  small:
    fontFamily: "'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif"
    fontSize: 12px
    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 16px
  body:
    fontFamily: "'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif"
    fontSize: 13px
    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 20px
  input:
    fontFamily: "'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif"
    fontSize: 13px
    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 20px
  reading:
    fontFamily: "'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif"
    fontSize: 14px
    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 21px

    fontFamily: "'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif"
    fontSize: 16px
    fontWeight: 600
    lineHeight: 22px

    fontFamily: "'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif"
    fontSize: 20px
    fontWeight: 600
    lineHeight: 26px

    fontWeight: 600

    letterSpacing: -0.02em

    fontSize: 12px
    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 16px

    fontSize: 12px
    fontWeight: 500
    lineHeight: 16px

    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 18px

    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 20px

    fontSize: 15px
    fontWeight: 400
    lineHeight: 22px

  md: 6px
  lg: 8px
  full: 9999px
spacing:

  space-4: 16px

  space-6: 24px

  control-xs: 24px
  control-sm: 28px
  control-md: 32px
  control-lg: 36px
  touch: 44px
  target-min: 24px
  row: 32px
  row-phone: 52px
  row-three-line: 72px
  group-header: 32px
  table-header: 40px
  chip: 20px
  badge: 20px
  kbd: 20px
  top-bar: 44px
  sidebar: 240px
  sidebar-collapsed: 56px
  pane: 420px
  list-column: 340px

  side-column: 320px
  property-row: 30px

  icon-lg: 20px

breakpoints:
  md: 768
  lg: 1024
  xl: 1280
  2xl: 1440
motion:
  duration-fast: 120
  duration-pane: 180
  ease-stack: "cubic-bezier(0.2, 0, 0, 1)"
chart-patterns:
  pattern-1: { type: lines, angle: 45, width: 1, pitch: 4, stagger: false }
  pattern-2: { type: dots, angle: 0, width: 3, pitch: 5, stagger: false }
  pattern-3: { type: cross, angle: 45, width: 1, pitch: 4, stagger: false }
  pattern-4: { type: squares, angle: 0, width: 3, pitch: 5, stagger: false }
  pattern-5: { type: lines, angle: 135, width: 1, pitch: 4, stagger: false }
  pattern-6: { type: dots, angle: 0, width: 2, pitch: 6, stagger: true }
  pattern-7: { type: lines, angle: 90, width: 1, pitch: 4, stagger: false }
  pattern-8: { type: squares, angle: 0, width: 4, pitch: 8, stagger: true }
theme-inputs:
  base: "oklch(1 0 0)"
  accent: "oklch(0.205 0 0)"
  contrast: 80
  density: compact
theme-fallbacks:
  selected: accent
  selected-foreground: accent-foreground
  strong-foreground: foreground
  faint-foreground: muted-foreground
  link: primary
  border-faint: border
  border-strong: muted-foreground
  success: foreground
  success-foreground: background
  warning: foreground
  warning-foreground: background
  info: foreground
  info-foreground: background
  overlay: "oklch(0 0 0 / 30%)"
  chart-6: chart-1
  chart-7: chart-2
  chart-8: chart-3
  chart-ink: foreground
  chart-heat: foreground
  tag-grey: muted-foreground
  tag-blue: muted-foreground
  tag-violet: muted-foreground
  tag-pink: muted-foreground
  tag-red: muted-foreground
  tag-orange: muted-foreground
  tag-yellow: muted-foreground
  tag-green: muted-foreground
  diff-added: muted-foreground
  diff-removed: muted-foreground
---

# STACK

## Overview

This file is the one design definition of the web apps and the admin areas that STACK serves. Every UI task starts with a read of this file. An agent in one product and an agent in another product build the same look from it. The public pages of a website are out of scope, because each site gets its own design.

The style is fast, dense, keyboard first and calm. Whitespace groups things and does not pad them. Grays carry about 95% of the screen. Color marks a state, and the one primary action of a region. The key data of a product, such as amounts, identifiers and states, is the most legible text on the screen.

The file follows the DESIGN.md format of Google Labs, spec version `alpha` (the `version` key of the front matter), and the lint of the CLI `@google/design.md` 0.4.0. A release tag of this repository carries the semantic version of the file, and `CHANGELOG.md` describes each release.

The components are shadcn-svelte 1.7.0 in the nova style, on Bits UI and Tailwind CSS 4. The component list is the registry snapshot of 2026-10-13 (`styles/nova/index.json`), which holds 61 components. Each release ships the files that STACK tested in `preview/src/lib/components/ui/`, and the exact versions of their packages in `preview/components.lock.json`. A product imports each component from the package, such as `@stack-kit/stack/ui/button`, as "Import a part" in the README says, and holds no copy of a file. It never runs `shadcn-svelte add` or `shadcn-svelte update`, because the CLI reads the live registry, which changes between two releases of STACK. The nova style draws 32 px controls, which is the density of this file.

The file describes the look in its own words. It names no outside product, and a check refuses such a name in this repository.

**How to read the front matter.**

- `colors` holds the light value of each role, and `colors.dark` holds the dark value under the same name. In dark mode, each `colors.dark.<name>` replaces `colors.<name>`. Every value is an `oklch()` string.
- `typography`, `rounded` and `spacing` hold the type steps, the radius levels and the sizes. A `<step>-phone` key of `typography` holds the value of that step below 768 px (see `## Layout`).
- `components` binds the parts of each component to the tokens. Each value is a reference, never a value. `## Components` gives the key grammar.
- Five extra keys: `breakpoints`, the width lines in px as numbers; `motion`, the durations in ms as numbers and the curve of the motion that a product writes itself (see `## Elevation & Depth`); `chart-patterns`, the pattern of each chart category; `theme-inputs`, the inputs that the theme generator takes when the command names none: a white page, the near-black primary of `default`, and the contrast level that gives the light `foreground` of `default` (see `## Themes`); and `theme-fallbacks`, the value that each added role takes in a theme that lacks it.

**The files that a product imports.** In this order: `tailwindcss`, `tw-animate-css`, `preview/src/lib/components/ui/shadcn-svelte.css`, `stack.css`, and then one theme file from `themes/`. A product with a theme switch imports no theme file, and links one instead, as "A theme switch in a product" in `## Themes` says. A script writes `stack.css`, `stack-plain.css`, `themes/default.css` and `chart-patterns.svg` from the front matter. Never edit one of them by hand: change this file, then run `npm run theme`. A product adds its own tokens in its own layer, with the prefix `product-`. When a token is general for a back office or a dashboard, the product proposes it as a pull request, as "Contribute from a product" in `README.md` says, and a later release adds it to this file.

**Content that no build sees.** A Tailwind class works only in content that the build of the product compiled. Two kinds of content get no build. One is a page with no build step. The other is a fragment that the code of the product adds while the page runs, such as an SVG picture that a script of the product draws. A picture or a mockup from an agent is never such a fragment: it shows as an image or as a page in a frame, as the last paragraph of this part says. The page links one theme file, then `stack-plain.css`, and the content uses CSS custom properties only. `stack-plain.css` holds the tokens of `stack.css` as plain custom properties, with the same names and values, so a class and its variable give one value:

- a color is `var(--color-<role>)`, such as `var(--color-card)` for `bg-card`. An added role falls back to its `theme-fallbacks` value, as in `stack.css`. An opacity modifier has the form of `## Colors`;
- a type step is `var(--text-<size>)` with `var(--text-<size>--line-height)`, and `var(--text-<size>--letter-spacing)` where the step has one. The class of each step is in `### Type steps`, so `body` is `var(--text-sm)`;
- a weight is `var(--font-weight-normal)`, `var(--font-weight-medium)` or `var(--font-weight-semibold)`, as `font-normal`, `font-medium` and `font-semibold`. The fonts are `var(--font-sans)` and `var(--font-mono)`;
- a radius is `var(--radius-sm)` to `var(--radius-4xl)`, as `rounded-sm` to `rounded-4xl`, and `var(--radius-full)` for `rounded.full`;
- a duration of `motion` is `var(--transition-duration-<name>)`, such as `var(--transition-duration-fast)` for `duration-fast`, and the curve is `var(--ease-stack)` for `ease-stack`;
- a key of `spacing` is `var(--spacing-<key>)`, such as `var(--spacing-space-4)` or `var(--spacing-touch)`. Any other step of the Tailwind scale is `calc(var(--spacing) * <n>)`, as `p-<n>`.

Below 768 px the file changes the same text tokens as `stack.css`. It sets no style on an element, so the content sets its own ground, text color and font. A page with a build gets every token from `stack.css`, and its own markup never needs `stack-plain.css`. The compiled CSS of such a page holds only the tokens that its classes use. So the page links `stack-plain.css` only for content that its build never sees.

A page in a frame, such as HTML from an agent in the sandboxed frame of "Content that the product did not write" in `architecture/web-app.md`, is a page with no build step. Four more rules apply to it:

- The host of the content serves one read-only copy of the theme file and `stack-plain.css` of the pinned tag at one path, such as `/stack/vX.Y.Z/`. The page links the theme file, such as `/stack/vX.Y.Z/themes/default.css`, then `/stack/vX.Y.Z/stack-plain.css`. The policy of the frame allows that path for stylesheets only.
- The frame does not get the class of the app, and a theme file draws light on `:root` and dark under `.dark`. So the app tells the content its mode, and tells it again when the mode changes. Content that runs no script cannot hear the mode, and stays in light mode. The content sets the class `dark` and `color-scheme: dark` on its own `<html>`, as `## Themes` says.
- The phone steps of `stack-plain.css` are a media query, `@media (width < 48rem)`, and in a frame a media query reads the width of the frame, not the width of the window (a browser rule, not measured). So a frame on a desktop is at least 768 px wide, or its content accepts the phone steps.
- A picture that shows as an image, such as an SVG in `<img>`, loads no stylesheet (a browser rule, not measured). So it holds its own values, copied from the files of the pinned tag.

An email is content that no build sees too, and no stylesheet reaches it, also not `stack-plain.css`. It writes plain values in the `style` attribute of each element, and `## Email` gives them. A printed document takes plain values too, because paper follows neither the theme nor the mode, and `## Print` gives them.

**Accepted lint warnings.** The lint of CLI 0.4.0 reports these warning classes for this file, and they are expected. Any other warning, and every error, fails `npm run lint`.

- `orphaned-tokens`: one warning for each `colors.dark.*` name, and for each light role that no `components` entry binds yet. The lint knows no modes, so it sees the dark values as unused.
- `broken-ref` at severity warning: one warning for each `borderColor` in `components`. The format has no border property, and this file adds `borderColor` for the edge of a control. `npm run lint` accepts a `broken-ref` warning on a `borderColor` only, so a misspelled property name, such as `textColour`, still fails. A `broken-ref` error, which is a reference that does not resolve, still fails.

**The checks.** `npm run check` runs the lint, the theme check, the contrast check, the contrast check of the example layer, the self-test of the theme generator, the names check, the markers check, the completeness check, the key check and the dry run of the index. The commit hook runs the theme check, the names check and the markers check, and refuses a commit when one fails.

## Colors

Use only the color roles of this file. Never write a raw color, a palette class such as `bg-blue-500`, or an arbitrary value such as `bg-[#2563eb]`. A new need is a new role in this file first, with a value in every theme and in both modes.

The values of the default theme are in the front matter and in `themes/default.css`. A theme changes values only, and never a name. `stack.css` maps each role to a Tailwind color in `@theme inline`, so that classes such as `bg-primary`, `text-muted-foreground` and `border-input` exist. It does not clear the default Tailwind palette, because the nova components draw their scrim with `bg-black/10`.

**One meaning per role.** A feedback role (`destructive`, `success`, `warning`, `info`) never marks the status of a product item, and a status color never marks an error. The one exception is a due date that comes close or passes: its text takes `warning` or `destructive`, with an icon on the same row (see `## Do's and Don'ts`). `primary` never marks a status. A product that needs status colors adds them in its own layer, with the prefix `product-`.

**A color of the product.** A product color token holds its value in `--product-<name>`, in `:root` and in `.dark`, and the product layer maps it in `@theme inline` as `--color-product-<name>: var(--product-<name>)`, as `stack.css` maps each role. So `bg-product-<name>` exists, and an opacity modifier such as `bg-product-<name>/10` works. The alias is not a second token, and it holds no value of its own. "Import the CSS" in `README.md` gives an example block. An example screen of the preview may hold a product layer like any product, with its pair file, in the folder of the parts of the example.

**The state of a job, a device, a connection or a check.** Such a state is feedback, and not the status of a product item. Its icon and its status badge take `success` when it runs or passes, `warning` when it waits for a person or holds, and `destructive` when it fails or stops. Its word says the state, such as Running, Waiting or Failed, so that the color never carries the state alone. A state outside the three, such as Queued, Skipped or Cancelled, takes a neutral icon in `muted-foreground` and its word. As for a status, the color shows only on the icon, the badge, a meter fill and a timeline dot (see `## Do's and Don'ts`). A product item, such as an order or a ticket, still takes a status role of the product.

**Contrast.** Text that carries information needs 4.5:1 on every ground that it sits on, and the lowest ratio decides. A control edge, the focus ring, a status icon, the dot of a label, the unread mark, the fill and the marks of a meter, a chart pattern and a chart fill need 3:1. A divider carries no information and needs no ratio. `npm run contrast` checks the pairs below in both modes of every theme, and fails on a pair below its minimum. A new color pair gets its check in the same change. A product checks the pairs of its own layer with the command `stack-contrast`, in both modes of each theme that it imports, and adds each new pair of its layer to its pair file in the same change. "Check the contrast of the product layer" in `README.md` gives the form of the pair file and the grounds that the command checks.

The pairs that the check reads:

- each role on its `-foreground` role, and `foreground` on `background`, at 4.5:1;
- each `components` entry with a `backgroundColor` and a `textColor`, at 4.5:1;
- `muted-foreground`, `faint-foreground`, `strong-foreground` and `link` on `background`, at 4.5:1;
- `muted-foreground` on `selected` and on `muted/50` over `background`, at 4.5:1: the title of a closed row that is selected or under the pointer, and a time or a count in a row. `faint-foreground` serves on `background` only, so it has no other pair;
- `strong-foreground` on `muted/50` over `background`, at 4.5:1: the value of a key figure that is a link, under the pointer (see `### figure-band`);
- `muted-foreground` on `sidebar`, at 4.5:1: the count of a menu item in the sidebar;
- `warning` and `destructive` on `selected`, at 4.5:1: the text of a due date in a selected row;
- `success`, `warning` and `destructive` as the icon of a state, on `background`, `card`, `popover`, `muted`, `selected` and `sidebar`, at 3:1: a job, a device, a connection or a check in a list, a card, a menu or the sidebar. Where another rule of this list already checks the role on the same ground, that pair covers the icon;
- each `tag-<color>` role as the dot of a label, on `background`, `card`, `popover`, `muted`, `selected` and `sidebar`, at 3:1;
- `input`, `ring` and `border-strong` on `background`, at 3:1;
- each status role (`success`, `warning`, `info`) as text and as an icon, on `background` and on its own 10% tint (`bg-warning/10`), at 4.5:1. Its `-foreground` on the role as a fill is in the first rule;
- on each ground that a chart sits on (`background`, `card` and `popover`): `chart-ink`, the ink of the pattern style, and each `chart-n`, a mark of the color pattern style, a fill of the color style and a line of the two color styles, at 3:1; and `foreground` and `muted-foreground`, the text of a chart, at 4.5:1. The same two pairs cover the comparison line, a reference line and the disc of an annotation;
- the lightest step of a heat map on `background`, `card` and `popover`, at 3:1: `chart-ink` at 50% in the pattern style and `chart-heat` at 50% in the two color styles; and `chart-heat` at full strength, the edge of a cell in the two color styles, on the same grounds at 3:1;
- the text of the floating layers on `popover`, at 4.5:1: `muted-foreground` (a caption, a description, a shortcut) and `destructive` (a destructive menu item), and `destructive` on the highlight of a destructive menu item over `popover` (`destructive/10`, and `destructive/20` in dark);
- the text that the nova components draw with an opacity modifier, at 4.5:1: `sidebar-foreground` at 70% on `sidebar` (a group label of the sidebar), and `destructive` at 90% on `card` (the description of a destructive alert) and at 80% on `card` (the description of an attachment that failed);
- `destructive` on `background` at 4.5:1 (an error text, and a meter fill past its limit), and the fill of a meter at 3:1: `primary` on `muted` (a progress bar on its track) and `primary` on `background` (a meter on its outlined track);
- the parts of a meter at 3:1: `primary` and `muted-foreground`, the two safe fills, and `foreground`, a threshold tick and a forecast marker. The check reads them on `background`, the ground of the outlined track, and on `card`, `popover`, `muted`, `selected` and `sidebar`. The hatch of a pending segment is `muted-foreground` on the track, so the pair of that fill on `background` covers it. Where another rule of this list already checks the role on the same ground, that pair covers the part;
- the parts of a `reorder-list`: the drop line in `foreground` at 3:1 on `background` and `selected`, the grounds of the rows next to it, and the text of the lifted row in `popover-foreground` and `muted-foreground` on `popover` at 4.5:1. The rules above already check each of these pairs;
- the parts of a `month-view` at 4.5:1: the number of today in `background` on `foreground`, the names of the days, the numbers and "3 more" in `muted-foreground` on `background`, and the number of a day of another month in `muted-foreground` on `muted/50` over `background`. The rules above already check each of these pairs;
- the parts of a `day-log`: the head in `foreground` and `muted-foreground` on `muted`, the caption, the time and the duration in `muted-foreground` on `background`, and the line of an empty day in `faint-foreground` on `background`, at 4.5:1. The bar of a span in `input` on `background` at 3:1. The rules above already check each of these pairs;
- the `unread-mark` in `foreground` at 3:1 on each ground that it sits on. In a row, these are `background`, `muted/50` over `background` under the pointer, `muted` for the current row, and `selected`. A message in the item column of band 2 sits on `popover`. In the sidebar, the grounds are `sidebar`, and `sidebar-accent` under the pointer or on the current page. The check reads `foreground` on `muted/50` and on `sidebar-accent`, and the rules above check the other pairs;
- the parts of a `mail-message` at 4.5:1 on `background`, and on `popover` in the item column of band 2. The name and the body are `foreground`. The address, the time, the recipients, the line of the sender, an open quote and a translation are `muted-foreground`. The reason of a reply that did not go out is `destructive`. On a folded line under the pointer, the name is `foreground` and the first words and the time are `muted-foreground`, on `muted/50` over `background`, and over `popover` in band 2. The check reads these pairs at 4.5:1, and the rules above check the other pairs;
- the parts of a `board` at 4.5:1: the text of a card in `card-foreground` and `muted-foreground` on `card`, the text of the lifted card on `popover`, and the count of a column in `muted-foreground` on `muted/50` over `background`. At 3:1 on that ground: the warning icon of a limit and the drop line in `foreground`. The rules above already check each of these pairs;
- the parts of a tree of a `table`: the check of a read-only box, `background` on `muted-foreground` at 4.5:1 (`checkbox-readonly`). Its fill in `muted-foreground` at 3:1 on `background`, on `muted/50` over `background` and on `muted`. The chevron and the dash in `muted-foreground` on the same grounds at 4.5:1. The rules above already check each of these pairs;
- `destructive` on `destructive/10` over `card` at 3:1 (the icon of an attachment that failed), and the key of a Kbd in a tooltip at 4.5:1: `background` on `background/20` over `foreground`, and `background/10` in dark;
- the grounds that the nova components make with an opacity modifier, each over `background` first, at 4.5:1: `primary-foreground` on `primary/80` (the hover of a primary button), `destructive` on `destructive/10` and on `destructive/20` (a destructive button, badge or menu item), and in dark `destructive` on `destructive/30` (the hover of a destructive button) and `foreground` on `input/30` (an input and an outline button);
- in dark, `foreground` on `input/30` and on `input/50` over `muted`, at 3:1: the icon of the copy button of a `code-block` at rest and under the pointer. In light that button has the `background` ground and the `muted` hover, which the rules above read;
- the text of a `diff` line at 4.5:1: `foreground` on the tint of an added and of a removed line (`diff-added/10` and `diff-removed/10`), over `muted`, `background`, `card` and `popover`; and `muted-foreground`, the line numbers, on the tint of a line over `muted`;
- the text of a changed word of a `diff` at 4.5:1: `foreground` on the tint of the word (`/30` over the tint of its line, which the check reads as `/37`), over the same four grounds;
- the modules of a `qr-code` on its tile at 4.5:1. They are `foreground` on `background` in light mode. In dark mode the tile swaps the two roles. A ratio does not change when its two colors swap, so the first rule checks both modes. The note of a value that is too long is `muted-foreground` on `muted`, which the first rule checks too;
- the email colors of `### Email colors`, in the light mode of each theme: the hex value of each role and the ratio of each pair, at the minimum of its row. The check fails when a stated value or ratio differs from the theme file, and when a theme file has no column there;
- the print colors of `### Print colors`, which no theme changes: each value is a gray, and each pair passes its minimum at the ratio of its row. The check also fails when the CSS of `### Print template` holds another color.

**Tuning.** A theme keeps the values of its grounds and its dividers, and the hue of each role. The one exception is a divider that does not show on a ground that it sits on: the theme raises its lightness only. A theme tunes the lightness and the chroma of its text, edge, primary, feedback and chart roles until every pair passes. The grounds, the dividers and the text of the default theme are the neutral base of shadcn-svelte, with seven tuned values: `muted-foreground` is `oklch(0.525 0 0)` in light, `input` is `oklch(0.64 0 0)` in light and white at 36% in dark, `ring` and `sidebar-ring` are `oklch(0.556 0 0)` in light, and `destructive` is `oklch(0.46 0.245 27.325)` in light and `oklch(0.739 0.191 22.216)` in dark. The base values fail 4.5:1 for muted text on `muted` and on `selected`, for destructive text on its 20% tint in light and for destructive text on its 30% hover tint in dark, and 3:1 for the control edge and the focus ring. The chart colors and the added roles are the values of this file.

**Tints.** A tinted badge or banner takes its role at 10% for the fill (`bg-success/10`) and at 35% for the edge (`border-success/35`). A tinted element never sits on a tinted ground. The one exception is a changed word of a `diff`, which takes the role of its line at 30% over the tint of the line (see `### diff`).

**Hover.** A hover state comes from its role with an opacity modifier, as the nova components do it: `hover:bg-primary/80`. No role holds a hover value.

**An opacity modifier in content that no build sees.** Such content (see `## Overview`) writes `<role>/<n>` as `color-mix(in oklab, var(--color-<role>) <n>%, transparent)`. Tailwind 4.3.3 compiles an opacity modifier to the same form in `oklab`, so the class and the plain value compute to one color. For example, the hover of a primary button, `hover:bg-primary/80`, is `background-color: color-mix(in oklab, var(--color-primary) 80%, transparent)`. Do not use `in oklch`. It gives the same color in another form, so the computed value differs from the class.

### Usage

| Role | Use for | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| `background` | The main ground: the page, a list, a pane, an input at rest | Every text role passes on it. |
| `foreground` | Body text, values and icons on `background` | The default text color. |
| `card` | The ground of a card, an alert and an attachment | A card has an edge and no shadow. |
| `card-foreground` | Text on `card` | |
| `popover` | The ground of a menu, a popover, a select list, the command palette and a dialog | The ground of every floating layer. |
| `popover-foreground` | Text on `popover` | |
| `primary` | The one primary action of a region; the fill of a checked check box and of an on switch | One primary per view, pane and dialog. Never a status. |
| `primary-foreground` | Text and icons on `primary` | |
| `secondary` | The fill of a secondary button | |
| `secondary-foreground` | Text on `secondary` | |
| `muted` | A subtle ground: a Kbd, a skeleton, the hover of an outline or a ghost button | |
| `muted-foreground` | Labels, descriptions, verbs in an activity line, times and counts, icons at rest; the comparison line and a reference line of a chart | Passes 4.5:1 on `background`, `muted`, `muted/50`, `selected`, `popover` and `sidebar`. |
| `accent` | The highlighted item in a menu and in a select list | A hover ground, never a brand color. The command palette marks its selected item with `muted` (`command-item-selected`). |
| `accent-foreground` | Text on `accent` | |
| `destructive` | A destructive action, the edge of an invalid field, an error message; the icon and the badge of a state that fails or stops | Never the status of a product item. The one colored edge on a control. |
| `border` | Dividers and the edges of layers | Carries no information, so it needs no ratio. |
| `input` | The edge of every control: an input, a select trigger, a check box, the track of an off switch | Passes 3:1 on `background`. |
| `ring` | The focus ring and the focus edge of a control | Passes 3:1 on `background`. Focus only. |
| `chart-1` | The fill of chart category 1 in the color style, the marks of `pattern-1` in the color pattern style, and line 1 of a line chart in the two color styles. The pattern style draws them in `chart-ink`. | Passes 3:1 on `background`, `card` and `popover`. |
| `chart-2` | Chart category 2 in the two color styles | As `chart-1`. |
| `chart-3` | Chart category 3 in the two color styles | As `chart-1`. |
| `chart-4` | Chart category 4 in the two color styles | As `chart-1`. |
| `chart-5` | Chart category 5 in the two color styles | As `chart-1`. |
| `sidebar` | The ground of the sidebar | A subtle ground. |
| `sidebar-foreground` | Text in the sidebar | |
| `sidebar-primary` | The primary action in the sidebar | |
| `sidebar-primary-foreground` | Text on `sidebar-primary` | |
| `sidebar-accent` | The hover ground and the current item in the sidebar | |
| `sidebar-accent-foreground` | Text on `sidebar-accent` | |
| `sidebar-border` | Dividers in the sidebar and its edge | |
| `sidebar-ring` | The focus ring in the sidebar | |
| `selected` | The ground of a selected row and of the current item in a list | A theme may give it a faint hue. Never a hover. A selected row of a `table` takes `muted` (`table-row-selected`), as nova draws it. |
| `selected-foreground` | Text on `selected` | |
| `strong-foreground` | Titles and a key figure, where `foreground` is not enough | |
| `faint-foreground` | Secondary text on `background` where no state changes the ground: the line of an empty group, the time of the last save in a form foot. The nova placeholder and field description use `muted-foreground`. | On `background` only. It falls below 4.5:1 on `muted`, `selected`, a hover ground, `popover` or `sidebar` in at least one theme, so text on those grounds uses `muted-foreground`. A disabled control uses `opacity-50` instead. |
| `link` | A link in running text | Underlined at rest. A navigation link uses `foreground` or `muted-foreground`. |
| `border-faint` | The divider between the rows of one dense group | Lighter than `border`. |
| `border-strong` | The hover edge of a property value; the edge of the current segment in a segmented control | Passes 3:1 on `background`. |
| `success` | The text, the icon and the fill of a success message; the icon and the badge of a state that runs or passes | Feedback only, never the status of a product item. Passes 4.5:1 as text on `background` and on its 10% tint. |
| `success-foreground` | Text on `success` | |
| `warning` | The text, the icon and the fill of a warning message; a due date that comes close; the icon and the badge of a state that waits | Feedback only. As text, only with an icon on the same row. A dark amber in light mode, so that it passes 4.5:1 as text on `background`, on `selected` and on its 10% tint. |
| `warning-foreground` | Text on `warning` | |
| `info` | The text, the icon and the fill of a neutral message | Feedback only. Passes 4.5:1 as text on `background` and on its 10% tint. |
| `info-foreground` | Text on `info` | |
| `overlay` | The scrim under a layer that a product draws itself | The nova dialog, sheet and drawer draw their own scrim. |
| `chart-6` | Chart category 6 in the two color styles | As `chart-1`. |
| `chart-7` | Chart category 7 in the two color styles | As `chart-1`. |
| `chart-8` | Chart category 8 in the two color styles | As `chart-1`. |
| `chart-ink` | The pattern style, the default chart style: the marks of the chart patterns, the lines of a line chart, the leader lines of a donut, and the steps and the edge of a heat map | Passes 3:1 on `background`, `card` and `popover` at full strength, and also at 50%, the lightest step of a heat map. |
| `chart-heat` | The hue of a heat map in the two color styles: its five steps and the edge of each cell (see `## Charts`) | Passes 3:1 at 50% on `background`, `card` and `popover`, so it is dark in light mode and light in dark mode, as `chart-ink` is. |
| `tag-grey`, `tag-blue`, `tag-violet`, `tag-pink`, `tag-red`, `tag-orange`, `tag-yellow`, `tag-green` | The dot of a label, a tag or a category that a person colors | Only as a dot, with the name in words, or with the name as the accessible name of a disc in a color picker: 6 px in a label badge (see `badge`), and a 16 px disc in a color picker (see `radio-group`). Never the ground of a badge, a row or a card, and never text. The person picks the color, so it never marks a status, a state or a chart category. A theme without these roles shows every dot in `muted-foreground`. Passes 3:1 on `background`, `card`, `popover`, `muted`, `selected` and `sidebar`. |
| `diff-added` | The tint of an added line of a `diff` at 10% (`bg-diff-added/10`), and of an added word in it at 30% over the line | Only as a tint, never as text, an edge or a fill at full strength. The sign carries the change, so the tint needs no ratio of its own. `foreground` passes 4.5:1 on each tint over `muted`, `background`, `card` and `popover`, and `muted-foreground` on the tint of a line over `muted`. A theme without this role shows the tint in `muted-foreground`. |
| `diff-removed` | The tint of a removed line of a `diff` at 10% (`bg-diff-removed/10`), and of a removed word in it at 30% over the line | As `diff-added`. A removed line is no error, so it never takes `destructive`. |

## Typography

Inter 4.1 is the one face of the interface and of the documents that a product renders, in the Latin, the Cyrillic and the Greek script. It is a variable font under the SIL Open Font License 1.1, with a weight axis and an optical size axis, and it covers the full Serbian Latin set. A printed document embeds it, with its own type steps in pt (see `## Print`). `### Other scripts` gives the faces of the other scripts.

A product loads Inter from its own assets through the npm package `@fontsource-variable/inter`, and never from a font service such as Google Fonts. A font service receives the address of each user on each load, and an admin area does not send that to a third party. An app with no third-party request also works from its cache and offline. The package pins the font version like any other dependency. The faces load with `font-display: swap`, so that the text shows at once. A document that a renderer on the server prints loads the static faces of `@fontsource/inter` instead, because Chromium embeds the variable face in a PDF as Type 3 fonts (see `### Print type`).

`font-sans` is Inter with a system fallback. An email names the same face and loads no font (see `### Email type`). `font-mono` is for code, logs and terminal text only. An identifier or a number is never monospace. Code, a command or a log that a person reads and copies as a whole goes into a `code-block`.

**Code or identifier.** A string that a person copies into a tool and never reads aloud is code, and it takes `font-mono`: a commit hash, a short hash, a branch name, a file path, a command, the name of an environment variable or a secret, and the value of an API key or an access token. A string that a person reads aloud or says to another person is an identifier, and it takes the identifier rule of `### Type steps`: a document number, a ticket number, a task number, and the name of a model or a plan. A long hash shows its start and its end with an ellipsis between them, such as `9f3c41d0…e21a`, and a tooltip and a copy button hold the whole value.

### Type steps

Each step holds a size and its line height together, so that the two never drift apart. `stack.css` writes the steps as the Tailwind text tokens in a `@theme` block, which a media query can change. Below 768 px it changes five of them, and `## Layout` gives the values.

| Step | Size / line height | Weight | Class | Use for |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `meta` | 11 / 14 px | 500 | `text-2xs` | Times, counts and the smallest labels |
| `small` | 12 / 16 px | 400 | `text-xs` | Badges and captions. The nova field description uses `body`. |
| `body` | 13 / 20 px | 400 | `text-sm` | The default interface text: buttons, menus, lists, labels |
| `input` | 13 / 20 px | 400 | `text-sm` from 768 px, `text-base` below | The value in an input, a select and a text area |
| `reading` | 14 / 21 px | 400 | `text-md` | Running text: a description, a message, a document body |
| `section` | 16 / 22 px | 600 | `text-base` | The section headings that a product writes itself. The titles of a card, a dialog, an alert dialog, a sheet and a drawer take this size at 500, as nova sets them. |
| `title` | 20 / 26 px, -0.01em | 600 | `text-xl` | Page titles |
| `figure` | 28 / 34 px, -0.02em | 600 | `text-3xl` | One key figure |
| `code` | 12 / 16 px | 400 | `font-mono text-xs` | Code, logs and terminal text |

The nova components set their text with `text-sm`, `text-xs` and `text-base`, so they follow the steps. They set their own weight, 500 (`font-medium`), on buttons, labels and titles. Below 768 px a nova input renders 16 px (`text-base`), so that a phone does not zoom on focus, and the native select takes the same 16 px through its phone key. Three nova literals reach no step: the small button, the small toggle and the names of the days in a calendar render 12.8 px, and the shortcut mark of the questionnaire renders 10 px. Their definitions state the rendered size. svelte-sonner sets a toast in a system font at a fixed 13 px, and the classes of `### sonner` give it Inter and the `body` step.

**Rules.**

- Weights 400, 500 and 600 only. No italic in the interface.
- Tabular figures (`tabular-nums`) on every amount, number, date, time, count, percent and meter label, so that a column of numbers aligns. An identifier, such as a ticket number, follows its own rule below.
- An identifier, such as a document number, a ticket number or a task number, is Inter 500 in `foreground`. A commit, a branch or a path is code, not an identifier (see Code or identifier above).
- An identifier takes tabular figures only in a column where identifiers align, such as a column of a table or the identifier cell of a list row, and there only on its number part: `INV-<span class="tabular-nums">0042</span>`. Two identifiers with the same prefix and the same number of digits then have the same width. Tabular figures in Inter also give the hyphen the width of a figure, 8.42 px in place of 6.02 px at 13 px and 500, and `calt` off, `case`, `ss01` and `cv01` do not keep it narrow (measured). Elsewhere, as in running text, a pane header, a badge or a breadcrumb, an identifier keeps the proportional figures of Inter.
- Sentence case everywhere. No label in upper case.
- The smallest text is 11 px on a desktop and 12 px on a phone, at the default text size and at every larger one (see `### Text size`).
- A 32 px row and a chip hold one line with an ellipsis. The full text goes into a tooltip and into the accessible name. Text wraps in a pane, a page and a dialog.
- A line of running text is at most 720 px wide (`spacing.reading-width`).
- Below 768 px running text takes `reading-phone`, 15 / 22 px, so that it is as large as the 15 px `body` text around it.
- A link in running text uses `link` and an underline at rest, because color alone does not mark it. A theme may set `link` to the value of `foreground`, and then the underline is the only mark (see `## Themes`). A link in navigation, such as a sidebar item or a breadcrumb, needs no underline.
- Optical size: a step up to 16 px uses the text optical size, `"opsz" 14`. The `title` and `figure` steps use the display optical size, `"opsz" 32`. Set `font-optical-sizing: none`, so that the 16 px step does not drift toward the display size. The optical size axis comes with `@fontsource-variable/inter/opsz.css`.

### Text size

A product may let each person pick a text size. The size is a step of the font size of the root element, such as `font-size: 93%` on `<html>`. Every rem size of `stack.css` then scales together: the type steps, the spacing steps, the heights of the controls and the widths of the app shell. No token changes. The width bands stay, because a media query reads a rem from the initial font size and not from the root element (Media Queries Level 4). At 115%, the bands still change at 768 px, the sidebar is 276 px wide and the pane 483 px, so the list keeps less width in each band (measured).

The default step is 100%. A product may offer larger steps, such as 115%, and smaller steps, such as 93%. The floors of `### Type steps`, 11 px on a desktop and 12 px on a phone, hold at the default step and at every larger step. A step under 100% takes `meta` under the floors: at 93% it renders 10.23 px from 768 px and 11.16 px below 768 px (measured). So only a person picks such a step, and a product never makes it the default.

On a phone, targets never shrink: every target stays at least 44 by 44 px at every step. The phone keys of `components` set their minimum in px, so they keep 44 px (measured at 93%). A target that a class sets in rem shrinks under 100%: `max-md:min-h-11` on a badge that is a link renders 40.92 px at 93% (measured). So a product that offers a step under 100% gives each such target a minimum in px, through a token of its own layer: `--spacing-product-touch: 44px` in its `@theme` gives the class `max-md:min-h-product-touch`. The custom property `--spacing-touch` of `stack-plain.css` is 2.75rem, so it shrinks too. From 768 px the controls scale with the step, so a 24 px button of size `xs` renders 22.32 px at 93% (computed, not rendered), under `spacing.target-min`. So the same product gives each such target a minimum of 24 px in the same way, such as `--spacing-product-target: 24px` and the classes `md:min-h-product-target md:min-w-product-target`, and every target keeps the floor of WCAG 2.2 criterion 2.5.8 at every step. Only the text may go under its floor, because the person picks the step.

One gap is open: below 768 px an input renders 1rem, so at 93% it renders 14.88 px (computed, not rendered), under the 16 px that keeps a phone from a zoom on focus (see `### Type steps`).

### Other scripts

Inter covers the Latin, the Cyrillic and the Greek script: `@fontsource-variable/inter` holds the subsets `latin`, `latin-ext`, `cyrillic`, `cyrillic-ext`, `greek`, `greek-ext` and `vietnamese`. A character that Inter does not cover takes the next face of `font-sans` that covers it, and then a face that the system picks for its script. Chromium 148 does not know `ui-sans-serif` and `ui-monospace`, so a stack of one of them alone renders Times. In the stacks of STACK, Chromium goes on to the next name (measured).

STACK adds no web face for another script, and a product loads none in the interface. Each system holds a face for each script of the table, so the text shows with no request, in the face of the other apps of the system. A web face for Chinese is also large: the package `@fontsource/noto-sans-sc` 5.3.1 unpacks to 74.6 MB, and `@fontsource-variable/inter` 5.3.0 to 1.9 MB (npm, read 2026-10-25). A product may name a stack of system faces for one script in its own layer, such as a `--product-font-zh` that a rule on `:lang(zh)` applies. Such a stack starts with Inter, so that the Latin letters and the figures in the block keep Inter.

| Script | `lang` | macOS 26.5, Chromium 148 | Windows 11, Chromium | Android | iOS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Arabic | `ar` | Geeza Pro | Tahoma | Noto Naskh Arabic | Geeza Pro |
| Hebrew | `he` | Lucida Grande | Segoe UI | Noto Sans Hebrew | Arial Hebrew |
| Devanagari | `hi` | Kohinoor Devanagari | Nirmala UI | Noto Sans Devanagari | Kohinoor Devanagari |
| Thai | `th` | Thonburi | Tahoma | Noto Sans Thai | Thonburi |
| Chinese, simplified | `zh-Hans` | PingFang SC | Microsoft YaHei | Noto Sans CJK | PingFang SC, listed as a download |
| Chinese, traditional | `zh-Hant` | PingFang TC | Microsoft JhengHei | Noto Sans CJK | PingFang TC, listed as a download |
| Japanese | `ja` | Hiragino Kaku Gothic ProN | Yu Gothic | Noto Sans CJK | Hiragino Sans |
| Korean | `ko` | Apple SD Gothic Neo | Malgun Gothic | Noto Sans CJK | Apple SD Gothic Neo |

The macOS column is measured with `CSS.getPlatformFontsForNode`, at 1280 and at 390 px. The Windows column is the first face that Chromium names for the script and that Windows 11 installs. Chromium names David for Hebrew and Meiryo for Japanese first, but Windows 11 installs these two only as optional fonts. The Android column is the face of the script in `fonts.xml` of Android. The iOS column is a face that the Apple Developer page "System Fonts" lists for iOS (read 2026-10-25), and no measurement checked it. Apple lists PingFang SC and PingFang TC as a download on iOS and on macOS, but macOS 26.5 holds PingFang in a system file and renders it with no download (measured). The Chromium source, the Windows 11 font list, `fonts.xml` and the list of Apple were read on 2026-10-25.

**Language.** The `lang` of an element picks the face of the Han characters, which Chinese, Japanese and Korean share in different forms. The same three characters render in PingFang SC with `lang="zh-Hans"`, in PingFang TC with `zh-Hant`, in Hiragino Kaku Gothic ProN with `ja` and in Apple SD Gothic Neo with `ko`. In an interface in English, a Japanese sentence with no `lang` of its own renders its kana in Hiragino Sans and its Han characters in the Chinese PingFang SC (measured). So content in another language carries its own `lang` (see `### Content in another language`).

**Line height.** Each step keeps its line height in every script. At every step, at 1280 and at 390 px, a line of Arabic, Hebrew, Devanagari, Thai, Chinese, Japanese or Korean is as high as a line of Latin. So is a line that mixes five scripts, and a paragraph of four such lines is 84 px high at `reading` at 1280 px and 88 px high at `reading-phone` at 390 px, as in Latin (measured). Some marks reach outside the line. A Thai tone mark reaches up to 2.19 px above a line of `body` at 390 px. Stacked Thai or Arabic marks reach up to 3.78 px below a line of `section` (measured). In running text they reach into the space between two lines, and no line moves.

**Ellipsis.** `truncate` cuts such marks, because its `overflow: hidden` clips at the edge of the line: at 390 px it cuts the tops of the Thai tone marks (measured). So a line that ends in an ellipsis and can hold such a script takes `overflow-x-clip text-ellipsis whitespace-nowrap` in the place of `truncate`. The ellipsis stays, and the marks stay whole (measured in Chromium 148; Safari not measured). These vendored parts draw `truncate` themselves: the sidebar menu buttons and the title and the description of an attachment. A product does not edit a vendored component, so the marks of a Thai or an Arabic name in them can lose their tops.

**Code.** The `code` step keeps its size and its line height in every script (measured). Menlo covers Latin, Cyrillic and Greek. On macOS, Arabic and Hebrew take Courier New, Thai takes Ayuthaya, and Devanagari, Chinese, Japanese and Korean take the faces of the table (measured). Chromium names Courier New for monospace Arabic and Hebrew on Windows too. Courier New keeps nearly the cell of Menlo, 7.20 px against 7.22 px at 12 px, but a Chinese character is 12.25 px wide (measured). So the columns of a log line with Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Devanagari or Thai text do not align.

**Documents.** A document that a product renders with no browser, such as a PDF or the image of a social card, has no system fallback. Its renderer uses only the faces that it gets. So the product gives the renderer a face that covers each script of the document, and names that face. STACK names none. A PDF that a browser makes on a server uses the fonts of that server, so that host needs a face for each script of the document.

The preview page "Scripts and direction" shows each script at each step, the Han characters in each language and the two ellipses.

### Content in another language

Content in another language or direction than the interface keeps the look of the interface: the same steps, the same roles and the faces of `### Other scripts`. Only its direction and its alignment follow the content. "Content in another language" in `architecture/web-app.md` gives the `lang` and the `dir` of each case.

- A block of running text, such as a message or a description, takes the `reading` step and wraps, as text in the language of the interface does. A block in a right-to-left language aligns to its right edge, and the period of a sentence ends it on the left (measured). The label, the time and the actions around the block keep the sides of the interface.
- A name or another value inside a sentence of the interface keeps the order of the sentence, because `<bdi>` isolates it (measured).
- A table cell keeps the alignment of its column. Its value in `<bdi>` keeps the left edge of the column, and a count after the value stays after it (measured).
- A value that a row cuts with an ellipsis takes `dir="auto"` and `text-left` on the element that cuts it, with the classes of the ellipsis in `### Other scripts`. The ellipsis then cuts the end of an Arabic title, on its left, and the title keeps its start and the left edge of the column. With `truncate` alone, the Arabic title loses its start (measured at 390 px).
- A field where a person types free text, such as a name or a note, takes `dir="auto"`. An Arabic or a Hebrew value then aligns to the right of the field (measured). The label, the description and the error keep the sides of the interface.
- Code keeps `dir="ltr"` and its left edge inside a block in a right-to-left language (see `### code-block` and `### code-editor`).

The preview page "Scripts and direction" shows each case. It shows the name in a sentence, the cut title and the code also in the form that breaks.

## Layout

Every screen of a web app works at every width, from a phone to a wide desktop. This section gives the space steps, the app shell, the five width bands, the page outside the shell and the layout of the shared parts of a screen. The values are in the front matter: `spacing` holds the steps and the sizes, and `breakpoints` holds the width lines. A size in this section names its token and the Tailwind class that draws it. Each class is a step of the Tailwind spacing scale, which is 4 px, such as `w-105` for 420 px. Never write an arbitrary value such as `w-[420px]`. A size of the product that is not a step of the scale holds its value in `--product-<name>`, and the product layer maps it in `@theme inline` as `--spacing-product-<name>: var(--product-<name>)`, so that `w-product-<name>` exists. A size that is a step of the scale needs no token. The sides in this section are those of a left-to-right interface, because the shell and the components run left to right only (see `### Direction`).

### Space

Space follows a 4 px grid. The 2 px and 6 px steps serve inside small controls only. The table names the gaps that a product writes itself. A nova component keeps its own padding, such as 10 px inside a button and 16 px inside a dialog, and its definition states it.

| Step | Size | Class | Use for |
|---|---|---|---|
| `space-0-5` | 2 px | `p-0.5` | The padding of a segmented control |
| `space-1` | 4 px | `gap-1` | The gap between chips, and between an icon and its count |
| `space-1-5` | 6 px | `gap-1.5` | The gap inside a chip or a badge, and between the parts of a breadcrumb |
| `space-2` | 8 px | `gap-2` | The gap in a toolbar and in a group header, and between two facts |
| `space-3` | 12 px | `pl-3`, `gap-3` | The left padding of a row and of a group header, and the gap between the cells of a row |
| `space-4` | 16 px | `pr-4`, `p-4` | The right padding of a row, the padding of a pane, and the edge of a page |
| `space-5` | 20 px | `gap-5` | The gap between the fields of a form |
| `space-6` | 24 px | `gap-6`, `px-6` | The gap between the sections of a page, and the side padding of a full page |
| `space-8` | 32 px | `pt-8`, `gap-8` | The top padding of an empty state, and the gap between the bands of a summary page |
| `space-12` | 48 px | `p-12` | The padding of an empty full page |

### App shell

One shell serves every screen of a web app that a person opens in a session: an inverted L. The sidebar holds the left edge from the top to the bottom. The top bar, the list and the detail pane share the space to the right of the sidebar. The command palette floats above all of them. A page that a person opens with no session, such as the sign-in page, has no shell (see `### Page outside the shell`).

| Part | Size | Token | Class | Ground and edge |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sidebar | 240 px wide, 56 px collapsed | `sidebar`, `sidebar-collapsed` | `w-60`, `w-14` | `sidebar`, with a `sidebar-border` edge on the right |
| Top bar | 44 px high | `top-bar` | `h-11` | `background`, with a `border` edge below |
| Banner, optional | At least 36 px high, across the list and the pane | none | `min-h-9` | An `alert` with a tinted feedback ground |
| List, table form | The rest of the width | none | `flex-1` | `background` |
| Detail pane, table form | 420 px wide | `pane` | `w-105` | `background`, with a `border` edge on the left |
| List column, work form | 340 px wide, 400 px from 1280 px | `list-column`, `list-column-wide` | `lg:w-85`, `xl:w-100` | `background` |
| Item column, work form | The rest of the width | none | `flex-1` | `background`, with a `border` edge on the left |

**Two forms of the split.** The list and the item share the space under the top bar in one of two forms. The table form gives the list the rest of the width and the item a pane of 420 px. It serves a log, a report and a list that a person compares by its columns, such as an audit log. The work form gives the list a narrow column and the item the rest of the width. It serves a list that a person works one item at a time. Examples are an inbox, a queue of cases, a pipeline of deals and a list of failed tasks. The person reads the item and acts on it, so the item needs the width. The list only has to show enough to choose the next item. When a person opens each row of a list, acts on it and goes to the next row, the list takes the work form. A view of a list keeps its own form, so a table view takes the table form (see "Views" in `### Lists`). `### Width bands` gives the widths of each form. `### Lists` gives the row of the work form, and `### Detail pane` gives the item column.

**Sidebar.** The nova `sidebar` draws the sidebar. Its default widths are 256 px and 48 px, so set the two widths on the provider: `<Sidebar.Provider style="--sidebar-width: 15rem; --sidebar-width-icon: 3.5rem">`. The head of the sidebar holds the workspace switcher, then the search, then the New button. An app with one workspace shows no switcher. The first row of the head then holds the mark and the name of the product, as a link to the first page of the sidebar (see `### sidebar`). The search opens the command palette and shows its key. The pages follow under the head. The foot holds the settings and the user menu. The user menu holds the theme switch, and the command palette holds the same action. In the collapsed sidebar, the search and the New button become icon buttons, each with a tooltip of its label and its key. Nova keeps the padding of its 48 px rail in the 56 px rail, so give `Sidebar.Root` the two classes of `### sidebar`, which put the icons in the center.

**Top bar.** The left side of the top bar holds the breadcrumbs, then the view tabs of the current list. The view tabs are two to four fixed views in a `tabs` list. In a list of four, each tab has an icon and a label, and below 1024 px (`lg`) the tabs show the icons only, with the label in a tooltip on hover and on focus, and in the accessible name of each tab (see `### tabs`). The right side holds the filter and the display controls as small buttons, 28 px high (`size="sm"`). Filter chips sit in the bar, between the view tabs and the buttons, until they need a second line. Then they move to a row of 36 px under the bar. The top bar holds no search, no theme switch and no copy of the New button of the sidebar head. On the page of a record that the New button does not create, the top bar ends with the create button of that record (see "Create button" in `### Lists`). No view bar floats over the content. While one or more rows are selected, the selection bar takes the place of the content of the top bar (see `### selection-bar`). The frame of the product wraps the content of the top bar in `@stack-kit/stack/parts/FramePlace` with the name `top-bar`, and the content of the phone header in a second `FramePlace` with the name `phone-header`. A place takes no box of its own, so the content keeps the layout of the frame. While a part claims the place, the place shows the part in the place of its content and carries `data-claimed`, so the frame can change its padding with `has-data-claimed:`. The polite live region of the place is in the page before the first claim, and it says the text of the part, such as "2 selected".

### Width bands

`breakpoints` holds four width lines: `md` at 768 px, `lg` at 1024 px, `xl` at 1280 px and `2xl` at 1440 px. `stack.css` writes them as the Tailwind breakpoints, so each line has its variant: `md:`, `lg:`, `xl:` and `2xl:`. The four lines make five bands. A class with no variant applies in every band, and a variant adds a change from its line up. `max-md:` holds a rule for the phone only. Tailwind keeps its `sm` line at 640 px, and that line is not a band. The nova components use `sm` inside a component only, such as `sm:max-w-sm` on a dialog, and a layout never uses it.

**Band 1: below 768 px, the phone (`max-md:`).** The sidebar and the top bar do not show. A 52 px header (`spacing.header-phone`, `h-13`) holds the title of the screen, a back button on an inner screen, and at most two actions. A 56 px tab bar (`spacing.tab-bar-phone`, `h-14`) at the bottom holds five tabs, each with an icon and a label. The fifth tab, More, opens the rest of the navigation in the sheet that the nova sidebar draws below 768 px. A row grows to 52 px (`spacing.row-phone`, `h-13`) and holds two lines: the identifier and the title, then the key values in `small` type. An item opens as its own screen, not in a pane. A menu opens as a bottom sheet (`drawer`), and a dialog fills the screen. The touch targets and the type steps change as the next two parts say. Each product decides which tasks a phone leaves out.

**Band 2: 768 to 1023 px (`md:`, and `bands.md` in script).** The sidebar collapses to 56 px and shows its icons with tooltips. The pane overlays the list, as in band 3. The properties panel becomes a row of property values under the title of the item. The properties of an item stay in the properties panel and never go into a side column, which moves under the main column below 1280 px (see `### Side column`).

**Band 3: 1024 to 1279 px (`lg:`, and `bands.lg` in script).** The full sidebar shows. The pane overlays the right side of the list at level 3 of `## Elevation & Depth`. Like the nova sheet, it has the `popover` ground, a `border` edge on its left and `shadow-lg`. The pane is not a modal layer. It has no scrim, and J and K still move the selection in the list. The list keeps its width.

**Band 4: 1280 to 1439 px (`xl:`, and `bands.xl` in script).** The full shell shows: the sidebar, the top bar, the list and the docked pane. A list may mark one data column as extra, and that column hides while the pane is open.

**Band 5: 1440 px and wider (`2xl:`, and `bands['2xl']` in script).** The full shell shows, and the extra column of the list stays while the pane is open. Each entry of `bands`, of `@stack-kit/stack/behaviour/width`, is true from its line up, and a part that picks its layout in script reads it (see "A layout that a script picks" in `### A screen page in print`).

**Work form.** The pane of the five bands above is the pane of the table form. The work form keeps the sidebar, the top bar and the screens of band 1. It gives the list and the item these widths:

| Band | List | Item |
|---|---|---|
| 1, below 768 px | The whole screen, in the phone rows of two lines | Its own inner screen |
| 2, 768 to 1023 px | The whole width | 420 px (`spacing.pane`, `w-105`) over the right side of the list, as the pane of band 3, only when the person opens it |
| 3, 1024 to 1279 px | 340 px (`spacing.list-column`, `lg:w-85`) | The rest of the width, docked on `background`, with a `border` edge on its left and no shadow |
| 4 and 5, from 1280 px | 400 px (`spacing.list-column-wide`, `xl:w-100`) | The rest of the width, docked |

STACK measured a list of the example in a frame as wide as the window. At 1440 px the sidebar, the list and the item were 240, 400 and 800 px. They were 240, 400 and 640 px at 1280 px, and 240, 340 and 699 px at 1279 px. At 1024 px they were 240, 340 and 444 px. At 900 px the rail was 56 px and the list 844 px, and no item showed until a click. The click put the item over the list at 420 px. The width of the item includes its edge. At each width, 390 px included, the page was as wide as the window (measured in Chromium 148). The extra column of bands 4 and 5 has no meaning in the work form, because its rows have no data columns.

**Inner screen.** In band 1, a screen that a person opens from the screen of a tab is an inner screen. Examples are an item, a document, a section of the settings and a step of a flow. It takes the place of the pane or the page of the wider bands, and it shows one thing. Its header holds the back button, the title and at most two actions. The tab bar does not show on an inner screen, so the screen keeps its height for that one thing. The back button leads to the screen that opened it.

**Foot bar.** An inner screen may end in a foot bar that holds its last actions, such as Comment and Accept under a document. The foot bar takes the place of the tab bar, and a screen never shows both. It is as high, 56 px (`spacing.tab-bar-phone`, `h-14`), on `background` with a `border` edge on top. It holds one or two buttons of 44 px (`button-phone`), 8 px apart (`gap-2`), with 16 px padding at the sides (`px-4`). Each button takes an equal part of the width (`flex-1`), and the primary button comes last. The bar sits under the part of the screen that scrolls, so it stays at the bottom edge, and it keeps the bottom inset under its 56 px. It comes after the content in the code, so a screen reader reaches it last. A screen with the tab bar holds its actions in its header, and a dialog that fills the screen holds them in its footer or its header (see `### dialog`).

**Page width.** Below 768 px no page is wider than the screen. A part whose content is wider, such as a table or a board, scrolls inside its own box. A page that is wider than the screen moves when an overlay opens. On a phone, Chromium makes `innerWidth` as wide as the page, and `clientWidth` stays as wide as the screen. The scroll lock of Bits UI takes the difference for a scroll bar, and it pads the body on the right by that width. Every modal layer takes the lock when it opens: a `dialog`, an `alert-dialog`, a `sheet`, a `drawer`, a `dropdown-menu`, a `context-menu` and a `select`. A `popover`, a `tooltip`, a `hover-card` and a `menubar` do not (Bits UI 2.19.5, vaul-svelte 1.0.0-next.7, read in the source). So the page reflows narrower under the open overlay, and its parts move. At 390 px a page of 443 px took 53 px of padding, and the trigger of a select moved 617 px down, off the screen. A page of 390 px took no padding, and no part moved (measured in Chromium 148 with touch emulation). An item of a grid or of a flex row is never narrower than its fixed content by default, such as the svg of a chart. Give such an item `min-w-0`, or give a grid with no column template `grid-cols-1`. A long word, such as the full address of a link, does not break by default, so give its text `wrap-break-word`. In an item of a grid or of a flex row, `wrap-break-word` does not make the item narrower, so the item also takes `min-w-0` (measured in Chromium 148). The columns of `PageColumns` and of `ReadingColumn` take `min-w-0`, and the reading column breaks a long word, so a page that holds its content in them gives no such class (see `### Side column` and `### Reading column`). Check each page at 390 px: `document.documentElement.scrollWidth` equals `clientWidth`.

**Touch targets.** Below 768 px every target is at least 44 by 44 px (`spacing.touch`). A phone key of `components`, `<data-slot>-phone`, gives a component its target, and `stack.css` writes it as `min-height` and `min-width` (see `## Components`). `button-phone` makes every button at least 44 by 44 px. A trigger or a close button that renders its own slot, such as `dialog-trigger` or `dropdown-menu-trigger`, has its own phone key, because the button rule does not reach it. A check box, a radio button and a switch reach 44 px through the row of their `field`. A click on the label sets the control too. The Phone part of each definition names its key. From 768 px a target is at least 24 by 24 px (`spacing.target-min`), as WCAG 2.2 asks in criterion 2.5.8.

**Phone type.** Below 768 px five type steps grow. `stack.css` changes the same text tokens in a media query, so every class and every component follows with no new class. The other steps stay.

| Step | Class | From 768 px | Below 768 px |
|---|---|---|---|
| `meta` | `text-2xs` | 11 / 14 px | 12 / 16 px (`meta-phone`) |
| `small` | `text-xs` | 12 / 16 px | 13 / 18 px (`small-phone`) |
| `body` | `text-sm` | 13 / 20 px | 15 / 20 px (`body-phone`) |
| `reading` | `text-md` | 14 / 21 px | 15 / 22 px (`reading-phone`) |
| `title` | `text-xl` | 20 / 26 px, -0.01em | 22 / 28 px, -0.01em (`title-phone`) |

The line height of `body` stays 20 px, so every nova height stays. An input renders 16 px below 768 px (see `## Typography`). The 12.8 px text of the nova small button is a fixed size, and it does not grow.

**Safe area.** A notch, a rounded corner or a home bar can cover an edge of the screen. So in every band, each part of the app that touches an edge of the screen keeps the safe area of that edge, with `env(safe-area-inset-*)`. The rule is not for band 1 only: a phone held sideways is 812 to 932 px wide, so it falls in band 2. The inset is padding outside the size of the part, so the 52 px header stays 52 px high under its top inset. A dialog that fills the screen is the one exception: it takes the insets as a transparent border inside its size (see `### dialog`). The parts take these insets:

- The sidebar, and its sheet in band 1: the top, the bottom and the left inset.
- The top bar, and the header of band 1: the top inset.
- The tab bar and the foot bar of band 1: the bottom inset.
- The column to the right of the sidebar: the right inset. In band 1 the sidebar does not show, so the column takes the left inset too.
- The list and the pane in bands 2 to 5, which reach the bottom edge: the bottom inset.
- A bottom sheet: the bottom, the left and the right inset. A dialog that fills the screen: all four.
- A page outside the shell: the head line takes the top, the left and the right inset, and the column takes the left, the right and the bottom inset.

A part that touches no edge of the screen takes no inset. No Tailwind class writes `env()`, and `## Do's and Don'ts` forbids an arbitrary value, so a style block writes the insets. With no inset, nothing moves. On iOS every inset stays 0 unless the viewport line of the app sets `viewport-fit=cover`, as "The app on a phone" in `architecture/web-app.md` says.

### Page outside the shell

A person opens some pages of a web app with no session. They are the sign-in page, the recovery page and the page of a setup link. They are also a page from a signed link in an email, a read-only link, and the error page of such a link. Such a page has no shell: no sidebar, no top bar, no header and no tab bar of band 1, and no command palette. In every band it has a head line and one column of content. "Pages with no session" in `architecture/web-app.md` gives the behavior: what a GET shows, what a POST spends, and what works with no script. The package ships the sign-in page, the recovery page, the page of a setup link, the link page and the read-only link as parts with their states. The product gives one function for each call to its server, and it keeps its own routes. Each function answers `{ ok: true }`, or a refusal `{ ok: false, state, until }`. The `state` is a state of a table below, and the `until` of a refusal is an ISO 8601 time where a pause ends. Each page takes the `product`, its `name` and its `mark`. A page that leaves out a function that the part needs fails the type check. The page `/outside` of the preview shows the link page, the read-only link, the sign-in page, the recovery page and the page of a setup link. Each state that follows a step, such as Done of the link page, shows after that step, with an invented answer of the server. The sign-in page also shows the form of an authenticator app. The preview does not show the report page and the look with no script.

**Head line.** The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/OutsidePage` draws the head line and the column of each such page, also of a page outside the shell that the product writes itself. The head line spans the top of the window. It is as high as the top bar, 44 px (`spacing.top-bar`, `h-11`), on `background` with a `border` edge below. It holds the mark of the product, 24 px high (`h-6`), then the name of the product in `body` at 600, 8 px apart (`gap-2`). It holds no navigation, no search, no user menu and no mode switch, because the person has no session. The mark is not a link, so the page keeps one action.

**Column.** The column starts 32 px under the head line (`pt-8`), at the left edge of the mark, and ends 48 px under its content (`pb-12`). The side padding is 24 px (`px-6`) from 768 px and 16 px below 768 px (`max-md:px-4`). So the mark and the title start 24 px and 16 px from the left edge (measured at 1280 and 390 px). The content is at most 720 px wide (`spacing.reading-width`, `max-w-180`), on the ground of the page. It has no `card` and no edge, because the page holds one object, and a box would add an edge with no meaning. The column is never centered: no `mx-auto`, no `justify-center` and no `items-center` on the page, also not for a sign-in page (see `## Do's and Don'ts`). So the mark, the title and the button keep one left edge in every band, as on a page in the shell. A form in the column, such as the code of a sign-in, is at most 384 px wide (`max-w-96`), the width of a dialog.

**Parts.** From the top, the column holds the title and its text, 8 px apart (`gap-2`), then the facts, then the action, 24 px apart (`gap-6`). The title is the one `h1`, in `title` type, and it names the action and the object, such as "Approve the quote QUO-0042?". The title wraps on a phone, so an identifier in it keeps one line (`whitespace-nowrap`): with no such class, "QUO-0042" breaks after its hyphen at 390 px (measured). The text under it is `body` in `muted-foreground`. OutsidePage draws the title and the text when the page gives `text`. The facts that the action fixes show in the summary grid of `### alert-dialog`: the object, the number, the date, the amount and the person (see "Actions with a lasting effect" in `architecture/web-app.md`).

**Action.** The page has one primary button, and its label repeats the verb of the button in the email, such as "Approve quote". An action that deletes or ends something takes `variant="destructive"`, as on an alert dialog. The page has no Cancel button, because a person who closes the page changes nothing, and the text of Ready says so. A second action, such as "Send a new link", is an `outline` button after the primary. A state line under the buttons gives a wait or the reason of a disabled button in `small` and `muted-foreground`. It gives a cancel or a failure in `body` with an icon, in `muted-foreground` or in `destructive`. The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/StateLine` draws the line, with `kind` `wait`, `cancel` or `fail`, and holds its two regions.

**Link page.** A page from a signed link has eight states. Each state keeps the head line and the column, and changes the title, the text and the action. Expired and Not valid show no facts, because the token of a link that does not work does not prove that the person may see the object. The other states show the object. The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/LinkPage` draws the page and its states, and `@stack-kit/stack/outside/Facts` draws the facts, also on a page that the product writes itself. The product gives `initial`, the state that its server found for the token. It gives `title`, `text`, `verb`, `facts` and `texts`, which name the object and its times, and `act`, the POST of the primary button. It gives `resend` where it can send the link again, and `variant="destructive"` for an action that deletes or ends something. The part writes the titles of Expired and Already used, the whole of Not valid, and the state line of Failed. With no script, the product answers the POST with the page in its next state, such as `done`, and gives `requestId` for Failed. In Expired and Not valid, give `facts: []` and no text that names the object. The part draws none of it, and a server page sends its props to the browser.

| State | Title | Text | Action |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ready | The action and the object as a question, such as "Approve the quote QUO-0042?" | What the action does, then "Nothing changes until you choose Approve quote." The facts follow. | The primary button with the verb of the link |
| Working | As in Ready | As in Ready | The button shows a `spinner`, keeps its label and its width, and is `disabled` with `aria-busy="true"` (see `### button`) |
| Done | The result, such as "You approved the quote QUO-0042" | What happens next. The facts stay, with the time of the action. | None |
| Expired | "This link has expired" | When the link expired, and how long a link works | "Send a new link", when the product can send the link again to the same address. Otherwise none, and the text names who sends a new link. |
| Already used | "This link was already used" | When the link was used, and its result, such as "The quote QUO-0042 was approved on 23 October at 16:05." | None |
| Changed | What changed after the email, such as "The quote QUO-0042 changed after the email" | Who changed it and when. The facts show each changed value with a warning icon, and the value of the email after it. | The primary button, disabled with a reason as `### button` says, and "Send a new link" when the product can send one. The state line gives the reason. |
| Failed | As in Ready | As in Ready, and a state line in `destructive` with an icon: what failed, the retry and the request id | The primary button, which tries again |
| Not valid | "This link does not work" | The address is not complete or it changed, and the fix: open the link from the email again. The text never says for whom or for what the link was. | None |

Done, Already used and Not valid end the flow. When the person has an account in the app, such a state may offer one link into the app as an `outline` button, such as "Open the quote". LinkPage does not draw that link, so a product that offers it writes the page with OutsidePage and Facts.

**Report page.** The link "This was not me" of a security notice opens a link page (see `### Security mail` in `architecture/web-app.md`). Its title asks about the event, such as "Report the new device as not yours?" The facts are the event, the device or the passkey, and the time. The text says what the button does: it suspends that device, ends the sessions of that device and tells the administrators. When that device holds the last passkey of the person that is not suspended, the text says that the button only tells the administrators. Three reports need a sign-in first. They are the report after a recovery, the report after a sign-in with an authenticator app and the report of a new authenticator app. Their page first shows the button "Sign in with a passkey" with the `key-round` icon. After that sign-in, "This was not me" suspends the passkey and the recovery code of the recovery. After a sign-in with an authenticator app, it disables the authenticator app and suspends the passkey that the device made. On the report of a new authenticator app, it disables that authenticator app. With no such sign-in, the button only tells the administrators. The link "This was not me" of a sign-in with an authenticator app works for a time, such as 7 days. For that time, the passkey that the device made after the sign-in does not count as a passkey of the person here. After a sign-in with that passkey, the button only tells the administrators. When the device made no passkey, the text says that the button ends the sessions of that device and tells the administrators. The button then takes `variant="destructive"` too, because it ends sessions. The primary button "This was not me" takes `variant="destructive"`, because it suspends the device. When it only tells the administrators, it takes the `default` variant. Done says what the app did: the device that it suspended and the sessions that it ended, or that it only told the administrators. After a suspension, Done also says how the person lifts it: with a sign-in with another passkey, or through an administrator. For an authenticator app, Done says that the person adds the authenticator app again. An event does not change, so the page never shows Changed. Expired has no "Send a new link": its text names the administrators. LinkPage draws a report that needs no sign-in, with the `destructive` or the `default` variant. The product writes a report that needs a sign-in first with OutsidePage and Facts.

**Read-only link.** A read-only link shows content and has no action, such as the visits of a month. Its title names the content. Its text says who can see the page and until when, such as "Anyone with this link can see this page until 31 October 2026." A table or a chart may be wider than the column, inside the side padding, and running text keeps the reading width. Its other states are Expired and Not valid, as on a link page. The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/ReadOnlyPage` draws it. The product gives `initial`, `title`, `until` and the content as `children`. The part writes "Anyone with this link can see this page until" and the date of `until`, and `text` adds more words after it, such as what the page does not show. The part also writes the texts of Expired and Not valid.

**Sign-in page.** The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/SignInPage` draws this page and its steps. The product gives `passkey`, `deviceCode`, `addPasskey`, `ondone` and `links.recovery`, and `appCode` where it offers an authenticator app. The title names the action and the product, such as "Sign in to Product", and one line of text names the method. The primary button "Sign in with a passkey" has the `key-round` icon, which its `spinner` replaces in Waiting. Where a new device signs in with a code from a device that is signed in, a `field-separator` with the word "or" follows. Then a `field` holds the label "Email address" and an `input` with `type="email"` and `autocomplete="username"`. The code works only with the address of the account (see `### Sign-in` in `architecture/web-app.md`). A second `field` holds the label "Code from another device" and an `input-otp` of 8 characters in two groups of four.
