The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/CodeInput` draws it with `kind="device"`, and with `kind="app"` the 6 digits below. `@stack-kit/stack/outside/codes` gives `readCode`, which reads a code as Crockford base32 does, so the server of the product reads it the same way. An `outline` Continue button stands beside the cells, and a description under them says where the code shows. The product gives that description as `codeHelp`, because it names its own settings. The code belongs to the sign-in that this device started, so the page shows Code expired for a code from an earlier visit. Continue always submits, so the button is never disabled. An empty address shows the field error "Type the email address of your account." A code of fewer than 8 characters shows "Type the 8 characters." Under the form, a `link` button "Use a recovery code" opens the recovery page. It has no side padding (`px-0`), so its text keeps the left edge of the column. On a phone it keeps the width of its text, because it opens a page and is not an action of the form. Where the product offers an authenticator app, a `link` button "Use a code from an authenticator app" comes before it, with the same look. The two links stack with no space between them, 32 px high each and 44 px on a phone (measured). A page that creates the first passkey, such as the setup of an account, has the same parts and states, with "Create a passkey" as its action.

| State | What the page shows | Region |
|---|---|---|
| Ready | The page as above | None |
| Waiting | The browser shows its passkey sheet. The passkey button shows a `spinner`, keeps its label and is `disabled` with `aria-busy="true"`. A state line in `small` and `muted-foreground` says "Choose a passkey in the window of your browser." | `role="status"` |
| Failed | The server refused the passkey. A state line in `destructive` with an icon says what failed and the fix, such as "This app does not know that passkey. Choose the passkey that you made here, or use a code from another device." The button acts again. | `role="alert"` |
| Cancelled | The browser closed its sheet with no passkey. A state line in `muted-foreground` with an info icon, never in `destructive`, says "No passkey was chosen." and the two ways on. | `role="status"` |
| Code wrong | The `field-error` "This code is not right for this address. Check the address and the 8 characters on the other device." A wrong address shows the same error, so that the page does not tell which addresses have an account. Each cell takes `aria-invalid="true"` until the person changes the code. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| Code expired | The `field-error` "This code has run out. Ask the other device for a new code." The cells are as in Code wrong. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| Not checked | A code function rejected, so the page has no answer from the server. The `field-error` "This app could not check the code. Choose Continue to try again." Continue stays. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |

**Code from an authenticator app.** The link "Use a code from an authenticator app" opens the sign-in page with the form of the authenticator app. That form takes the place of the form of the code from another device (see `### Authenticator app` in `architecture/web-app.md`). The link is a plain link to the same page with the parameter `method=app`, so it works with no script. The part offers the link only when the product gives `appCode`. The passkey button, its states and the `field-separator` stay. The form holds the `field` "Email address" as above. A second `field` holds the label "Code from your authenticator app" and an `input-otp` of 6 digits in two groups of three (see `### input-otp`). An `outline` Continue button stands beside the cells. The description under them is "Open the authenticator app on your phone and type the 6 digits that it shows for Product." Continue always submits. An empty address shows "Type the email address of your account.", and a code of fewer than 6 digits shows "Type the 6 digits." Under the form, a `link` button "Use a code from another device" opens the first form again, and "Use a recovery code" follows it. The page shows one code form at a time, because each code needs the address, and two forms would ask for it twice.

| State | What the form shows | Region |
|---|---|---|
| Ready | The form as above | None |
| App code wrong | The `field-error` "This code is not right for this address. Check the address and the 6 digits in your authenticator app." A wrong address shows the same error, and so does the code of a person whose workspace turned the authenticator app off. Each cell takes `aria-invalid="true"` until the person changes the code. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| App code used | The server already accepted a code of that step. The `field-error` "This code was used already. Wait for the next code in your authenticator app. If you did not sign in with it a moment ago, sign in with a passkey and check the security page." The cells are as in App code wrong. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| App paused | After five wrong codes, the server takes the next code only after a wait. The `field-error` names the end of the wait and the other way, such as "Too many wrong codes. Try again after 14:35, or sign in with a passkey." After 100 wrong codes in a row, the server disables the authenticator app. The `field-error` then says "This authenticator app works no more. Sign in with a passkey or use a recovery code, then add the authenticator app again." Continue stays. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| Not checked | A code function rejected, so the page has no answer from the server. The `field-error` "This app could not check the code. Choose Continue to try again." Continue stays. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |

**Passkey on a new device.** After a right code from another device or from an authenticator app, the sign-in page shows the step "Make a passkey on this device" (see `### Sign-in` in `architecture/web-app.md`). The title is "Make a passkey on this device". The text is "With a passkey here, this device signs in with no code next time. On a computer that you borrowed, choose Not now: you then stay signed in for this session only." The primary button "Create a passkey" has the `key-round` icon. An `outline` button "Not now" follows it. A new passkey and Not now both run `ondone`, and the product then opens the address that the person asked for before the sign-in. The step has no facts and no field.

| State | What the step shows | Region |
|---|---|---|
| Ready | The step as above | None |
| Waiting | The browser shows its sheet that makes a passkey. Create a passkey shows a `spinner`, keeps its label and is `disabled` with `aria-busy="true"`. A state line in `small` and `muted-foreground` says "Follow the steps in the window of your browser." | `role="status"` |
| Failed | The server refused the new passkey. A state line in `destructive` with an icon says what failed and the fix, such as "This app could not save the passkey. Choose Create a passkey to try again, or choose Not now." | `role="alert"` |
| Cancelled | The browser closed its sheet with no passkey. A state line in `muted-foreground` with an info icon, never in `destructive`, says "No passkey was made." and the two ways on. | `role="status"` |

**Recovery page.** A person with no passkey and no device that is signed in gets back in here (see `### Ways back in` in `architecture/web-app.md`). The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/RecoveryPage` draws it. The product gives `recoveryCode`, `setupCode`, `addPasskey`, `notNow` and `ondone`. The page takes the saved recovery code or a setup code from an administrator. The title is "Use a recovery code", and its text says when to use each code. The page holds two forms, one for each code, and each form posts on its own. The first form holds a `field` with the label "Email address" and an `input` with `type="email"` and `autocomplete="username"`, as on the sign-in page. The code works only with the address of its account. A second `field` holds the label "Recovery code", an `input` and the description "The code has 16 characters. You saved it when you made your passkey." The input takes `autocapitalize="characters"`, `spellcheck="false"` and `autocomplete="off"`, and it takes the code with or without the hyphens. Its Continue is the primary button. An empty address shows the field error "Type the email address of your account." A code of fewer than 16 characters shows "Type the 16 characters." A `field-separator` with the word "or" follows. The second form holds a `field` with the label "Setup code from an administrator" and an `input`. Its description is "An administrator reads you this code by phone. It has 26 characters." The input takes `autocapitalize="characters"`, `spellcheck="false"` and `autocomplete="off"`, and it takes the code with or without the spaces and the hyphens. Its Continue is an `outline` button, and a code of fewer than 26 characters shows "Type the 26 characters." Each Continue always submits, so neither button is ever disabled. A right code in either form leads to the step that creates a passkey. It takes the title, the text, the buttons and the states of the step "Make a passkey on this device". So it also offers "Not now". On this page, Not now calls `notNow`, which spends the code. The functions `addPasskey` and `notNow` each answer with the new recovery code. So both ways end with that code, which the page shows once, as "Recovery code" below says. Continue under the code runs `ondone`, and the product goes on. The page has two forms and not one field that takes either code, because only the saved code needs the address of its account. So each form shows only the fields and the errors of its own code.

| State | What the page shows | Region |
|---|---|---|
| Ready | The page as above | None |
| Code wrong | The `field-error` "This recovery code is not right for this address. Check the address and the 16 characters." A wrong address shows the same error, so that the page does not tell which addresses have an account. The input takes `aria-invalid="true"` until the person changes the code. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| Paused | After five wrong codes, the server takes the next code only after a wait, as `### Ways back in` in `architecture/web-app.md` says. The `field-error` names the end of the wait and the other way, such as "Too many wrong codes. Try again after 14:35, or ask an administrator of your workspace for a setup link or a setup code." After 100 wrong codes in a row, the `field-error` names only the other way, such as "This recovery code works no more. Ask an administrator of your workspace for a setup link or a setup code." Wrong setup codes count for each client address, and the second form shows the same wait, such as "Too many wrong codes. Try again after 14:35." Continue stays. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| Setup code wrong | The `field-error` "This setup code is not right. Check the 26 characters with the administrator." The input takes `aria-invalid="true"` until the person changes the code. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| Setup code used | Somebody used the code, or its 10 minutes ran out. The `field-error` "This setup code works no more. Ask the administrator for a new code." The input is as in Setup code wrong. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |
| Not checked | A code function rejected, so the page has no answer from the server. The `field-error` "This app could not check the code. Choose Continue to try again." Continue stays. | The `alert` role of `field-error` |

**Recovery code.** After the first passkey of an account, and after each recovery, the page shows the new recovery code once. The title is "Save your recovery code". The text says three facts. The code gets the person in with no passkey and no device. It works once, and the page shows it only now. A `code-block` holds the code in four groups of four, such as `7K4Q-X2MD-R8F3-W6PN`, with its copy button. The primary button Continue opens the app, and an `outline` button "Print" follows it. The code never goes into an email or into the address of the page.

**Setup link.** The setup link of an administrator opens a page that creates a passkey, with "Create a passkey" as its action. The part `@stack-kit/stack/outside/SetupLinkPage` draws it. The product gives `addPasskey`, `notNow` and `ondone`, and each of the first two answers with the new recovery code. The title names the product, such as "Set up a passkey for Product". The text, the buttons and the states are those of the step "Make a passkey on this device". So the page also offers "Not now". Not now calls `notNow`, which spends the token of the link, as Not now on the recovery page spends the code. After the new passkey or Not now, the page shows the recovery code. A link that does not work shows Expired, Already used or Not valid, as a link page does. The text of Expired names the administrator, who makes a new link, and the page has no "Send a new link". On the screen of the administrator, the link shows in the part `qr-code` (see `### qr-code`), and the person scans it with a phone. That part takes `kind="text"`, so it has no button "Open the link". Only the phone of the person opens the link, never the browser of the administrator. By phone, the screen of the administrator shows the setup code in a `code-block`, in groups of four, and the administrator reads it aloud. A line under the code gives the administrator the first words to say. An example is "Open the sign-in page of Product and choose Use a recovery code. Type this code in the second field." A right setup code on the recovery page leads to the same page that creates a passkey. The instruction of the `qr-code` says "Let the person scan this code. Never send the link in a message." A line over the `code-block` of the setup code says "Read this code aloud. Never send it in a message."

**No script.** A link page is a plain form, so it works with no script: the browser posts the form, and the next page shows the result. Working does not show then, and the browser shows its own load mark. A passkey needs a script. So a `<noscript>` block in `<head>` hides the passkey button with a style rule, and a `<noscript>` line shows in its place: "A passkey needs JavaScript. Turn on JavaScript, or use a code from another device." OutsidePage writes both blocks from its `noscript` line, and the rule hides each element with the attribute `data-needs-js`. The code form posts with no script. On the form of an authenticator app, the line says "A passkey needs JavaScript. Turn on JavaScript, or use a code from your authenticator app." That form and its link work with no script too. The sign-in page and the recovery page show the step "Make a passkey on this device" only with a script, because their script sends the code and then shows the step. With no script, the code form posts, and the product answers the post of a right code itself. The setup link opens at that step, so its line says "A passkey needs JavaScript. Turn on JavaScript, or choose Not now." On the setup link, Not now is a form that posts, so it works with no script.

**Phone.** Below 768 px the head line and the column reach the edges of the screen, and no part has an edge or a ground of its own. The buttons take the full width of the column (`max-md:w-full`) and stack with the primary on top, as the footer of a dialog does. The code row stacks too: the cells, then Continue at the full width. Every button is 44 px high (`button-phone`). Each cell of the code is 32 px wide and 44 px high (`input-otp-slot-phone`, see `### input-otp`). The row of 8 cells is 272 px wide, and the row of 6 cells is 208 px wide (measured at 390 px). The page touches all four edges, so it keeps the safe area of each edge in every band, as `### Width bands` lists. No Tailwind class writes `env()`, so a style block writes the insets. It sets the padding of the head line and of the column, and the height of the head line, which is 44 px plus the top inset. With a top inset of 47 px, the head line is 91 px high, and its part under the inset stays 44 px (measured in Chromium at 390 by 844 px and at 844 by 390 px, with emulated insets).

**Accessibility.** The `<title>` of the page names the state first, then the product, such as "This link has expired · Product", and it changes with the title of the page. A sign-in page keeps "Sign in · Product". The head line is the `header` landmark and the column is the `main` landmark, with no skip link, as "Structure and order" in `architecture/web-app.md` says. The title is the one `h1`, and a part under it that has a heading takes `h2`. No control takes the focus on load, so a person reads the facts before the button. Waiting and Cancelled show in a `role="status"` region and a failure in a `role="alert"` region. Each region is in the page from the first render, so a screen reader reads each new text. Working needs no region, because its `spinner` has the `status` role. A focused button that turns `disabled` loses the focus to `<body>` (measured in Chromium 148). So when Working or Waiting ends, the page puts the focus back. The focus goes to the title when the state removes the button, as Done does, and the title takes `tabindex="-1"` for this. It goes to the button when the button stays, as in Failed and Cancelled.

### Lists

**Group header.** A grouped list has a 32 px header (`spacing.group-header`, `h-8`) above each group, at level 1: the `muted` ground and a `border` edge below. The header has 12 px padding on the left, 16 px on the right and an 8 px gap. It holds a fold chevron in `muted-foreground`, then the status icon at 16 px when the list groups by status. The group name in `body` type at 600 and the count in `muted-foreground` come next. On the right it may show one total of the group in `small` type and `muted-foreground`. Numbers use tabular figures. The header sticks under the top bar while the rows of its group scroll. A folded group points its chevron right and hides its rows. A group of closed items may start folded, and the product decides which group does.

**Row.** A row is 32 px high (`spacing.row`, `h-8`), on `background`, with a `border-faint` divider below. It has 12 px padding on the left, 16 px on the right and a 12 px gap between the cells, in `body` type. The cells come in a fixed order from the left: the check box, the status, the identifier and the title. The data columns of the product follow at fixed widths. The title takes the rest of the width and ends in an ellipsis. A time or a count in a row is `muted-foreground`, which passes 4.5:1 on every ground of a row. The check box shows on hover, on focus and while any row is selected. In a list grouped by status, the 14 px status icon replaces the status badge. A list grouped in another way shows the badge. A grouped list has no row of column headers, and the display menu names the columns. A table view keeps its header row (see `### table`).

**Row of the work form.** A list in the work form has no data columns, so its row puts the facts of the item in two or three lines. Line 1 holds the identifier and the title in `body` type. Line 2 holds the key values in `small` type. Line 3, where the item needs it, holds one more fact in `small` type and `muted-foreground`, such as the first words of the newest message. Each line is 20 px high (`h-5`) and ends in an ellipsis. The end of a line holds its time, its value or its marks, in `muted-foreground` with tabular figures. The row has 6 px above its lines (`pt-1.5`) and 6 px under them, the 1 px divider included. So a row of two lines is 52 px high (`spacing.row-phone`, `h-13`), as the phone row of band 1. A row of three lines is 72 px high (`spacing.row-three-line`, `h-18`). On a list of the example, a row of three lines was 72 px and each line 20 px. The first line started 6 px under the top of the row (measured). The row keeps the padding, the gap and the order of the cells of a row. The check box and the status icon stand on the height of line 1, and the lines follow. A list gives every row the same number of lines, so every row has one height. Below 768 px the row keeps two lines, as the phone row. A row holds no action button, because the item column beside it holds the actions of the item. The head of a group, such as a plan over its child items, is not a row. It may hold one `outline` button of size `sm` that acts on every row of the group, such as "Approve all 3". The item column holds the actions of one item only, so it cannot hold that action. The row states stay. The row of the item that the column shows is the current row. It takes `aria-current="true"` and the `muted` ground, because the column always shows one item, and the person must see which.

**Unread mark.** In a list with a read state, line 1 of each row ends in the place of the `unread-mark`, after the time. The place is 8 px wide and stands 8 px after the time, so the times of the rows align (measured). An unread row shows the mark there, and its title takes 500 (`font-medium`), as its identifier does. So the state shows as a shape and as a weight, and never as a color alone. "Read state" in `### Detail pane` says when an item counts as read. Then the mark goes at once, and the title goes back to 400.

**Row states.** Each state of a row shows as a ground, a mark or a change of the text.

| State | Look |
|---|---|
| Rest | `background` |
| Hover | `muted` at 50% (`hover:bg-muted/50`), as the nova table row draws it |
| Focused, with J and K or Tab | The focus ring of `## Shapes`, inside the edge of the row (`ring-inset`), so that the next row does not cover it |
| Selected | The `selected` ground, and the check box on |
| Focused and selected | Both at once |
| Current, in the work form | The muted ground, and `aria-current="true"` on the button of line 1 |
| Unread, in a list with a read state | The `unread-mark` at the end of line 1, and the title at 500 |
| Closed | The title in `muted-foreground`. A cancelled item is also struck through (`line-through`). |
| Failed write | The row keeps the value of the person, and an inline line under the row offers the retry |

**Selection.** While one or more rows are selected, the selection bar holds their count and their actions in the place of the content of the top bar (see `### selection-bar`). No row moves when the bar shows, so the next click lands on the row that the person aims at. The selection never shows in a row under the top bar, because such a row moves every row down by 36 px and the next click lands on another row. A row of filter chips under the top bar stays while the selection bar shows.

**Order.** A list in an order that the person sets, such as the steps of a sequence, is a `reorder-list`. Each row has a handle, and its More menu holds Move up and Move down. A list that sorts by a field shows no handle, because a move there has no meaning.

**Create button.** The New button of the sidebar head creates the main record of the product, such as an invoice or a ticket, from every page. A record that the New button does not create, such as a machine or a loop, gets a create button at the head of its list:

- A page that lists the record: the right end of the top bar, after the filter and the display controls.
- A list in a section of a page, a pane or the settings, such as the labels of a project: the right end of the heading of that section.
- A list with no item yet: its empty state holds the same action as its one button, under the same label (see `### empty`), and the button of the top bar stays.

The button is `outline`, of size `sm`, with an icon at its start that shows the verb, such as `plus` for Create and Add. Its label is a verb and the record, such as "Create loop", "Add machine" or "Import a backlog". The command palette holds the same action under the same label. Below 768 px the button of the top bar goes into the header of band 1, as one of its two actions. There it is a `ghost` icon button of 44 by 44 px (`size="icon"`, `size-11`) with the same icon, and its label is its accessible name.

**Mark all as read.** A list with a read state has one action that marks every unread item of its filter as read. It is an `outline` button of size `sm` with the `check-check` icon, and its label names the count, such as "Mark 12 as read". It stands first on the right side of the top bar, before the filter and the display controls. It shows only while the filter holds an unread item. When it goes, the controls after it keep their place (measured). The action runs at once. The button then goes, and the focus moves to the current row. While the item column is closed, the focus moves to the first row (measured). A `sonner` toast says "12 marked as read" with Undo. Undo gives the mark back to those 12 items only (measured), so the button shows again. The command palette holds the same action under the same label. While rows are selected, the selection bar holds Mark as read and Mark as unread for them. Each applies to every selected row, read or unread, so the selection bar never blocks it. Below 768 px the button goes into the header of band 1, as the create button does. There it is a `ghost` icon button of 44 by 44 px with the same icon. Its label is its accessible name (measured at 390 px).

**Notices.** A list of notices tells each person about new work, such as a case for that person or a task that failed. The person opens each notice and then goes to the next, so the list takes the work form and the unread mark. Line 1 names the actor and the object, as a line of activity does (see "Words" in `architecture/web-app.md`). When the list sorts by time, it groups its rows by day under the head of a day of `### day-log`. The item column shows the record that the notice names.

**Views.** The view tabs of the top bar show one list in other forms, and each view keeps the filter of the list. A table compares the items by their columns (see `### table`). So a table view of a list in the work form takes the table form of the split (see `### App shell`). A board puts the items in columns by one field, such as the stage, and the person moves an item to another column (see `### board`). A month view puts the items on the days of a month by one date, such as the due date (see `### month-view`). A log by day groups the entries of a log by the day of their time (see `### day-log`).

**Density.** Under the 44 px top bar, a window of 1280 by 900 px holds 856 px of list. That is 26 rows of 32 px, group headers included: 25 item rows with one group header in view, and 24 item rows with two. In the table form, a screenshot at 1280 by 900 px shows at least 25 rows, group headers included. In the work form, 856 px hold 15 rows of two lines or 11 rows of three lines, with one group header. So a screenshot of the work form at 1280 by 900 px shows at least 15 rows of two lines, or 11 rows of three lines. A banner and a filter row take space from each count. So does any line above the rows. Above its rows, the list of the example holds a line of text of 48 px and three group headers. So it showed 9 rows of three lines (counted). The selection bar takes none, because it takes the place of the top bar.

### Detail pane

**Pane.** The pane shows the selected item beside the list, 420 px wide (`spacing.pane`, `w-105`), on `background` with a `border` edge on its left. It opens on selection and follows J and K. Enter opens the item as a full page. Esc closes the pane and keeps the selection, and the list keeps its scroll position. The pane belongs to the table form of the split. Every list that a person works one item at a time, such as an inbox, takes the work form and its item column (see `### App shell`).

**Pane header.** The header has 16 px padding. It holds the identifier in `section` type and the status badge. On the right it holds the position of the item in the list, such as "3 / 41", with up and down icon buttons. A close button comes last. One row of actions follows: at most one primary button for the current state of the item, then the secondary actions, then a More menu. The sections of the pane come next, each with a heading in `small` type at 600 in `muted-foreground`. The product names the sections.

**Properties panel.** A pane or a page shows the fields of an item as a properties panel. Each field has a row of at least 30 px (`spacing.property-row`, `min-h-7.5`). The label takes a 112 px column (`spacing.property-label`, `w-28`), in `small` type and `muted-foreground`. The value sits in a 24 px box (`spacing.property-value`) in `small` type. A ghost `button` of size `xs` draws that box: 24 px high, with 8 px side padding and the 6 px radius (`rounded-md`). The box has no edge at rest. On hover it takes the `muted` ground and a `border-strong` edge (`hover:border-border-strong`). A click or Enter opens a menu or a picker. A value is never a round pill, because a round shape marks a status.

**Property states.** A value has these states: rest, hover, and focus with the focus ring. Open shows the menu under the value. Empty shows a placeholder word in `muted-foreground`. A value that the system sets is plain text with no box and no hover. A locked value is plain text, with a lock icon in the heading of the section and a tooltip that gives the reason. A line under a value, in `meta` type and `muted-foreground`, may name the source of the value. The pane takes the `popover` ground in bands 2 and 3, so no text in the pane uses `faint-foreground`.

**Item column.** In the work form of the split, the item column takes the place of the pane. It holds the parts of the pane: the header with the position of the item and Close, the row of actions, and the sections. It follows J and K, and Enter opens the item as a full page, as from the pane. The address names the item of the column with one key and the identifier, such as `?item=QUO-0042`, so a reload and a link keep it. In band 2 the column lies over the list as the pane does, on the `popover` ground. From 1024 px it docks (see "Work form" in `### Width bands`). Below 768 px the column does not show, and the page shows the item on its own inner screen. STACK ships the work form as three parts. `@stack-kit/stack/parts/ListSplit` draws the list and the item column, `@stack-kit/stack/parts/SplitRow` draws one row, and `@stack-kit/stack/parts/ItemColumns` places the content of the item beside its properties. `itemInAddress` of `@stack-kit/stack/behaviour/item` keeps the item in the address. A page calls it with a function that gives the keys of the rows that show, and gives the result to ListSplit as `address`. Each row then takes only the `key` of its item. From 768 px, ListSplit binds J, K, Down, Up, Enter and Esc. Enter opens the full page at the address that the `href` function of the page gives. A page that keeps the open item itself gives ListSplit `open` and `key` in place of `address`, and gives each row `id`, `current` and `onopen`.

**First row.** From 1024 px, when the address names no item, the item column shows the first row of the list, and the address does not change. So the screen is never half empty. The first row is the top row that shows, so a row of a folded group is never the first row. Below 1024 px no item opens by itself, because the item covers the list there. A list with no row shows its empty state across both columns. `itemInAddress` takes the first key that the `shown` function of the page gives, and its `firstRow` is then true.

**Read state.** An item that the column shows with no act of the person stays unread, and it keeps its unread mark. "Unread mark" in `### Lists` draws that mark. It counts as read when the person opens it: with a click, with J or K, or with an address that names it. It also counts as read when the person acts on it in the column, such as with a reply. The mark of its row then goes at once, and the count beside the page drops by one. The person did not choose the first row, so a read mark that changes by itself can hide an item that nobody read. The More menu of the item column holds Mark as unread, and Mark as read while the item is unread. Mark as unread gives the row its mark back, and the item stays unread until the person opens it again (measured). The `onread` function that the page gives to `itemInAddress` runs when the item counts as read through a click, J, K or an address. It does not run for the first row.

**Esc and Close.** Esc and the close button close the item column, and the list takes the whole width. The address keeps the key with no value, such as `?item=`, so a reload keeps the column closed. The focus then goes to the row of the item, so it never falls to the page. A click on a row, J or K opens the column again. At 1280 px, Esc gave the list 1,040 px, and J opened the next row in the column (measured). The column shows with no click, so Esc reaches it last. Esc first closes an open layer, then it clears the selection, as `### selection-bar` says. Then Esc closes the column. `close()` of `itemInAddress` writes the key with no value and moves the focus to the row. ListSplit runs it on Esc, and the Close button of the page calls it. While the item holds an open form, the `keysOff` function of the page turns J, K, Enter and Esc of the list off, so that a key does not lose the change.

**Scroll.** The list column and the item column each scroll on their own (`overflow-y-auto`). Each column keeps the bottom safe area (see "Safe area" in `### Width bands`). When only the item changes, the list keeps its scroll position, and the item column scrolls to its top. At 1024 by 500 px, a click on another row kept the list at 300 px of scroll. The item went from 136 px of scroll to 0 px (measured).

**Properties beside the content.** The item column is 444 to 800 px wide from 1024 to 1440 px, and wider in a wider window. So the column places the properties panel by the width of its own box, with a container query, and not by the window. The box is the column less its side padding (`@container` on the padded part). From a box of 672 px (`@2xl`), the properties panel stands at the right of the content. At that line the content keeps 368 px, so the properties stay in view at the cost of the width of the content. It takes a column of 288 px (`@2xl:w-72`) with a `border` edge on its left. From a box of 1,152 px (`@6xl`), that column is 420 px (`spacing.pane`, `@6xl:w-105`). At that line the content keeps 716 px, so the wider column takes at most 4 px from the 720 px of the content. Below 672 px the properties panel follows the content at the full width of the box, with a `border` edge on top. At 1440 px the box was 767 px, so the properties stood beside the content, which kept 463 px. The box was 672 px at 1345 px. At 1344 px it was 671 px, and the properties followed the content, as at 1280 and 1024 px. At 1825 px the box was 1,152 px, the column 420 px and the content 716 px. At 1824 px the column was 288 px and the content 720 px (measured). In band 2 the column lies over the list at 420 px. There its properties panel is a row of values under the title, as band 2 says.

**Side column and reading column.** The properties column is a part of the item, and not a side column. A side column belongs to a page and holds the facts and the links of its object. It docks by the window from 1280 px (see `### Side column`). The properties column follows the selection and the box of the item. The content of the item keeps at most 720 px (`spacing.reading-width`, `max-w-180`). It starts at the start edge of its box, as `### Reading column` says.

### Side column

A page may hold a side column beside its main column. The side column holds the facts and the links of the object of the page. It also holds the records and the actions that support the main column, such as the seats beside the machines of a fleet. It is a part of the page and not a pane: no selection opens it, and it has no close button. The part `PageColumns` (`@stack-kit/stack/parts/PageColumns`) holds the main column and the side column with the width, the edges, the padding, the scroll and the order below. The page gives the main column as its children, the side column as its `side` snippet and the name of the side column as `sideLabel`, and it writes no class.

**Width.** The side column is 320 px wide (`spacing.side-column`, `w-80`), on `background` with a `border` edge on its left. It has 16 px padding at its sides (`px-4`), and the top padding of the main column, so that the two columns start on one line. It stands at the right of the main column from 1280 px (`xl:`), the line where the pane docks. There the main column is 720 px wide with its padding, so a line of running text is 672 px (measured at 1280 px). Below 1280 px the main column would keep less, so the side column moves under it. It then takes the full width and a `border` edge on top in place of the edge on its left. Its side padding is that of the page: 16 px below 768 px and 24 px from 768 px (`px-4 md:px-6`).

**Scroll.** From 1280 px each column scrolls on its own (`overflow-y-auto`), as the list and the pane do, so the facts stay in view. A page with a band that scrolls with both columns, such as a row of totals, scrolls as one. Below 1280 px the page scrolls as one.

**Pane.** A page with a side column opens the pane in the place of the side column. While the pane is open, the side column does not show, and Esc shows it again.

**Order.** The side column comes after the main column in the code. So a screen reader and the Tab key reach it after the main column, in the order that a person reads the page in every band. No class changes that order, such as `order-first` or `flex-row-reverse`. The side column is an `aside` with a name in the language of the product, such as `aria-label="Facts of the run"`, so it is a complementary landmark (measured in Chromium 148).

**Settings.** A section of the settings that is a form keeps the 640 px of a form, so its side column stays under the form in every band (see `### Settings and forms`).

### Reading column

A column of running text, such as the body of a ticket, a document or a chat, is at most 720 px wide (`spacing.reading-width`, `max-w-180`), as `### Type steps` says. In a wider region, the column starts at the start edge of the region, after its side padding. The rest of the width stays empty at the end. The column is never centered: no `mx-auto`, as on a page outside the shell (see `## Do's and Don'ts`). So each line keeps its place when the window or a pane changes the width of the region (measured at 1280 and 1440 px, with and without a pane). The part `ReadingColumn` (`@stack-kit/stack/parts/ReadingColumn`) holds the 720 px at the start edge, puts its parts 24 px apart (`space-6`) and breaks a long word, and the page gives it no class.

**Padding.** The side padding of the region sits outside the 720 px, so a line keeps the full reading width. On a page that padding is 16 px below 768 px and 24 px from 768 px (`px-4 md:px-6`), as on a page outside the shell. A part with a padding of its own, such as a chat, keeps that padding. A divider or a band that crosses the region, such as the edge under the header of a chat, keeps the full width of the region. The scroll bar also stays at the edge of the region. Only the content stops at 720 px.

**Chat.** The chat beside a ticket is a pane and not a side column. From 1280 px it docks at the width of the pane, 420 px (`spacing.pane`, `xl:w-105`), with a `border` edge on its left. Below 1280 px a switch in the top bar shows the ticket or the chat (measured at 1024 and 1280 px). Below 768 px an icon button in the header opens the chat in the place of the ticket, with a back button to the ticket. It is an `aside` with a name in the language of the product, such as `aria-label="Chat with the agent"`.

### Settings and forms

**Settings.** A settings page has a section nav of 220 px (`spacing.settings-nav`, `w-55`) on the left and a form of at most 640 px (`spacing.settings-form`, `max-w-160`). A section that holds a list beside the editor of one item, such as the roles of a workspace, takes the full width beside the nav. Its two columns need more than 640 px. The nav keeps the personal settings of the person in a group apart from the settings of the workspace. Each section of the form has a heading with one line of description, and the section can fold. A row holds a label and its control. A sticky save button stays `disabled` until a field changes. It has no reason to give, so it leaves the tab order. A control that a rule blocks, such as a setting that only an owner changes, is disabled with a reason, as `### button` says. Below 768 px the section nav is the first screen, and each section opens as its own screen.

**Form foot.** A long form, such as an editor, has a sticky foot with the save action and the primary action. When the form saves itself, the foot shows the time of the last save in `faint-foreground`. Below 768 px a long form on an inner screen ends in the foot bar of `### Width bands`, which takes the place of the form foot. In print the foot takes `print:static`, so that it ends the form (see `### A screen page in print`).

**Authenticator app.** Where the product offers an authenticator app, the security page of the account has a section "Authenticator app" (see `### Authenticator app` in `architecture/web-app.md`). Its line of description states the limit: "Sign in with 6 digits from an app on your phone. A passkey is safer: a false page can ask for the 6 digits and use them at once." The `outline` button "Add an authenticator app" opens the setup inside the section. In a session that no passkey made, the button first asks for a passkey, as the sign-in page does. The setup opens after a right passkey. Replace and Remove do the same. The link "This was not me" of a sign-in with an authenticator app works for a time, such as 7 days. For that time, the passkey that the device made after the sign-in cannot add, replace or remove an authenticator app. In a session that such a passkey made, the button "Add an authenticator app" is disabled with a reason, as `### button` says. Replace and Remove are disabled with the same reason. The reason names the rule and the date: "You made this passkey after a sign-in with an authenticator app. It can do this from 3 November 2026." The setup holds a `qr-code` with `kind="text"` and the instruction "Scan this code with the authenticator app on your phone, or type the setup key into it." The name of the QR code is "QR code of the setup key", and its caption is "Setup key". The code block beside the QR code shows the setup key in groups of four, and its copy button writes the key with no space (see `### qr-code`). At 390 px the key takes two lines, and it breaks between two groups (measured). A `field` follows with the label "Code from your authenticator app" and an `input-otp` of 6 digits, as on the sign-in page. The primary button "Add the authenticator app" stands beside the cells. The description is "Type the 6 digits that the authenticator app shows now." A short code shows "Type the 6 digits." A wrong code shows "This code is not right. Type the code that your authenticator app shows now, or scan the code again." After 10 minutes the setup ends, and the field error says "This setup has run out. Choose Add an authenticator app again." With an authenticator app, the section shows the date when the person added it, and two `outline` buttons. When a workspace of the person does not allow an authenticator app, the section shows only the line "Your workspace Belgrade office does not allow an authenticator app." and no button. "Replace" opens the same setup, and "Remove" asks first in an `alert-dialog` with the `destructive` variant. After Remove, the security notice of the removal goes out. The preview shows the setup in the showcase of `qr-code`, with the field of the first code. It does not show the security page, the states of the setup, Replace or Remove yet.

### Summary page

A summary page shows the state of one area at a glance. Examples are the page of today, the summary of a campaign and the report of a month. Its key figures stand in one band under the title of the page, before every other part (see `### figure-band`). The lists and the meters of the page follow in bands with dividers, 32 px apart (`space-8`). The figures, the lists and the meters take no card, as `## Do's and Don'ts` asks. The part `SummaryBands` (`@stack-kit/stack/parts/SummaryBands`) holds the bands: each child is one band, and the part draws the `border` divider between two bands, 32 px from each band. The page gives it no class.

### Chart legend

A chart legend sits in the normal flow under the chart, and never in the padding of the chart. A legend that wraps onto more lines pushes the content under it down. So the legend never covers an axis, a bar or the hole of a donut, in any band. `## Charts` gives the look of the legend.

### Direction

The shell and the components run left to right only. A product ships no interface in a language that writes from right to left, such as Arabic or Hebrew, and sets no `dir="rtl"` on `<html>`. Content in such a language inside the interface works: it takes its own `lang` and `dir`, as `### Content in another language` says.

A measurement of the preview under `<html dir="rtl">` decided this, in Chromium 148 at 1280 px. A layout that supports right to left puts each text and each icon at its mirror place, about the middle of the window.

- 38 of the 67 showcases mirror fully. 27 keep at least one part in place. These parts include the table, the slider, the select of the native kind, the input group, the time field, the alert and the chart. The other 2 of the 67 are the code block and the code editor, which stay left to right on purpose.
- The floating layers stay left to right. Bits UI gives the content of a select, a menu, a context menu and a popover `dir="ltr"`, because its `dir` prop defaults to `ltr` and does not read the page. A submenu opens to the right, and only Right opens it.
- The slider keeps its range on the left, and Right raises the value. In the calendar, Right moves to the next day, which a right-to-left grid shows on the left.
- 19 of the 29 icons that point left or right do not turn: in the breadcrumb, the pagination, the carousel, the item, the sidebar trigger and a button. The buttons of the calendar turn.
- The vendored components hold 206 physical direction classes in 40 of the 61 folders, and 33 logical ones (counted). The 206 include about 13 centering classes and a few that Tailwind 4 already writes as logical properties. They also include the slide animations of a floating layer, which follow its physical side.
- Outside this part, the rules of this file name a side, the left or the right, on 131 lines (counted).

Some parts follow the direction already. Bits UI reads the direction of the page for the arrow keys of the tabs, the toggle group, the radio group, the accordion, the toolbar and the pagination. So Left moves to the next tab (measured on the tabs). The nova sidebar places itself with logical classes. In the example screens it moves to the right edge with its edge on the left, and the pane moves to the left. But 201 of the 294 texts and icons of that screen do not mirror (measured).

A product that needs a right-to-left interface proposes it as a pull request. The work has these parts:

- `dir` on `<html>` from the locale, and the `dir` prop on each part of Bits UI that has one. These parts are the root of a menu, a context menu, a menubar, a navigation menu, a slider and a scroll area, and the content of each floating layer.
- A logical class in the place of each physical class of a vendored component: `ps-` and `pe-`, `ms-` and `me-`, `start-` and `end-`, `border-s` and `border-e`, `rounded-s` and `rounded-e`, `text-start` and `text-end`. The rules of this section then name the start and the end in the place of the left and the right.
- `rtl:rotate-180` on each icon that points forward or back.
- A key handler for the calendar, until Bits UI reads the direction there.
- A decision on the direction of the time axis of a chart.
- The same measurement again, with content in Arabic or Hebrew.

## Elevation & Depth

Depth comes from steps of the ground first. A shadow belongs to a floating layer only, and a static surface has no shadow.

| Level | Ground | Edge | Shadow | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | `background` | none | none | The page, a list, a pane |
| 1 | `sidebar` or `muted` | `border` | none | The sidebar, a group header |
| 2 | `card` | `ring-1 ring-foreground/10` | none | A card |
| 3 | `popover` | `ring-1 ring-foreground/10` | `shadow-md`; `shadow-lg` for a submenu and a sheet | A menu, a popover, a select list, a hover card, the lifted row of a `reorder-list`, the lifted card of a `board` |
| 4 | `popover` | `ring-1 ring-foreground/10` | none, over a scrim | A dialog and an alert dialog |

The nova components use these Tailwind shadows and no others, except the chart tooltip, which draws `shadow-xl` (see `### chart`). A layer that a product draws itself takes the same classes. The nova dialog, sheet and drawer draw their scrim as `bg-black/10` with a small blur. A scrim that a product draws itself uses `bg-overlay`.

**Motion.** Motion is short and calm. Animate opacity and transform only, never a size or the layout. No bounce, no spring and no loop.

- The nova components animate through `tw-animate-css`. A popup fades, zooms from 95% and moves 8 px from its side in 100 ms. A dialog fades and zooms from 95% in 100 ms.
- Motion that a product writes itself takes `motion.duration-fast` for hover, press and color, and `motion.duration-pane` for a pane, with the curve `motion.ease-stack`. A pane slides in from the right. A row that changes its place in a `reorder-list`, and a card that changes its place on a `board`, slides there in `motion.duration-fast`, because it moves a short way.
- Each key of `motion` is also the name of its class, such as `transition-colors duration-fast ease-stack` on a hover. `stack.css` writes a duration as `--transition-duration-<name>` and the curve as `--ease-stack`, because Tailwind CSS 4 reads the class `duration-<name>` from `--transition-duration-<name>` (measured with 4.3.3). A raw value, such as `duration-150` or `duration-[120ms]`, is a raw duration.
- Under `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` nothing moves. A change of color or opacity of `motion.duration-fast` or less stays, because it does not move. A transition that moves, such as a pane that slides in, also takes `motion-reduce:transition-none`, and a row of a `reorder-list` jumps to its new place. Smooth scrolling stops, and an animated image stays still.

## Shapes

**Radius.** A theme sets one `--radius`. `stack.css` derives the levels from it: `rounded-sm` is 0.5 of it, `rounded-md` is 0.75 of it, and `rounded-lg` to `rounded-4xl` are equal to it. At `--radius: 0.5rem` that gives 4, 6 and 8 px, which are `rounded.sm`, `rounded.md` and `rounded.lg` in the front matter. Nothing is larger than 8 px, except the full radius.

| Radius | Where the nova components draw it |
|---|---|
| 8 px: `rounded-lg`, `rounded-xl`, `rounded-4xl` | Buttons, inputs, select triggers, text areas, tab lists, badges, menus, popovers, cards, dialogs, alerts, toasts, items, attachments, bubbles and empty states, and an item of the command palette in a dialog |
| 6 px: `rounded-md` | An item in a menu, a select list or the sidebar, a tab trigger, a day in a calendar, the small and the extra small button, a skeleton, a tooltip, the scroll button of a message scroller and the key mark of a questionnaire |
| 4 px: `rounded-sm` and `rounded-[4px]` | A check box, a Kbd, the image of an item, the check box of a questionnaire, an item of the command palette in a popover, a trigger of a menubar and the action button of a toast |
| full: `rounded-full` | The shapes that are round by nature: an avatar, a radio button, a switch, a slider, a progress track, a scroll bar thumb, the buttons of a carousel and the reactions of a bubble |

The status badge, the dot of a label and the unread mark are the elements that a product makes round on purpose (`rounded-full`). A property value, a count badge and a chip keep the radius of their component.

**Borders and focus.** Every border is 1 px. The focus ring is the only thicker line. It shows on `:focus-visible` only. The nova controls draw it as a 3 px ring of `ring` at 50% (`ring-3 ring-ring/50`) outside a 1 px edge in `ring`, and the ring follows the radius of its element.

**Icons.** One icon set: Lucide, through `@lucide/svelte`. The sizes are 14, 16 and 20 px (`spacing.icon-sm`, `icon-md` and `icon-lg`), and 16 px is the default. The nova components draw their icons at 16 px (`size-4`) with the default stroke, and an icon that a product places takes the same stroke, so that all icons on a screen match.

**Status icons.** A status icon is a ring on a 14 px grid, 12 px across, that fills as the item advances. A mark inside a disc is cut out, so that it reads on any ground. A status icon is round because it is an icon, not an element. It is 14 px in a row, a chip and an activity line, and 16 px in a group header. Each product draws its own set from this recipe.

## Components

Each component of the registry snapshot has one definition in this section, under a `###` heading with its registry name. A definition has nine parts, in this order: Purpose, Anatomy, Variants, Sizes, States, Tokens, Accessibility, Example and Phone. Each part is a paragraph that starts with its name in bold, such as `**Purpose.**`, and a fenced code block follows the Example part. `npm run components` checks that each component has a definition with the nine parts, and that each key in a Tokens part exists in `components`. A part that the registry lacks and that STACK adds, such as `time-field` or `code-editor`, has a definition in the same form under its own name, after the component nearest to it. STACK ships each such part as a file of `preview/src/lib/stack/`, and a product imports it from `@stack-kit/stack/parts/<Name>`, such as `@stack-kit/stack/parts/CodeEditor`. The check lists such parts in `STACK_PARTS`.

**The keys of `components`.** A key is `<data-slot>[-variant][-size][-state]`. `<data-slot>` is the `data-slot` value that the component sets on the part, such as `button` or `select-item`. A part that sets no `data-slot`, such as each part of `calendar` or a toast of `sonner`, takes the name of its part attribute without `data-`, such as `calendar-day` for `[data-calendar-day]` or `sonner-toast` for `[data-sonner-toast]`, and it can have no phone key, because `stack.css` selects a phone key by its `data-slot`. A part of a library can set neither a `data-slot` nor a part attribute, such as the gutter of `code-editor` (`.cm-gutters` of CodeMirror). Its key is the `data-slot` of the slot that holds it and the name of the part, such as `code-editor-gutter`. That last segment names a part, not a variant, and such a part can have no phone key either. A size key holds `height`, `padding`, `rounded` and `typography` only. A phone key, `<data-slot>-phone`, holds what changes in the slot below 768 px. Its touch target is `height`, `width` or `size`, each a reference to a spacing token such as `"{spacing.touch}"`. `stack.css` writes `height` as `min-height`, `width` as `min-width` and `size` as both, in `@layer base` under the phone media query, so that a utility class of a product still wins. Its `typography`, a reference to a type step, gives the slot the font size and the line height of that step. `stack.css` writes it in `@layer utilities` after the utilities of Tailwind, so that it beats the text class of the component itself. `npm run theme -- --check` refuses any other property in a phone key. The last segment is a state only when it is a name in the table below. A value is a reference, such as `"{colors.primary}"`, and never a value. A key that sets only one of `backgroundColor` and `textColor` takes the other from its nearest parent key, which is the key without its last segment. `borderColor` is the one property beyond the eight of the format.

**States and their selectors.** A state names its selectors in both forms that the components use. Bits UI writes a `data-state` value for checked, open and active, and a presence attribute for disabled, highlighted, selected, placeholder and readonly. Hover, pressed and focus-visible are pseudo-classes. The custom variants of `preview/src/lib/components/ui/shadcn-svelte.css` match both forms: `data-open:` matches `[data-state="open"]` and `[data-open]`.

| State | Meaning | Selectors |
|---|---|---|
| (none) | Rest | none |
| `hover` | The pointer is over the element | `:hover` |
| `pressed` | A pointer or a key presses the element | `:active` |
| `focus-visible` | The element has keyboard focus | `:focus-visible` |
| `disabled` | The element takes no input | `:disabled`, `[data-disabled]` |
| `readonly` | The value shows and does not change | `[data-readonly]`, `[readonly]` |
| `invalid` | The value fails validation | `[aria-invalid="true"]`; a time field: `[data-invalid]` on its box and `[aria-invalid="true"]` on each segment, which Bits UI writes; a code editor: `[aria-invalid="true"]` on its text, a child of the box, which `has-aria-invalid:` matches on the box |
| `loading` | An action runs | `[data-loading]`, which the product sets |
| `open`, `closed` | A popup is open or closed | `[data-state="open"]` or `[data-open]`; `[data-state="closed"]` or `[data-closed]`; a tooltip: `[data-state="instant-open"]` or `[data-state="delayed-open"]` |
| `checked`, `unchecked`, `indeterminate` | The value of a check box, a radio button or a switch | `[data-state="checked"]` or `[data-checked]`, and the same for the other two. A switch has no `data-checked`. A choice of a questionnaire has `[data-checked]` or `[data-unchecked]` and no `data-state`. |
| `selected` | An item in a list is chosen | `[data-selected]`, `[aria-selected="true"]`; a table row: `[data-state="selected"]`, which the product sets |
| `highlighted` | An item has the keyboard or pointer highlight | `[data-highlighted]` |
| `lifted` | A row of a `reorder-list` or a card of a `board` moves with the pointer or with the keys | `[data-lifted]`, which the product sets |
| `current` | The current page, step or tab | `[aria-current]`; a tab: `[data-state="active"]` or `[data-active]`; a sidebar item or a page link: `[data-active="true"]`, which the `data-active:` variant matches; the mark of a step of a `stepper`: `[data-state="current"]`, which the product sets |
| `placeholder` | A select shows its placeholder | `[data-placeholder]`; an empty segment of a time field: `[aria-valuetext="Empty"]` |
| `on`, `off` | A toggle is on or off | `[data-state="on"]` and `[aria-pressed="true"]`; an item of a single toggle group: `[data-state="on"]` and `[aria-checked="true"]` |
| `today` | The current date in a calendar or a month view | `[data-today]` |
| `outside-month` | A day of the month before or after, in the grid of a calendar or a month view | `[data-outside-month]` |
| `range-start`, `range-middle`, `range-end` | The first day, a day inside and the last day of a chosen range | `[data-range-start]`, `[data-range-middle]`, `[data-range-end]` |
| `idle`, `uploading`, `processing`, `error`, `done` | The upload state of an attachment | `[data-state="uploading"]` and the others, which nova sets from the `state` prop |
| `done`, `failed` | A step of a `stepper` is done, or the process stopped at it | `[data-state="done"]` or `[data-state="failed"]` on the item and on its mark, which the product sets. A next step has `[data-state="next"]` and the look of rest. |

**Opacity modifiers.** A key holds a role, never an opacity. Where a component draws a role with an opacity modifier, its definition names the modifier. In dark mode most form controls take an `input/30` ground (`dark:bg-input/30`) and draw the invalid edge in `destructive/50`. A ground with a modifier, such as the 10% tint of a destructive button, gets no key, because the contrast check reads a key at full strength. `## Colors` lists the pairs on such grounds that `npm run contrast` checks.

**New components.** The components keep the nova look and take the tokens of this file, and a product never restyles one. A component enters this file with its nine parts and all its states in one change. The preview shows each state that a prop can force before a screen uses the component.

### button

**Purpose.** A button starts an action, and its label is a verb that names the result. A view, a pane and a dialog each have at most one primary button, which is the `default` variant. With an `href` prop the button renders a link.

**Anatomy.** One `button` slot, a `<button>` or an `<a>`, with an optional icon before or after the label. An icon marks its side with `data-icon="inline-start"` or `data-icon="inline-end"`, and the padding on that side shrinks by 2 px. Icons are 16 px, 12 px in `xs` and 14 px in `sm`.

**Variants.** `default` is the primary action: a `primary` fill that becomes `primary/80` on hover. `outline` is the secondary action: a `background` ground with a `border` edge, and in dark an `input/30` ground with an `input` edge. `ghost` has no ground until hover (`muted`, `muted/50` in dark), and serves toolbars, rows and icon buttons. `destructive` is `destructive` text on a 10% tint (20% in dark), and its label names what goes away. `secondary` is a `secondary` fill with no edge, and `link` is `primary` text that underlines on hover.

**Sizes.** `xs` is 24 px, `sm` 28 px, `default` 32 px and `lg` 36 px high, with 8 px side padding in `xs` and 10 px in the others. `xs` and `sm` take the 6 px radius, and the others take 8 px. The text is `body` at 500, `small` in `xs`, and a fixed 12.8 px in `sm`. The icon sizes `icon-xs`, `icon-sm`, `icon` and `icon-lg` are squares of the same four heights.

**States.** Hover changes the ground as the variant says, and pressed (`:active`) moves the button down 1 px. Focus-visible (`:focus-visible`) turns the edge `ring` and adds the 3 px halo of `## Shapes`. Disabled has two forms. A button with no reason to give, such as a save button before a field changes, takes `disabled`: `:disabled` draws it at 50% opacity, the pointer does not reach it, and it leaves the tab order. With `href`, `disabled` renders a link with `aria-disabled="true"`, no `href` and `tabindex="-1"`. Nova does not draw that link at 50%, so `stack.css` does, and the page gives it no class (measured). Disabled with a reason: a button that a rule blocks, such as Accept on a stale proposal or a control in a row, stays in the tab order. So a keyboard user reaches it and learns why it does not act. It takes `aria-disabled="true"` and not `disabled`. Nova draws only `:disabled`, so it also takes `aria-disabled:cursor-not-allowed aria-disabled:opacity-50` (measured: full opacity without the two classes). Never give it `pointer-events-none`, because then its tooltip does not open on hover (measured). The browser still fires its click on a pointer click, on Enter and on Space, and a submit button still submits its form (measured). So the click calls `event.preventDefault()` while the rule blocks the action. On a submit button, this also stops the form, also on Enter in a field (measured). The part `ReasonButton` (`@stack-kit/stack/parts/ReasonButton`) does all of this: the page gives it `reason`, a sentence while the rule blocks and `undefined` while the action may run, and its `onclick` does not run while a reason shows. It also takes the props of a button, `href` and `disabled`, and `describedby` for a reason that the page shows as text. The page gives it no class for this. The Accessibility part gives the reason. Invalid (`[aria-invalid="true"]`) draws a `destructive` edge and a `destructive/20` halo. Expanded (`[aria-expanded="true"]`) keeps the hover ground while the menu of the button is open. Loading: the product sets `[data-loading]` and `aria-busy="true"`, disables the button and shows a `spinner` in place of the start icon, so that the label and the width stay.

**Tokens.** `button`, `button-outline`, `button-outline-hover`, `button-secondary`, `button-xs`, `button-sm`, `button-lg`, `button-invalid`, `button-phone`.

**Accessibility.** The label names the action in the words of the person, never "OK" alone. An icon-only button has an `aria-label` and a `tooltip` that shows the label and the key. Bits UI sets `aria-haspopup` and `aria-expanded` on a button that opens a menu. A button that is disabled with a reason gives the reason in two ways. The button is the trigger of a `tooltip` that holds the reason, and the tooltip opens on hover and on keyboard focus. Its `aria-describedby` points to a line with the same text, which does not show from 768 px on a pointer device (`hidden max-md:block pointer-coarse:block`, see Phone). There the line stays out of the accessibility tree and still gives the description. While the tooltip shows, Bits UI points `aria-describedby` to the tooltip, which holds the same text. Chromium then gives the button the name "Accept", the state disabled and the description "Refresh the stale proposal first.", at rest and on focus (measured in Chromium 148). Where the reason shows as text next to the button, such as the reason line of an `alert-dialog`, `aria-describedby` points to that text, and the button needs no tooltip.

**Example.**

```svelte
<Button>Save invoice</Button>
<Button variant="outline" size="sm">Filter</Button>
<Button variant="ghost" size="icon-sm" aria-label="Archive"><ArchiveIcon /></Button>
<Button disabled={!changed}>Save</Button>

<!-- Disabled with a reason: in the tab order, with the reason in a tooltip and in aria-describedby. -->
<ReasonButton reason={stale ? 'Refresh the stale proposal first.' : undefined} onclick={accept}>Accept</ReasonButton>
```

**Phone.** Below 768 px every button is at least 44 by 44 px (`button-phone`), and its text takes the 15 px `body` step. The 12.8 px text of `sm` is a fixed size and does not grow. A tap shows no tooltip (measured at 390 px). So below 768 px, or on a touch screen (`pointer-coarse`), the reason line of a button that is disabled with a reason shows under the button or its row, in `small` and `muted-foreground`. It stays the description (measured).

### button-group

**Purpose.** A button group joins related controls into one, such as a split button with its menu, or an input with its search button. Use it for two to four actions of equal weight.

**Anatomy.** The `button-group` slot holds `button`, `input`, `select-trigger` and `button-group-text` items, with optional `button-group-separator` lines between them. The items share their edges: the inner corners are square, and each item after the first has no left edge. A `button-group-text` item is a `muted` box with a `border` edge for fixed text, such as a URL scheme.

**Variants.** `orientation="horizontal"` is the default. `orientation="vertical"` stacks the items and joins their top and bottom edges. A group inside a group keeps an 8 px gap.

**Sizes.** The group takes the height of its items, so all items take one size: 32 px, or 28 px with `size="sm"` on each button. The outer corners keep 8 px, also for `xs` and `sm` buttons.

**States.** Each item keeps its own states and selectors. A focused item rises above its neighbours (`*:focus-visible:z-10`), so that the next item does not cover its halo. `[data-orientation="vertical"]` marks a vertical group.

**Tokens.** `button-group-text`. The separator is a 1 px line in the input role, and it has no key, because a divider needs no ratio.

**Accessibility.** The group has `role="group"`. Give it an `aria-label` when its buttons do not name the group, such as "Text alignment". The menu button of a split button has its own name, such as "More save options".

**Example.**

```svelte
<ButtonGroup.Root aria-label="Save">
  <Button>Save</Button>
  <ButtonGroup.Separator />
  <Button size="icon" aria-label="More save options"><ChevronDownIcon /></Button>
</ButtonGroup.Root>
```

**Phone.** Below 768 px each button is 44 px high (`button-phone`), and so is an input (`input-phone`). The group is then 44 px high, and a `button-group-text` item stretches to that height.

### toggle

**Purpose.** A toggle turns one option on or off, such as bold text or a filter that applies at once. For a setting that the product saves, use a `switch`.

**Anatomy.** One `toggle` slot, a `<button>` with an icon, a label or both. Icons are 16 px, and 14 px in `sm`.

**Variants.** `default` has no ground and no edge at rest. `outline` adds a 1 px `input` edge. When on, both take the `muted` ground.

**Sizes.** `sm` is 28 px, `default` 32 px and `lg` 36 px high, each at least as wide as it is high, with 10 px side padding. `sm` takes the 6 px radius and a fixed 12.8 px text. The others take 8 px and `body` at 500.

**States.** On: `[data-state="on"]`, which Bits UI writes, and `[aria-pressed="true"]`. Off is `[data-state="off"]`. Hover shows the `muted` ground. Focus-visible, disabled (`:disabled`, `[data-disabled]`) and invalid (`[aria-invalid="true"]`) look as on a button, and the invalid edge shows only in `outline`.

**Tokens.** `toggle`, `toggle-outline`, `toggle-sm`, `toggle-lg`, `toggle-on`, `toggle-phone`.

**Accessibility.** The label names the option, never the state, such as "Bold", and `aria-pressed` gives the state. An icon-only toggle has an `aria-label` and a `tooltip`.

**Example.**

```svelte
<Toggle aria-label="Bold" class="max-md:min-w-11" bind:pressed={bold}><BoldIcon /></Toggle>
<Toggle variant="outline" size="sm" class="max-md:min-w-11" bind:pressed={archived}>Show archived</Toggle>
```

**Phone.** Below 768 px a toggle is 44 px high (`toggle-phone`), and its text takes the 15 px `body` step. Its minimum width stays 28, 32 or 36 px (`min-w-7` to `min-w-9`), because a nova utility wins over the base layer of `stack.css`, so a `width` in the phone key does not reach it. Give each toggle `max-md:min-w-11`: a toggle of one icon or one short word is then 44 by 44 px, and a wider toggle does not change.

### toggle-group

**Purpose.** A toggle group holds toggles that act as one control. `type="single"` picks one value, such as the view or the density of a list. `type="multiple"` holds independent options, such as text styles.

**Anatomy.** The `toggle-group` slot holds `toggle-group-item` slots, and each item is a toggle. With `spacing={0}`, the default, the items join: the inner corners are square, and in `outline` the inner edges merge. With a larger `spacing` the items stand apart and keep their corners.

**Variants.** `default` and `outline` are as on `toggle`, and the group gives its variant to each item. A segmented control is a single group of size `sm` on a `muted` ground with 2 px padding (`spacing={0.5}`, `class="bg-muted p-0.5"`). Its on item takes the `background` ground and a 1 px `border-strong` inset edge (`data-[state=on]:bg-background data-[state=on]:inset-ring data-[state=on]:inset-ring-border-strong`).

**Sizes.** As on `toggle`: 28, 32 and 36 px, which the group gives to each item through `size`. Joined items have 8 px side padding. A segmented control is 32 px high: 28 px items in 2 px of padding.

**States.** On: `[data-state="on"]`, with `[aria-checked="true"]` on an item of a single group and `[aria-pressed="true"]` on an item of a multiple group. Disabled: `[data-disabled]` on the group and on each item. Hover and focus-visible look as on `toggle`, and a focused item rises above its neighbours.

**Tokens.** `toggle-group-item`, `toggle-group-item-outline`, `toggle-group-item-sm`, `toggle-group-item-on`, `toggle-group-item-phone`.

**Accessibility.** Bits UI gives an item of a single group the radio role, and the arrow keys move between the items. Give the group an `aria-label`, and give each icon-only item an `aria-label`.

**Example.**

```svelte
<script lang="ts">
  const segment = 'data-[state=on]:bg-background data-[state=on]:inset-ring data-[state=on]:inset-ring-border-strong max-md:min-w-11';
</script>

<ToggleGroup.Root type="single" size="sm" spacing={0.5} class="bg-muted p-0.5" aria-label="View" bind:value={view}>
  <ToggleGroup.Item value="list" class={segment}>List</ToggleGroup.Item>
  <ToggleGroup.Item value="board" class={segment}>Board</ToggleGroup.Item>
</ToggleGroup.Root>
```

**Phone.** Below 768 px each item is 44 px high (`toggle-group-item-phone`). Its minimum width stays as on `toggle`, so give each item `max-md:min-w-11` too, as the example does. An item of one short word, such as All in a segmented control, is then 44 by 44 px, and 35.5 by 44 px without the class (measured at 390 px). A segmented control is then 48 px high.

### input

**Purpose.** An input takes one line of text, a number, an email address or a password. Every input sits in a `field`, with a visible label above it.

**Anatomy.** One `input` slot, a native `<input>`. It has no ground in light and an `input/30` ground in dark, a 1 px `input` edge and the 8 px radius. A `type="file"` input draws its button as `body` text at 500. An icon, a prefix or a suffix goes into an `input-group`.

**Variants.** None. The value is `foreground` in the `input` step, and the placeholder is `muted-foreground`. A placeholder shows an example of the format, never the label.

**Sizes.** 32 px high, with 10 px side padding and 4 px top and bottom padding. The input fills the width of its container (`w-full`).

**States.** Focus-visible (`:focus-visible`) turns the edge `ring` and adds the `ring/50` halo, and nova draws no hover. Disabled (`:disabled`) takes an `input/50` ground (`input/80` in dark) at 50% opacity. Invalid (`[aria-invalid="true"]`) draws a `destructive` edge and a `destructive/20` halo, and a `field-error` under the input names the rule and the fix. Read-only (`[readonly]`) looks as at rest. When the value never changes, show plain text, or add a lock icon in an `input-group`.

**Tokens.** `input`, `input-invalid`, `input-phone`.

**Accessibility.** The `label` names the input through `for` and `id`. `aria-describedby` points to the description and to the error. Set `type`, `inputmode` and `autocomplete`, so that a phone shows the right keyboard and fills the value.

**Example.**

```svelte
<Input id="email" type="email" autocomplete="email" placeholder="ana@example.org" bind:value={email} />
```

**Phone.** Below 768 px the input is 44 px high (`input-phone`). Its text renders 16 px (`text-base`), so that a phone does not zoom on focus.

### input-group

**Purpose.** An input group adds an icon, a text, a key or a button to an input or a text area, inside one edge. Use it for a search field, an amount with its currency, a URL with its scheme, or a message box with its send button.

**Anatomy.** The `input-group` slot draws the edge, the ground and the focus ring. It holds one `input-group-control`, which is an input or a text area with no edge, and `input-group-addon` slots with `align` set to `inline-start`, `inline-end`, `block-start` or `block-end`. An addon holds `muted-foreground` text in `body` at 500, an icon, a `kbd`, or a ghost `InputGroup.Button` of size `xs` (24 px, 5 px radius). A click on an addon focuses the input, unless the click is on a button.

**Variants.** A search field puts a search icon in an `inline-start` addon and takes the `muted` ground in light (`class="bg-muted"`), and Esc clears it. An amount puts the currency in an `inline-end` addon, and the control takes `text-right tabular-nums`. The locale sets how the amount parses (see `architecture/web-app.md`). A message box puts a text area in the control and its actions in a `block-end` addon.

**Sizes.** 32 px high with an input. With a text area or a block addon, the group takes the height of its content. An inline addon has 8 px padding on its outer side.

**States.** Focus-visible on the control turns the edge of the group `ring` and adds the `ring/50` halo (`:has([data-slot=input-group-control]:focus-visible)`). Invalid: a control with `[aria-invalid="true"]` turns the edge `destructive`, also in dark. Disabled: a disabled control gives the group an `input/50` ground at 50% opacity.

**Tokens.** `input-group`, `input-group-invalid`, `input-group-addon`, `input-group-control`, `input-group-phone`, `input-group-control-phone`.

**Accessibility.** The control takes the label of its `field`. An icon in an addon is decorative (`aria-hidden="true"`), and an addon button has an `aria-label`, such as "Copy". A text in an addon that the person needs, such as the currency, also goes into the label or the description.

**Example.**

```svelte
<InputGroup.Root>
  <InputGroup.Input id="amount" inputmode="decimal" class="text-right tabular-nums" bind:value={amount} />
  <InputGroup.Addon align="inline-end">EUR</InputGroup.Addon>
</InputGroup.Root>
```

**Phone.** Below 768 px the group and its control are 44 px high (`input-group-phone`, `input-group-control-phone`), and the value renders 16 px. A button in an addon is 44 px high (`button-phone`), so it fills the height of the group.

### input-otp

**Purpose.** The code input takes a short one-time code, such as the code for a new device, with one character in each cell. It takes a pasted code at once and moves the caret by itself.

**Anatomy.** The `input-otp` root holds one hidden native input and `input-otp-group` slots. Each group is a row of joined `input-otp-slot` cells, and an `input-otp-separator`, a minus icon, stands between two groups. A cell shows one character in `body` and a blinking caret when it is active. Only the outer corners of a group take the 8 px radius.

**Variants.** None. Set `maxlength` to the length of the code, and `pattern` to the characters that it allows. Bits UI gives the hidden input `inputmode="numeric"` by default, so a phone shows the number keys only (Bits UI 2.19.5, read in its source). A code with letters sets `inputmode="text"` and `autocapitalize="characters"` on the root.

The code for a new device has 8 characters of Crockford base32 in two groups of four (see `### Sign-in` in `architecture/web-app.md`). It takes `maxlength={8}` and `pattern="^[0-9A-TV-Za-tv-z]+$"`: the 32 symbols in both cases, and I, L and O, which Crockford reads as 1, 1 and 0. The page binds the value through a function that writes each letter as a capital, O as 0, and I and L as 1. So a typed `ok4q` shows as `0K4Q`, the code that the server reads (measured). Bits UI refuses a whole paste when one character fails the pattern, such as the hyphen of `7K4Q-X2MD` (measured). So the root takes a `pasteTransformer` that removes the spaces and the hyphens. For a sign-in code, the part `CodeInput` of `@stack-kit/stack/outside/CodeInput` writes the binding and the `pasteTransformer` itself.

The code of an authenticator app has 6 digits in two groups of three (see `### Authenticator app` in `architecture/web-app.md`). It takes `maxlength={6}` and `pattern={REGEXP_ONLY_DIGITS}` of Bits UI, which is `^\d+$`. It keeps the default `inputmode="numeric"`. It takes the same `pasteTransformer`, so a paste of `123 456` with its space fills the 6 cells (measured).

**Sizes.** Each cell is 32 by 32 px, so a group of four cells is 128 px wide. Two groups and the 16 px separator make a row of 272 px (measured at 1440 px). Two groups of three make a row of 208 px (measured at 1440 px).
