| `chart-ink` | 19.80 | 17.18 | 16.23 | 15.86 |
| `chart-1` | 3.66 | 3.65 | 4.43 | 3.38 |
| `chart-2` | 3.14 | 4.32 | 3.12 | 5.16 |
| `chart-3` | 3.13 | 4.40 | 3.15 | 5.23 |
| `chart-4` | 3.55 | 3.36 | 3.02 | 5.20 |
| `chart-5` | 3.10 | 4.43 | 3.12 | 5.20 |
| `chart-6` | 4.20 | 3.05 | 3.11 | 5.22 |
| `chart-7` | 3.08 | 4.49 | 3.29 | 4.56 |
| `chart-8` | 4.04 | 3.09 | 3.23 | 4.63 |

### Edges

Bars, slices and the top line of each stacked area have a 1 px edge in the ground under the chart: `background`, `card` or `popover`. The rule is the same in every style. The edge is a thin gap between two neighbours, so that two shapes that meet stay apart. A shape of the pattern style has no ink edge, and its marks show its outline. The chart takes its ground as a prop, because an SVG edge cannot read the ground under it. Set the edge once for the chart, through `props.bars`, `props.arc` or `props.line`. The one exception is the cell of a heat map: it has a 1 px edge in the strongest step of its scale, `chart-ink` or `chart-heat` at full strength, which is dark in light mode and light in dark mode (see `### Heat map`).

The top line of an area has no fill. Between two stacked areas it is the gap. The top line of the highest area lies on the ground, so it does not show. An area of one category has no neighbour to keep apart, so its top line is no gap. It is the line of its category, 2 px in `chart-ink` or `chart-1` (see `### Line chart`). In the ground, the top of that area would show only through the marks of its pattern. A reference line has a 1 px edge in the ground at each side, so that it shows on the fill of an area.

LayerChart draws a 1 px black edge on each bar by default, so every chart sets its edge. LayerChart gives the props of a series, its fill too, to the top line of its area. So clear that fill with the class `[&_.lc-area-line]:fill-none` on the container.

### Labels on the chart

Put the name and the value of each category on the chart, next to its shape, where they fit. A person reads a label on the chart without a match of patterns or colors.

- **Bar.** The value of each bar stands 6 px above the bar, in `small` at 500 in `foreground`, with tabular figures. A stacked bar shows the total of the categories that show. The plot leaves 20 px above its top for the value of the tallest bar.
- **Values that do not fit.** When the value of any bar is wider than its bar, such as on a chart of the 24 hours of a day, no bar shows its value. A value such as 41.7M is 37.5 px wide in `small` at 500 (measured). The chart then shows only the value of the tallest bar, above it, and the first of them when two bars are equally tall. The tooltip and the data table give every value.
- **Donut.** A 1 px leader line in `chart-ink` goes from the middle of the outer edge of each slice 12 px straight out, and then 16 px to the side, away from the center. The label stands 4 px past the end of the line: the name of the category in `small` at 500 in `foreground`, and its value 15 px under it, in `small` in `muted-foreground` with tabular figures. A label on the right starts at the line, and a label on the left ends at it. A label grows away from the ring: in the upper half the line ends at the value and the name sits above it, and in the lower half the line ends at the name.
- **Labels that meet.** Two labels of one side are at least 34 px apart, and each label stays inside the container. A label that moves for this stands on the circle 28 px outside the ring, at its new height. So no label covers the ring, and two leader lines never cross (measured on a donut of eight slices). The container leaves 28 px above and under the ring and 96 px at each side for the labels.
- **Total.** The center of a donut holds the total in the `figure` step with tabular figures, and a word under it, such as total, in `small` and `muted-foreground`. The ring is 40% of the outer radius wide, so the total fits in the hole.
- **Line.** Each line of a chart with two metrics names itself at its right end, such as Mobile, and so does the comparison line, such as Previous 28 days. A reference line names itself and its value there, such as Limit 8. The label stands 4 px past the end of the plot, at the height of the end of its line: the name in `small` at 500 in `foreground`, and a value in `small` in `muted-foreground` with tabular figures. Two labels stand at least 16 px apart, in the order of their heights, and inside the height of the plot. The plot leaves room at its right for the widest label, measured in the font of the labels after the web font loads. A line shows no values on it, so the tooltip and the data table give each value.
- **Legend.** A bar chart with one bar for each category and a donut name each category on the chart, so they have no legend. A line chart names its lines at their right ends, so it has no legend either. A chart with one category, such as a line or an area of one metric, has no legend and no name on its shape: its caption names the metric, such as Page views per day. The categories of a stacked bar or a stacked area share one bar or one area, and their names do not fit there. So a stacked chart has the legend of `### Legend`. A heat map has the legend of its steps (see `### Heat map`).

The labels on the chart are `aria-hidden`, because the data table gives a screen reader the same values with their names. A value has the same text on the chart, on the axis, in the tooltip and in the table, such as 18k.

### Axes and grid

The value axis has three ticks: 0, the middle and the maximum. The maximum is the largest value, rounded up to a whole number of units. The unit is a fifth of the power of ten of the largest value, or 2 below 10, so the middle is a whole number: 138 gives 140, 101 gives 120, and 7 gives 8. A stacked chart takes its largest stack, and a chart with reference lines takes the largest of its values and of its reference values. Set the domain to 0 and the maximum with `yNice={false}`, and give the same ticks to the axis (`props.yAxis.ticks`) and to the grid (`props.grid.yTicks`).

The grid has a line for each tick in `border` at 50%, and no line on the category axis. The base line at 0 is a 1 px line in `border`, a little darker than the grid. Draw it in the `belowMarks` snippet, so that a bar covers it, and set `rule={false}`, because the container sets the stroke of the LayerChart rule to 0. The axis labels are `small` in `muted-foreground`, and the axes have no tick marks.

**A long category axis.** A bar chart over time, such as one bar for each hour of a day, can have more x values than its axis can name. The hours are x values and not categories, so the eight categories of `### Categories` do not limit them. The axis then names every nth x value, where n is the smallest of 1, 2, 3, 4, 6 and 12 for which n times the distance from the center of one bar to the center of the next is at least the widest name plus 8 px. The first x value always has its name. A time axis puts the other names on round times: an axis of hours names the hours that are a multiple of n, such as 0:00, 6:00, 12:00 and 18:00 at n = 6, and an axis of days that cannot name every day names the first day of each week. A name that comes closer than 8 px to the name of the first x value is left out. The tooltip and the data table name every x value. Give the axis the list of the x values that it names (`props.xAxis.ticks`), because on a band axis LayerChart 2.7.0 names every x value, and two names can meet. A bar chart with one bar for each category keeps the name of every category on its axis, because there the name on the axis is the name of the category (see `### Labels on the chart`).

**A time axis of a line chart.** A line chart and an area of one category take the same rule on a time scale. There the distance is the distance from one x value to the next. An axis of days names every day when the names fit, and else the first day and each Monday, or every second or fourth Monday when the weeks do not fit either. The first x value stands on the value axis, so its name starts at the origin, and it never meets the 0 of the value axis. A centered name keeps 8 px from the end of that first name. The plot leaves at least half the widest name at its right, for the name of the last x value. Give the axis the list of the x values that it names (`props.xAxis.ticks`), and draw the first name with `textAnchor="start"` through the `tickLabel` snippet of the axis.

### Legend

A stacked chart and a heat map have a legend, and no other chart has one, but a stacked chart of one category has no legend (see `### Labels on the chart`). This part gives the legend of a stacked chart, and `### Heat map` gives the legend of the steps. The legend sits in the normal flow under the chart, and never in the padding of the chart (see `## Layout`). Render the `Legend` of LayerChart after the chart container. Give it the context that the chart binds (`bind:context`) through `setChartContext`, and draw each item through its `children` snippet. The default item of LayerChart is a color swatch in a button with no `aria-pressed`, so a chart never shows it. `@stack-kit/stack/parts/PatternLegend` is this legend: an own chart of a product renders it after its container, with the context, the prefix of its patterns and its categories. A pointer over an item highlights its category on the chart.

Each item is a `button` with `aria-pressed`. It holds the 12 px tile of its category (`spacing.legend-tile`) and the label of its category in `small`. The tile is a `<rect>` with the fill of the shapes of its category: the same `url()` in the pattern style and in the color pattern style, and `chart-n` in the color style. It has no edge, as its shapes have no ink edge. The marks of each of the eight patterns show the tile on the ground (measured at 1440 px in light and in dark).

**Toggle.** While every category shows, the first click on an item shows only its category. Each further click adds or removes one category, and when the last shown category goes, every category shows again (measured). An item whose category is hidden keeps its label in `muted-foreground` and its tile at 30%.

```svelte
<script lang="ts">
  import { untrack } from 'svelte';
  import { Legend, setChartContext } from 'layerchart';
  let { context, prefix, categories, style = 'pattern' } = $props();
  setChartContext(untrack(() => context));
  const shown = (key: string) => {
    const keys = context.series.selectedKeys.current;
    return keys.length === 0 || keys.includes(key);
  };
</script>

<Legend class="mt-2 block">
  {#snippet children({ seriesItems, values })}
    {@const keys = seriesItems ? seriesItems.map((s) => s.key) : values.map(String)}
    <div class="flex flex-wrap items-center justify-center gap-x-3 gap-y-1 text-xs">
      {#each keys as key (key)}
        {@const n = categories.findIndex((c) => c.key === key) + 1}
        <button
          type="button"
          aria-pressed={shown(key)}
          class={['flex items-center gap-1.5 rounded-md border border-transparent px-1.5 py-0.5 outline-none focus-visible:border-ring focus-visible:ring-3 focus-visible:ring-ring/50 max-md:min-h-11', shown(key) ? 'text-foreground' : 'text-muted-foreground [&_svg]:opacity-30']}
          onclick={() => context.series.selectedKeys.toggle(key)}
        >
          <PatternTile {prefix} {n} {style} />{categories[n - 1].label}
        </button>
      {/each}
    </div>
  {/snippet}
</Legend>
```

`PatternTile` (`@stack-kit/stack/parts/PatternTile`) is the tile of the legend and of the tooltip, here with the pattern fill of the pattern style and the color pattern style, and with the solid fill of the color style:

```svelte
<svg width="12" height="12" aria-hidden="true" class="shrink-0">
  <rect width="12" height="12" fill="url(#{prefix}-pattern-{n})" />
</svg>
<svg width="12" height="12" aria-hidden="true" class="shrink-0">
  <rect width="12" height="12" fill="var(--chart-{n})" />
</svg>
```

### Tooltip

The tooltip names each category in text, beside its value. `Chart.Tooltip` takes the name from the chart config, so give each category a `label` there. A category with no label shows its raw key. The marker of a row is the 12 px tile of its category, or no marker. The default marker of `Chart.Tooltip` is a square of the series color, which shows color only and turns clear for a pattern fill. So draw each row through the `formatter` snippet. The value is Inter at 500 with tabular figures, as `## Typography` says, and not the monospace of the default row. On a time axis, `labelFormatter` gives the tooltip the heading of the table row, such as Apr, and never a raw date. A bar chart with one bar for each category has one series, so its row takes the category from the bar under the pointer, `context.tooltip.data`. The marker of a row of a line chart is a 12 px tile with a piece of its line across the middle. The piece is a solid line, two dots of line 2, or a dash, a gap and a dash of the comparison line. `@stack-kit/stack/parts/LineTile` draws that tile from the look of the line.

`Chart.Tooltip` makes a direct child svg of a row 10 px, so the tile sits in a `<span>`, which keeps it at 12 px (measured):

```svelte
{#snippet tooltipRow({ item })}
  {@const n = categories.findIndex((c) => c.key === item.key) + 1}
  <span class="flex shrink-0"><PatternTile {prefix} {n} {style} /></span>
  <div class="flex flex-1 items-center justify-between gap-2 leading-none">
    <span class="text-muted-foreground">{categories[n - 1].label}</span>
    <span class="font-medium text-foreground tabular-nums">{format(item.value)}</span>
  </div>
{/snippet}
```

### Accessibility layer

A chart is a `<figure>` with a `<figcaption>` that names it. The svg of the chart takes the same name as its `title`, through `props.svg`. Its `aria-describedby` points to two elements: a summary of one or two sentences that states what the chart shows, and the data table.

The table holds every value as text. It is a native `<table>` with a caption, a column header for each category and a row header for each x value. So a screen reader reads each value with its category and its x value. Hide the table with `sr-only` on a wrapper, because `sr-only` does not shrink a `<table>` itself. Measured: 1280 by 156 px with the class on the table, 1 by 1 px with the class on the wrapper. A button under the chart shows the table: "Show the data as a table", with `aria-expanded` and `aria-controls`.

**Open gap.** The tooltip layer of a bar chart adds a second `svg` with `role="figure"` and no name. A screen reader can stop on it. The area chart and the donut have no such figure (measured). This release has no fix for it.

### Rendering

Draw every chart with LayerChart on its SVG layer, and import the charts from `layerchart/svg`. Never use the canvas layer: it keeps the colors of its first draw when the mode changes. Render a chart on the client only, and never pass the `ssr` prop. With LayerChart 2.7.0 and Svelte 5.57.2, the server puts the chart at the origin of the page. With no size, the server draws it at 100 by 100 px. Give the container a fixed height, such as `h-64`, so that the page does not move when the chart draws. So no chart reaches an email: a digest shows its figures and a short table (see `## Email`).

A chart in a grid or in a flex column takes `min-w-0` on its `<figure>`. A grid with no column template can take `grid-cols-1` below its first breakpoint instead. A grid item keeps the width of its content by default, and at 390 px the charts made the page 471 px wide with no `min-w-0` and 390 px with it. In the showcase, a chart stayed 427 px wide in a column of 358 px without it (measured).

### Heat map

A heat map shows a count over two dimensions, such as the sign-ins of a week by weekday and time of day, or over an image, such as the clicks on a screenshot of a page. Each count is a cell. A heat map has no categories, so it has no patterns: each cell takes one of five steps, and a higher step means a larger count. Draw each cell as an SVG `<rect>`, and never on a canvas (see `### Rendering`). `@stack-kit/stack/parts/HeatMap` draws the grid of this section. A height class in its `class`, such as `h-56`, sets the height of the chart container, and the other classes place the part.

**The five steps.** STACK owns the steps, in every theme and in both modes. A step is one color at an opacity: 50, 62, 75, 88 and 100%, from the smallest count to the largest. The color is `chart-ink` in the pattern style and `chart-heat` in the two color styles. In light mode a higher step is darker, and in dark mode it is lighter, as the ink of the pattern style is. Draw a step as the color with its opacity on the `<rect>`, such as `fill="var(--chart-ink, var(--foreground))"` and `fill-opacity="0.62"`.

The lightest step passes 3:1 on `background`, `card` and `popover`, in both modes of every theme, and `npm run contrast` checks it. The ratios from `npm run contrast` on 2026-10-24, on the lowest of the three grounds:

| Color | default light | default dark | graphite light | graphite dark |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart-ink` at 50%, the lightest step of the pattern style | 3.69 | 5.06 | 3.29 | 5.03 |
| `chart-heat` at 50%, the lightest step of the two color styles | 3.30 | 3.32 | 3.29 | 3.32 |
| `chart-heat` at full strength, the edge in the two color styles | 16.55 | 9.62 | 14.56 | 9.01 |

**Why `chart-heat`.** No chart color reaches 3:1 at 50%: the highest is 2.29:1, by the color math of `npm run contrast`. So the two color styles take one more role, `chart-heat`, in the hue of `chart-1` of the theme: 255 in `default` and 275.206 in `graphite`. A heat map then takes the main chart hue of its theme. A scale of one hue reads by its lightness, so a person with no color vision still reads its order. The lightness of `chart-heat` lets its 50% step pass 3:1, so it is dark in light mode and light in dark mode. It has the chroma of `chart-1`, lowered only to stay inside sRGB. In `graphite` that is 0.159 in light, as `chart-1`, and 0.09 in dark, because sRGB holds at most 0.091 at L 0.82 in that hue. A theme that lacks it gives it `foreground` (see `theme-fallbacks`).

**The edge.** Each cell has a 1 px edge in the strongest step of its scale: `chart-ink` at full strength in the pattern style, and `chart-heat` at full strength in the two color styles. So the edge is dark in light mode and light in dark mode. A dark edge in dark mode would not show on the dark ground, and a cell over an image would lose its outline. The edge passes 3:1 on every chart ground, so it outlines each cell also where the fill is faint, such as the lowest step over an image. This edge is the one exception to the rule that a chart shape has no ink edge (see `### Edges`). The cells of a grid meet, and two cells that meet share one edge: put each `<rect>` on a half pixel, at x + 0.5 and y + 0.5, with the width and the height of a cell.

**A grid.** Each cell of a grid is a square. The names of the rows stand on the left and the names of the columns under the grid, in `small` and `muted-foreground`. A grid has no value axis and no grid lines. Two names never meet. When the column names do not fit under their cells, the axis names every nth column by the rule of "A long category axis" (see `### Axes and grid`). There the distance from one center to the next is the side of a cell. The first column always has its name, and the tooltip and the data table name every column. Measure the names in the font of the axis labels after the web font loads, because a fallback font has other widths. At 390 px the names 0:00 to 20:00 under cells of 42 px take every second column, because 20:00 is 36.3 px wide in the 13 px step. At 1280 px they take every column. A grid of 24 hours names every sixth hour at 390 px and every third hour at 1280 px (measured on the preview on 2026-10-24). In LayerChart, a `Chart` with text values on `x` and on `y` puts both on band scales. Give `xRange` and `yRange` a function of the size that gives each cell the same side in whole pixels, so that the cells are square and each edge falls on a whole pixel. Give the bottom axis the list of the column names that it shows (`ticks`). `tooltipContext={{ mode: 'band' }}` gives each cell its own tooltip (measured on the preview).

**Over an image.** The chart draws the image at 30% opacity on the ground under the chart, such as with `opacity-30` on the image, with one value for every theme and both modes. The image then reads as context, and the cells stand in front of it. Each cell over the image is the box of the element that it counts, so it need not be a square. It shows its step at 60% of its opacity, rounded to a whole percent: 30, 37, 45, 53 and 60%. So the element under a cell stays in view, and a person sees which element got the count. At full opacity the highest step would hide that element. The edge stays at full strength. Over an image the steps fall below 3:1: on the bare ground the lowest step at 30% is 1.91:1 to 2.67:1 in `chart-ink` and 1.91:1 to 2.01:1 in `chart-heat`, by the color math of `npm run contrast`, and the image under a cell lowers the ratio more. So the edge outlines each cell, the legend names each step in words, and the tooltip and the data table give each value.

**The legend.** A heat map has a legend under the chart, in the normal flow, where `### Legend` puts a legend. It lists the five steps in order, from the smallest count to the largest. Each item holds the 12 px tile of its step (`spacing.legend-tile`) and the range of the step in words, in `small`, such as 0 to 9, 10 to 49, 50 to 99, 100 to 499 and 500 and more. The product sets the five ranges, and writes each number as the table writes it. The tile shows the step as a cell shows it: the same color at the same opacity, with the same 1 px edge. So over an image the tile takes 60% of its step, on the ground under the chart. The legend is a list (`<ul>`) with the name "The steps of the heat map" (`aria-label`), and its items are not buttons, because a person does not hide a step.

**The tooltip and the table.** The tooltip and the data table give the value of each cell. The tooltip of a grid cell has the names of its row and its column as its heading, and one row with the tile of the step, the name of the value and the value. The data table of a grid has a row header for each row, a column header for each column and the value in each cell. Over an image the table has a row for each cell. A heat map has the accessibility layer of every chart (see `### Accessibility layer`).

### Line chart

A line chart shows one or two metrics over time, such as the page views of each day. Use it for a rate or a level, which a person reads as a height at each time. Draw it as a `LineChart` of `layerchart/svg`, with one series for each line and the date of each row on the x axis. The value axis, the grid and the base line are those of `### Axes and grid`. `@stack-kit/stack/parts/TimeChart` draws the line chart and the area of this section, with the comparison, the reference lines and the annotations. A height class in its `class`, such as `h-56`, sets the height of the chart container, and the other classes place the part.

**The lines.** A line has no area to fill, so its stroke takes the place of a pattern. Line 1 is solid and 2 px wide. Line 2 is dotted: dots of 3 px whose centers are 6 px apart, which `stroke-dasharray="0 6"` draws with round ends. The color is `chart-ink` in the pattern style and `chart-n` in the two color styles. Line 2 is dotted in every style, because two lines can cross, and a name at the right end does not tell them apart where they cross. Each line has round ends and round joins. A series carries its stroke in its `props`: `strokeWidth`, and `stroke-dasharray` and `stroke-linecap` as attributes, because LayerChart maps only `strokeWidth` to its attribute.

**Two metrics.** Two lines share one unit and one value axis, such as the visits on desktop and on mobile. A metric with another unit takes its own chart. A second value axis puts two scales on one plot, and a person then compares two heights that have no relation. More than two metrics take more charts, or a stacked area when they are the parts of one whole.

**One metric.** The caption names the metric, such as Page views per day. So the line has no name on the chart, and the chart has no legend.

**A line or an area.** A line is the default. Take an area for one metric whose amount from 0 counts, such as the storage in use or the memory of a machine against its limit. Two metrics and a comparison take lines, because an area hides the line under it. An area is an `AreaChart` with one series, and its fill is pattern 1, or `chart-1` in the color style. Its top line is the line of category 1: 2 px in `chart-ink`, or in `chart-1` in the two color styles. That line is no gap in the ground, because one area has no neighbour to keep apart (see `### Edges`). LayerChart gives the props of a series, its fill too, to the top line of its area. So draw the area with no line, and draw its top line as a `Spline` of the same values with no props of the series.

**The comparison with the previous period.** A chart of one metric may show the previous period of the same length, such as the 28 days before. Its values stand at the same x values as the current period, so the first day of each period stands at the start of the axis. The comparison line is dashed and 2 px wide, with dashes of 6 px and gaps of 4 px, in `muted-foreground` in every style. No category takes a dash or `muted-foreground`, so the comparison never looks like a category. It lies under the line of the metric, and its name stands at its right end, such as Previous 28 days.

**The change as text.** The change text stands above the plot, in `small`. It gives the value of the current period at 500, then up or down, the change in percent, and the value of the previous period. An example is "39.1k, up 17% from 33.5k in the previous 28 days". The value of a period is the value of the whole period as the product counts it, such as the sum of the page views, or the p75 of all the samples of the period. The change takes no color and no arrow alone, because a rise is not good for every metric. When the previous value is 0, the change text gives the change as an amount, such as up 120 from 0.

**Reference lines.** A reference line marks a named value, such as a target, a threshold or a limit. A chart holds up to four of them, as a meter holds up to four ticks (see `### progress`). Each is a 1 px line in `muted-foreground` across the plot in every style, with `shape-rendering="crispEdges"`. It has a 1 px edge in the ground at each side, so that it shows on the fill of an area. It lies over a fill and under every line, and a line keeps its look where it passes a reference line. Its name and its value stand at its right end, such as Limit 8 (see `### Labels on the chart`). The value axis reaches the largest reference value, so every reference line lies inside the plot.

**Annotations.** An annotation names an event at one time, such as a launch or an outage. A chart holds up to nine, so that each number has one digit. The numbers follow the order of time, from 1. Each annotation is a numbered disc on the time axis: 16 px wide, in `foreground`, with its number in `background` in `meta` at 500 with tabular figures. The discs stand in a row 4 px under the base line, each at the x value of its event. The names of the time axis stand under that row, so the plot leaves 40 px under the base line. Two discs that meet move apart along the axis, in the order of their numbers, until 2 px are between them. A 1 px guide in `border` runs from the top of the plot to the disc, at the x value of the event, and it lies under every line. The guide carries no information, so it needs no ratio. An annotation stands on the x value of a row of the data, so that the table holds it.

**The list of the annotations.** An ordered list under the chart gives the text of each annotation in `small`: the same disc with its number, the date in `muted-foreground`, and the text. The discs on the chart are `aria-hidden`, because the list and the data table give the same text.

**The tooltip and the table.** The tooltip has the date as its heading, and one row for each line and for the comparison. The marker of a row is the tile of its line (see `### Tooltip`). The data table has a row header for each x value and a column for each line and for the comparison. A column for the annotations holds the number and the text of each annotation on the row of its date. A second table lists the reference lines, with the name and the value of each. The button under the chart shows both tables. `aria-describedby` of the svg points to the summary, the change text and both tables.

**Contrast.** Each line passes 3:1 by its pair on the ground under the chart: `chart-ink` or `chart-n` (see `### Chart colors`), and `muted-foreground` or `foreground` for the parts that are no category. A 2 px line at 1x crosses some columns of device pixels with no pixel at full strength. So the preview measured the strongest pixel of each column along each line in Chromium on 2026-10-25. It measured each line on `background`, in both modes of both themes at 1x, 1.25x, 1.5x and 2x, in each style. It also measured line 1 and line 2 on `card` and on `popover` in dark mode. No column fell below 3:1, and each run of 3 px held a pixel at the ratio of its pair. At 1x the weakest column held 91% of its pair, on the top line of an area. The lowest was 3.23:1, line 1 of the two color styles in `chart-1` on `popover` in graphite dark, on a pair of 3.38. On `background` the lowest was 3.49:1, on a pair of 3.66, and on `card` it was 3.49:1, on a pair of 3.65. From 1.25x each column held a pixel at its pair. Each dot of line 2 and each dash of the comparison held a pixel at its pair. The lowest dot was 3.12:1, in `chart-2` on `background` in graphite light. A reference line covered whole pixels at its pair, also on the solid fill of the color style, and its edges there were the ground.

### Sparkline

A sparkline shows the shape of one metric over time in a small space: in a row of a list or a table, or beside a key figure. It is the line of category 1, 2 px in `chart-ink` in the pattern style and in `chart-1` in the two color styles. It has no axis, no grid, no base line, no labels on the chart, no tooltip and no data table. Its value axis runs from its lowest value to its highest value, and not from 0, because it shows the shape of the change, and a person reads no value from it. Each sparkline takes its own scale, so the heights of two sparklines do not compare, and a small change fills the same height as a large one. The change in words beside it gives the size. When every value is the same, the line draws across the middle. It is 80 by 24 px (`w-20 h-6`), and its points stay 2 px inside the top and the bottom, so that the line is not cut. Draw it as a `LineChart` of `layerchart/svg` in a `Chart.Container` with the class of its size, with `axis={false}`, `grid={false}`, `rule={false}`, `highlight={false}` and `tooltipContext={false}`. `@stack-kit/stack/parts/Sparkline` draws it so: its `class` sets the size of its box and can place it, such as with a margin, and the box keeps 80 by 24 px when the class gives no size.

**Its value as text.** The value stands beside the sparkline as text, such as the total of the period in the next column of a row, or the key figure that the sparkline follows. A change goes in words, as the change of a comparison does (see `### Line chart`).

**Its accessible name.** The box of the sparkline is a `<span>` with `role="img"` and an `aria-label` that states the metric, the period, and the first and the last value. An example is "Page views of /pricing per day, 1 to 28 September: from 231 to 313". The `img` role makes the svg in it presentational, and the svg takes `aria-hidden`, so a screen reader reads only the name (measured in the accessibility tree of Chromium). A sparkline gives the value of no single day. A product that needs those values shows a line chart of the metric.

### Flow

A flow shows how often a person goes from one page or state to the next, such as from the start page to the pricing page, or how often a ticket goes from Open to In progress. A flow is a `### table` with three columns: from, to and count. Each row is one pair of a from and a to. The rows are sorted by the count, largest first, and the head of the count column sets `aria-sort="descending"`. The count aligns right with tabular figures, as each column of numbers does. The caption names the flow and its period. The flow table is a part of STACK and not of the registry. A product imports it from `@stack-kit/stack/parts/FlowTable`, and gives the caption, the rows and the format of a count.

**Why no Sankey diagram.** A Sankey diagram draws each pair as a band, and the width of the band is the count. STACK draws no Sankey diagram, for four reasons:

- A product has many more pages than eight, and the patterns of `### Categories` tell only eight categories apart.
- The bands cross and cover each other, and a person compares the widths of two bands poorly. A count in a column reads exactly, and a table sorts and filters.
- The names of the pages do not fit beside the bands at 390 px.
- A Sankey diagram needs the same table for a screen reader, so the table alone carries the whole flow.

### Parts of a whole

The parts of one whole in a row, a card or a header are words. Examples are the child tickets of a ticket by status, and the time that a ticket spent in each stage. Each part shows its count or its time, in the order of the process, with the done part first. An example is "1 of 5 done · 1 Ready · 2 In progress · 1 In testing". One share of the whole that grows to done, such as the merged tasks of a ticket, may also show as a `progress` bar. The bar stands beside its count in words, such as "1 of 3 tasks merged". A view that compares the parts as a picture takes a chart: a donut or stacked bars.

**Why no segment bar.** A segment bar draws each part as a piece of one bar, and the length of the piece is the count. STACK draws no segment bar, for four reasons:

- The words give every part and its count, so the bar adds no fact.
- A piece tells its part by a status color or by a pattern. A status color shows only on its icon, its badge, a meter fill and a timeline dot (see `## Do's and Don'ts`), so a bar of status colors adds a place.
- A bar in a row is 4 to 8 px high and about 48 px wide, so a piece of one item of six is 8 px wide. Such a piece holds no label and one to three marks of a pattern, so a person cannot match it to a legend.
- A screen reader needs the words anyway, so the words alone carry the whole.

## Email

This section gives the look of the emails that a product sends from its back office: a request for an action or a confirmation, a notification of an event, a digest or a weekly report, and an alert. A marketing email is out of scope, as the public pages of a website are. A product in TypeScript or JavaScript can import `@stack-kit/stack/email`, whose functions `text` and `html` write the two parts of an email of this section. `html` takes the colors of `### Email colors` as its second argument.

An email is content that no build sees, and no stylesheet reaches it, also not `stack-plain.css`. Gmail and Outlook do not read a CSS custom property that the email declares (caniemail, `css-variables`). So an email writes each color, each size and each font as a plain value in the `style` attribute of its element. `### Email colors` gives the color values of each theme, and `### Email template` gives a ready email. A product may write its emails in any language, such as in a Worker in Rust, so this section gives values and markup, and no component.

A fact about a mail client in this section comes from the caniemail data of 2026-09-16, read on 2026-10-25, and names the feature of the data in parentheses. The preview shows the first four content kinds of `### Content kinds` at 600 px and at 375 px on the page `/email`.

### Plain text first

Every email has a plain text part, and the product writes it first. The HTML part follows from it, and it says nothing that the plain text part lacks: each fact, each figure, each row of a table and each link of the HTML part is also in the plain text part, in the same order. A client that shows the plain text part, and a person who turns HTML off, get the whole email.

- The message is `multipart/alternative`, with the plain text part first and the HTML part last. A client shows the last part that it can show (RFC 2046, section 5.1.4).
- One line for each paragraph. The product never wraps a paragraph by hand: the client wraps it to its own width, and a paragraph that the product wrapped at 72 characters breaks again on a phone. The transfer encoding `quoted-printable` keeps each encoded line at 76 characters or less with soft line breaks, and the client joins the lines again (RFC 2045, section 6.7). A line of a message holds at most 998 characters (RFC 5322, section 2.1.1), and the encoding keeps that limit too.
- A blank line separates two paragraphs and two blocks.
- A link takes two lines: a line that names the target and ends in a colon, such as "Open INV-0042:", then the full address on a line of its own.
- A fact is one line, "Label: value". A row of a table is one line, with a middle dot ( · ) between its cells, as "Words" in `architecture/web-app.md` separates facts. The first line of a table names the columns, with the same middle dot. A figure is one line with its change in words, such as "Documents sent: 128, 12 more than last week".
- No markup: no Markdown, no asterisks, no line of dashes and no table drawn in characters.

### Email layout

An email has one column, at most 600 px wide, at the start side of the window of the client. The column is never centered, as `## Do's and Don'ts` asks for a page. So the email reads as a message from the product, and each line starts where the subject and the sender start above it.

- **The frame.** The `<body>` and an outer table of 100% width take the `background` ground. The cell of the outer table has 24 px padding at the top, 16 px at each side (`space-4`, the edge of a page) and 32 px at the bottom. An inner table of 100% width with `max-width: 568px` holds the blocks, so the column is 600 px wide at most, and as wide as the window below 600 px. Classic Outlook for Windows, the Word engine, ignores `max-width` (caniemail, `css-max-width`). So a conditional table of 568 px wraps the inner table, and only Outlook reads it: there the column has its width. The column is a table in every client.
- **No media query.** The email has the same values at every width, and it holds no `<style>` element. The Gmail apps drop a `<style>` element for an account of another provider, and Outlook for Windows reads no media query (caniemail, `html-style` and `css-at-media`). At 375 px the column is 343 px wide.
- **The head.** The name of the product in text, in `body` at 600 with a 20 px line, then a middle dot and the name of the workspace in `muted-foreground` at 400. A 1 px `border` rule lies 16 px under it. The head holds no image that the email depends on, because a client can block images. A product may put its logo before the name as an image at most 24 px high. The logo takes `alt=""`, because the name follows as text, and an opaque ground of its own, because a transparent logo can vanish on a ground that a client darkens.
- **The heading.** One `<h1>` 24 px under the rule, in `heading`. It says the event or the task in one sentence.
- **The blocks.** The blocks of the content kind follow the heading: a text 8 px under the heading, two texts 16 px apart, and every other block 24 px under the block before it.
- **The facts of an item.** A card: the `card` ground, a 1 px `border` edge, the 8 px radius and no shadow, with 12 px padding at the top and the bottom and 16 px at each side. Each row is 32 px high, as `spacing.row`: 4 px padding above and under a 24 px line. The label takes a 112 px column (`spacing.property-label`) in `small` with a 24 px line, in `muted-foreground`, and the value follows 16 px after the column, in `body`. An identifier takes weight 500. This is the properties panel of a pane (see `### Detail pane`), so an email about an item reads like the item. A value that does not fit wraps in its cell: at 375 px a date and a time take two lines.
- **The primary action.** One button, 24 px under the last block, at the start side, as wide as its label. Its label is a verb that names the result, as `### button` asks. A table cell holds the `primary` ground, a 1 px `border` edge and the 8 px radius. A link in the cell holds the label in `body` at 600 with a 20 px line, in `primary-foreground` and with no underline, and 11 px padding at the top and the bottom and 19 px at each side. So the button is 44 px high (`spacing.touch`), and the whole button is the target. Outlook for Windows reads padding on a table cell only, and no radius (caniemail, `css-padding` and `css-border-radius`). So the cell also carries `mso-padding-alt` with the same padding: there the corners are square, and only the label is the target (not measured). In light the edge is close to the ground, so it barely shows. It outlines the button when a client darkens the light colors and keeps the dark ground of the button (see `### Dark mode in a mail client`).
- **The address of the action.** 12 px under the button, the full address of the same link on one line, in `code` and `muted-foreground` with its underline, and with `word-break: break-all`, so that a long address wraps at any character. A person whose client does not show the button copies the address. The address is code by the rule of `## Typography`, because a person copies it into a browser and never reads it aloud.
- **Secondary links.** Each secondary link stands on a line of its own, 24 px under the address and 8 px apart, in `body` and `link` with its underline. Its text names the target, such as "Open the invoices paid in October". A secondary link is never a second button.
- **The foot.** 32 px under the last line, a 1 px `border` rule, then 16 px, then up to three lines in `small` and `muted-foreground`, 8 px apart. The first line says why the person gets the email, and it names the rule that sends it, such as "You get this email because you follow INV-0042 in Northfield Ltd.", as a reason line names its rule ("Words" in `architecture/web-app.md`). The second line is a link to the setting that turns the email off. Only an email that a person turned on and can turn off has it, such as a notification or a digest. A request for an action, a confirmation and an alert that goes to every owner have none, because the person cannot stop them. The last line names the sender: the company that sends the email and its postal address. A link in the foot takes `muted-foreground` and its underline.
- **One-click unsubscribe.** An email that has the line that turns it off also carries two headers: `List-Unsubscribe` with one HTTPS address, and `List-Unsubscribe-Post: List-Unsubscribe=One-Click`. Its DKIM signature covers both headers (RFC 8058, section 3.1). A POST to the address turns the email off with no other step. SvelteKit refuses a form POST from another origin before `hooks.handle`, and a mail provider posts from its own origin. So the product answers the one-click address in the Worker before SvelteKit, or on a route that the CSRF check does not cover. The address holds a token that nobody can guess (RFC 8058, section 3.1). The answer needs no cookie, and a repeat of the POST answers 200 (see "Signed links" in `architecture/web-app.md`).

### Email type

The face is `font-sans` of the interface, with the system faces that look most like Inter after it:

```
'Inter Variable', Inter, 'SF Pro Display', -apple-system, BlinkMacSystemFont, 'Segoe UI', Roboto, 'Helvetica Neue', Arial, sans-serif
```

An email loads no font. Of the main clients in the data, Apple Mail, Samsung Email and Thunderbird read `@font-face`, and Gmail and Outlook do not load a remote font (caniemail, `css-at-font-face`). A remote font is also a request on each open, which tells its host when the person reads the email, as a font service learns the address of each user (see `## Typography`). So Inter shows where the device of the person has Inter installed, and every other device shows its system face. The names after Inter are the system faces of Apple devices, of Windows and of Android, then two common faces and the generic family. The preview shows both faces.

The code face is the stack of `font-mono` with Consolas, the monospace face of Windows: `ui-monospace, Menlo, Consolas, monospace`.

| Step | Size / line height | Weight | Use for |
|---|---|---|---|
| `heading` | 20 / 28 px, -0.01em | 600 | The `<h1>` |
| `section` | 16 / 24 px | 600 | An `<h2>`, such as the heading of a table |
| `body` | 15 / 24 px | 400; 500 for an identifier | Running text, the value of a fact, a cell of a table and a secondary link. The head and the label of the button take this size at 600 with a 20 px line. |
| `small` | 13 / 20 px | 400; 500 for the header of a column | A caption, the label of a figure and its change, the line under a table and the foot. The label of a fact takes this size with a 24 px line, so that it sits on the line of its value. |
| `figure` | 28 / 36 px | 600 | A figure of a digest |
| `code` | 13 / 20 px, the code face | 400 | The address of the action, and the lines of a log |

**Rules.**

- An email is read on a phone as often as on a desktop, and it has no phone steps. So `body` is 15 px, the size of `body-phone`, with the 24 px line of running text. The smallest text is 13 px.
- Each element that holds text carries the family, the size, the line height, the weight and the color in its own `style` attribute. No element takes them from its parent.
- Weights 400, 500 and 600 only, no italic, and sentence case, as `## Typography` asks.
- Tabular figures (`font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums`) on each amount, number, date, time and count. An identifier takes them only in a column of identifiers, and there only on its number part, as `### Type steps` asks. A client that drops the property shows proportional figures, and a column of amounts still aligns at its end.

### Email colors

An email takes the light values of the theme that the product imports, as hex values. A product with a theme switch takes the values of its default theme. An email has no dark values (see `### Dark mode in a mail client`). Each value is the 8-bit sRGB value that a browser paints for the `oklch()` value of the theme file, by the color math of `npm run contrast`.

`graphite` has its own column. A product that imports `graphite` shows its indigo primary on every screen, so its email shows the same primary.

`npm run contrast` computes each value and each ratio of the two tables again from the theme files, and fails when one differs from the table or falls below its minimum, and when a theme file has no column. A new theme adds its column in the same change. A product with a theme of its own runs `npm run contrast -- <file>` in a clone of this repository, and the check prints the email values of that file.

| Role | Use in an email | `default` | `graphite` |
|---|---|---|---|
| `background` | The ground of the email | `#ffffff` | `#f8f8f9` |
| `card` | The ground of the facts of an item | `#ffffff` | `#fefeff` |
| `border` | The edge of a card and of the button, the rule under the head and over the foot, and the line between two bands or two rows | `#e5e5e5` | `#dedede` |
| `muted` | The ground of a log | `#f5f5f5` | `#e9e9ea` |
| `foreground` | Text | `#0a0a0a` | `#2f2f31` |
| `muted-foreground` | A label, a caption, the address of the action and the foot | `#6a6a6a` | `#5b5c5e` |
| `link` | A secondary link | `#325ac2` | `#3f60d9` |
| `primary` | The ground of the button | `#171717` | `#474da6` |
| `primary-foreground` | The label of the button | `#fafafa` | `#ffffff` |
| `success` | The dot of a state that runs or passes | `#0a7e3a` | `#007828` |
| `warning` | The dot of a state that waits | `#866402` | `#816501` |
| `destructive` | The dot of a state that fails or stops | `#bc0000` | `#b40022` |

Each pair below passes its minimum: 4.5:1 for text, and 3:1 for the button against the ground and for a dot.

| Pair | Use | Minimum | `default` | `graphite` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `foreground` on `background` | Text | 4.5 | 19.80 | 12.59 |
| `foreground` on `card` | The value of a fact | 4.5 | 19.80 | 13.26 |
| `foreground` on `muted` | A log | 4.5 | 18.16 | 11.01 |
| `muted-foreground` on `background` | A caption, the address of the action and the foot | 4.5 | 5.41 | 6.31 |
| `muted-foreground` on `card` | The label of a fact | 4.5 | 5.41 | 6.64 |
| `link` on `background` | A secondary link | 4.5 | 6.22 | 5.09 |
| `primary-foreground` on `primary` | The label of the button | 4.5 | 17.18 | 7.29 |
| `primary` on `background` | The button against the ground | 3 | 17.93 | 6.87 |
| `success` on `background` | The dot of a state | 3 | 5.17 | 5.32 |
| `warning` on `background` | The dot of a state | 3 | 5.47 | 5.21 |
| `destructive` on `background` | The dot of a state | 3 | 6.69 | 6.68 |

**The state of a job, a device, a connection or a check.** An email shows such a state as a dot (●) in its role and the word of the state, with the roles of `## Colors`: `success`, `warning` and `destructive`. A state outside the three shows its word only. The status of a product item, such as Paid, shows as a word only, because its color is in the layer of the product. A product that wants a dot for it takes the hex value of its own `product-` color.

### Dark mode in a mail client

An email declares no color scheme and holds no dark values. It forces no colors: no `color-scheme` meta element or property, no `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` block, and no trick that keeps a client from changing a color, such as a background image. A dark set would reach few clients: Gmail and Outlook for Windows read no `prefers-color-scheme`, and the Gmail apps read only the value `light only` of the `color-scheme` meta element (caniemail, `css-at-media-prefers-color-scheme` and `html-meta-color-scheme`). A person who reads mail in dark mode chose dark, and `light only` would undo that choice.

A client in dark mode does one of three things with the email, and the email stays legible in each:

- It shows the email as sent: a light email in a dark client.
- It darkens the light colors and keeps the dark colors, a partial inversion. The white ground turns near black and the text turns light. The button keeps its dark ground, and its `border` edge turns gray and outlines it. Without the edge, the button was a dark box on a ground of nearly the same lightness (measured in Chromium with its forced dark of web content, which darkens in this way; a mail client can differ).
- It inverts every color, a full inversion. The ground turns black, the text white, and the button light with dark text. The preview models it with `filter: invert(1) hue-rotate(180deg)`, which keeps the hue of a dot and of a link near its hue; a mail client can differ.

Three things hold in each case. Every text sits on a ground that the email sets itself. No image carries a mark or a word, so nothing vanishes on a dark ground. The state of a job shows as a word next to its dot, so a change of hue loses no state. The client chooses the dark colors and their ratios, so the contrast check reads the light values only.

### Content kinds

Four rules hold for every kind:

- **A link changes nothing.** A link in an email opens a page, and the page asks for the action. A signed link opens a page with a button, and only that button spends the token (see "Signed links" in `architecture/web-app.md`). A mail filter or a link preview can open a link before the person does, and a link that acts on a GET request then acts with no person.
- **The subject** says what happened or what the person can do, in sentence case, in at most about 60 characters, with the object in the first 40, because an inbox list on a phone shows the start of a subject only. It names no product in front, because the name of the sender names the product. No emoji, no capitals for emphasis and no exclamation mark.
- **The preheader** is one sentence of at most about 90 characters that adds the next fact, and it never repeats the subject. It is the first element of `<body>`: a `<div>` with `display:none; max-height:0; overflow:hidden; mso-hide:all;`. Thirty pairs of `&#847;&#160;` follow the sentence, so that an inbox list shows no text of the body after it (not measured). The preheader holds no code, no link and no other secret, because a phone can show the subject and the preheader on its locked screen.
- **The order** is the same in every kind: the head, the heading, the blocks of the kind, the button, the address of the action, the notes, the secondary links and the foot.

**A request for an action or a confirmation.** The link is a signed link of "Signed links" in `architecture/web-app.md`. The subject names the action and the object, such as "Approve quote Q-0093 for Miller Ltd" or "Confirm your email address for Docline". The preheader says by when the person can act, such as "Open the page, check the quote and approve it by 31 October 2026." The blocks are one text that says what to do, then the facts of the object in a card. The button label is the verb of the link page, such as "Approve quote". Two notes in `body` follow the address of the action: until when the link works, and that opening the link changes nothing, with what to do when the person did not expect the email. The foot names the address that got the email, and it has no line that turns the email off. An email carries no sign-in link and no code of any kind (see `### Sign-in` in `architecture/web-app.md`).

**A notification of an event.** The subject names the actor, the event and the object, such as "Miller Ltd paid INV-0042", as a line of activity names them ("Words" in `architecture/web-app.md`). The preheader gives the next facts, such as the amount and the time. The blocks are one text that says the result, then the facts of the item in a card. The button opens the item, and a secondary link may open a list. The foot names the rule that sent the email, and it has the line that turns such emails off. One event is one email. A product that sends many events of one kind in a short time sends a digest instead.

**A digest or a weekly report.** The subject names the report, the workspace and the period, such as "Weekly report of Northfield Ltd, 19 – 25 October 2026". The preheader gives the key figures in words. The blocks are one text that says the change in one sentence, the figures, then at most one short table.

- The figures stand in one band of figures, as a summary page shows them (see `### figure-band`). The blocks of an email are at most 568 px wide, and below a box of 672 px a band holds two figures in a row. Each figure takes half the width, with 12 px padding at the top and the bottom and 32 px between the two. A 1 px `border` line lies over the first row and under each row. A figure has its label in `small`, the figure in `figure`, and its change in words in `small`, such as "12 more than last week" or "The same as last week". A change is never only an arrow or a color. A digest has at most four figures.
- The table has a heading in `section`, at most three columns and at most ten rows. Its column headers are in `small` at 500 in `muted-foreground`, each row has a 1 px `border` line under it, each cell has 8 px padding at the top and the bottom and 12 px between two cells, and an amount aligns at the end. A line in `small` under the table says how many rows it leaves out.
- A digest has no chart. A chart renders on the client only (see `### Rendering`), and a picture of a chart is an image that the email depends on.

The button opens the full report. The foot has the line that turns the report off, and the message has the two headers of one-click unsubscribe.

**An alert.** The subject starts with the word of the state, then the object, such as "Failed: the nightly import of bank statements", because a person sorts the alerts in the inbox list by the state. The preheader says when it happened and what it means. The blocks are the state line, one text that says what the person loses, the facts of the job in a card, then the last lines of the log.

- The state line holds the dot in its role with `aria-hidden="true"`, the word of the state at 500, then a middle dot and the time in `muted-foreground`.
- The log is in `code` on the `muted` ground, with the 8 px radius and 12 px 16 px padding, one line of the log on each line. A caption in `small` and `muted-foreground` stands 8 px over it and names the time zone of its times. Each line starts with the exact time of a log ("Formats" in `architecture/web-app.md`).
- The button opens the job, and it never runs the job again: a retry is an action, and a link changes nothing.

The foot names the rule that sent the alert. An alert that goes to every owner has no line that turns it off.

**A security notice.** The app sends it when a new device signs in and when a recovery ends. It also sends it when the person adds or removes a passkey or an authenticator app (see `### Security mail` in `architecture/web-app.md`). It also goes out when a workspace removes the authenticator app of the person. It also goes out when the app shows a new recovery code outside a recovery. A sign-in with an authenticator app in a browser that the server does not know sends the notice of a new device. Its facts name the authenticator app. The subject names the event, such as "A new device signed in to your account", "Your account has a new authenticator app", "Your account has no authenticator app now" or "A passkey was removed from your account". The preheader gives the time and the device. The blocks are one text that says what happened, then the facts of the event in a card. The facts are the event, the device or the passkey, and the time. The text also says that the person needs to do nothing when the person caused the event. The text names whom the person contacts, such as the administrators, and how to reach them outside the app. The button is the signed link "This was not me", and its page asks before it acts. One note in `body` follows the address of the action: until when the link works. The foot names the address that got the email. It has no line that turns the email off, because the person cannot stop a security notice. The notice of a report from "This was not me" has the same parts and no button. Its text says what the app suspended or disabled and which sessions it ended, or that it suspended nothing. When a workspace removes the authenticator app, the notice has the same parts and no button, because an administrator made the change. Its text names the workspace. The notice holds no code and no link that signs in, so a product may show it inside the app as it came.

### Language, dates and times

- An email takes the locale of the person who gets it: the locale that the person chose in the product, else the locale of the workspace, else the default locale of the product. It never takes the locale of the person who caused the event. The subject, the preheader, both parts and every format follow that locale.
- `<html>` and the outer table carry `lang` and `dir`, such as `lang="sr-Latn-RS" dir="ltr"`, with the tag of "Message keys" in `architecture/web-app.md`. A quotation in another language, such as a message from a client, takes its own `lang` on its element.
- In a right-to-left language, the email takes `dir="rtl"`, and each left and right of the template changes sides: the side padding of a cell, `align`, `text-align` and the side of a column of amounts.
- A time never shows by its age, such as "2 hours ago", and no word such as today or yesterday stands for a date. The person reads the email later, maybe on another day.
- A date with a time takes the day, the month, the year, the time and the zone: `new Intl.DateTimeFormat(locale, { day: 'numeric', month: 'long', year: 'numeric', hour: '2-digit', minute: '2-digit', timeZone, timeZoneName: 'short' })`. In the zone Europe/Belgrade it writes "24 October 2026 at 14:20 CEST" in `en-GB`, "October 24, 2026 at 02:20 PM GMT+2" in `en-US` and "24. Oktober 2026 um 14:20 MESZ" in `de-DE` (measured with Node 20.20, ICU 78.2 and CLDR 48). The option `dateStyle` cannot carry the zone: `Intl` throws a TypeError for `timeZoneName` next to `dateStyle` (measured).
- The zone is the zone of the person who gets the email: the zone that the person set, else the zone of the workspace. Each time names its zone.
- A period takes `formatRange` with the day, the month and the year. In `en-GB` it writes "19 – 25 October 2026", with a thin space on each side of the dash (measured).
- The lines of a log take the exact time of a log, `YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss` ("Formats" in `architecture/web-app.md`), and a caption names the zone once.
- Numbers, amounts and percents go through `Intl.NumberFormat` in the locale of the person.
- A product with no `Intl`, such as a Worker in Rust, takes the same CLDR data from a library, such as ICU4X, and compares a sample of its output with the output of `Intl`.

### Email accessibility

- `<html>` carries `lang` and `dir`, and `<title>` holds the subject.
- Every table that lays out the email has `role="presentation"`, so a screen reader reads its cells as text. The table of a digest is a data table: it has no `role`, and each column has a `<th scope="col">`.
- One `<h1>`, the heading. A digest gives its table an `<h2>`. No level is skipped, and the head is not a heading.
- Link text names the target, such as "Open INV-0042" or "Turn off the weekly report". Never "click here", "here" or "more". The address under the button is the address itself.
- No image carries information. When a product adds an image that does, its `alt` says the same in words. A logo next to the name takes `alt=""`.
- The dot of a state has `aria-hidden="true"`, and the word next to it carries the state.
- Contrast: the pairs of `### Email colors`, at 4.5:1 for text and at 3:1 for the button against the ground and for a dot.
- The smallest text is 13 px, and the button is 44 px high.
- The preheader has `display:none`, so a screen reader skips it.
- The plain text part holds the whole email (see `### Plain text first`).

### Email template

The template is the notification of `### Content kinds`, in the `default` colors. Another kind changes the blocks between the heading and the button, the notes and the foot. The preview builds the four kinds from the same code, and the page `/email` shows an error when this template or one of the blocks below differs from that code.

The product escapes `&`, `<`, `>`, `"` and `'` in every value that it puts into the HTML. An `href` takes only an absolute `https:` address that the product builds on its own origin, so a value never supplies the scheme or the host. A header value, such as the subject, holds no CR and no LF, and the product encodes it with RFC 2047. The preview code throws on an address that does not start with `https://`. The template also carries `dir`, and the product escapes it as it escapes `lang`.


The plain text part of the same email:

```text
Docline · Northfield Ltd

Miller Ltd paid INV-0042

The payment covers the full amount, so Docline closed the invoice.

Invoice: INV-0042
Client: Miller Ltd
Amount: €1,240.00
Paid: 24 October 2026 at 14:20 CEST
Status: Paid

Open INV-0042:
https://docline.example/northfield/invoices/INV-0042

Open the invoices paid in October:
https://docline.example/northfield/invoices?status=paid&month=2026-10

You get this email because you follow INV-0042 in Northfield Ltd.
Turn off the emails about paid invoices:
https://docline.example/northfield/settings/email
Docline Ltd · 12 Mill Lane · Riverton 4100
```

**The blocks of the other kinds.** Each block goes into a row of the column, as the facts of the template do: `<tr><td style="padding-top:24px;">`, the block, then `</td></tr>`. The top padding is the gap of `### Email layout`.
