## Print

This section gives the look of a document that a product renders for print or as a PDF, such as an invoice, a report or a list. It also gives the rules for a screen page that a person prints. A document is a page of paper, so it has its own part, as an email has. Paper is white in every theme and in both modes, and a printer may print in black only. So a document takes plain values and no token of `stack.css`: the colors of `### Print colors` as hex values, and the sizes of `### Print type` in pt. The CSS of `### Print template` holds every value, and a product copies it into the style sheet of its documents.

A fact about a renderer in this section comes from Chromium 148, through `page.pdf()` of Playwright 1.60, and from the Poppler tools `pdfinfo`, `pdffonts`, `pdftotext` and `pdftoppm`, measured on 2026-10-25. Another renderer can differ. The preview renders a sample report on A4 and on Letter on the page `/print`.

### Paper

- **Sizes.** A document prints on A4, 210 by 297 mm, or on Letter, 8.5 by 11 in (215.9 by 279.4 mm). A product takes the size that the person picked in the product, else the size of the workspace, else A4. The CSS sets the size in `@page`, such as `size: A4` or `size: letter`, and the renderer takes it, such as with `preferCSSPageSize: true` in `page.pdf()`.
- **Margins.** 20 mm at the top and at the bottom, and 18 mm at each side, on both sizes. The head and the foot of each page stand in the top margin and in the bottom margin (see `### Head and foot of a page`). A printer cannot print at the edge of the paper, and 18 mm keeps the text away from that edge (not measured on a printer).
- **One layout for both sizes.** The text box is 174 by 257 mm on A4, and 179.9 by 239.4 mm on Letter. The text has no fixed width, so it fills the width of each size, and only the page breaks move. A part with a fixed width, such as a chart, is at most 174 mm wide, the narrower of the two, which is 657 CSS px.
- **Units.** The type takes pt and the paper takes mm. A CSS px is 0.75 pt, or 0.26 mm, so a 1 px line is 0.26 mm wide on paper.

### Print colors

A document takes the four values below in every theme and in both modes. Each value is a gray: its red, green and blue parts are equal. So a printer in black only prints each value as its own gray, and a color printer adds no hue (not measured on a printer). A document never prints the accent of a theme, a status color, a tag color or a chart color. A state keeps its word, such as Paid or Overdue, as `## Colors` asks, so the state stays on paper.

The values are hex values. Chromium writes `oklch(0.525 0 0)` into the PDF as the sRGB value 0.4164 0.4165 0.4165, and `#6a6a6a` as 0.4157 0.4157 0.4157 (measured). So Chromium prints an `oklch()` value correctly, within 1/255. A hex value is the color form that every renderer reads, and its three parts stay equal in the PDF.

| Role | Use in a document | Value |
|---|---|---|
| `paper` | The ground of the page. The document draws no other ground. | `#ffffff` |
| `ink` | Text, a figure, the marks and the lines of a chart, the rule under the head of a table, and the rule over the totals | `#000000` |
| `ink-muted` | A label, a caption, the head of a column, the head and the foot of a page, the name of a value on a chart, and a note | `#595959` |
| `rule` | The rule between two rows of a table, the rules of the key figures, and a grid line of a chart | `#b7b7b7` |

| Pair | Use | Minimum | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ink` on `paper` | Text, a figure, and the marks and the lines of a chart | 7 | 21.00 |
| `ink-muted` on `paper` | A label, a caption, and the head and the foot of a page | 7 | 7.00 |

`npm run contrast` reads both tables. It fails when a value is not a gray, or when `paper` is not `#ffffff`. It fails when a ratio differs from the table or falls below its minimum. It also fails when the CSS of `### Print template` holds a color that the first table does not give. The minimum is 7:1, the enhanced level of WCAG 2.2 (1.4.6), and not 4.5:1. The smallest text of a document is 8 pt in `ink-muted`, and a printer draws a gray as a pattern of dots (not measured on a printer). `rule` is a divider, so it carries no information and needs no ratio (see `## Colors`). It is darker than `border`, so that a thin line shows on paper.

Each step of the sample report reaches the ratio of its pair in the PDF, at 300 dpi and also at 96 dpi. The strongest pixel of `ink` text is gray 0, 21.00:1, and the strongest pixel of `ink-muted` text is gray 89, 7.00:1. This holds at 8 pt, in a margin box and on a chart too (measured with `pdftoppm`).

**No ground.** A document carries no information in a ground: no band behind a row, a head or a total, and no fill behind a state. The print dialog of a browser drops each ground by default, and `page.pdf()` drops it too unless the call sets `printBackground`. A ground of `#eeeeee` printed nothing, and a ground with `print-color-adjust: exact` printed (measured). The rules and the weights carry the structure of a document, and the paper is its only ground.

### Print type

The face is Inter, with the stack of `font-sans`: `'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif`. The renderer embeds the font in the PDF.

- The document loads Inter from the assets of the product, as `## Typography` says. The renderer waits for `document.fonts.ready` before it prints, because a face that has not loaded prints as the next face of the stack. A face that a page declared under `@media print` only had not loaded when `page.pdf()` printed, and the next face printed (measured). `pdffonts` lists the fonts of a PDF, and each font of the list is Inter.
- Chromium embeds Inter Variable as Type 3 fonts, one set for each weight that the document uses. Each font has a Unicode map, so the text of the PDF stays searchable and copyable (measured with `pdffonts` and `pdftotext`). It embeds only the glyphs that the document uses. The letters ł, ø, ő, ş and ř of the sample, and their capitals, come out of the PDF as text (measured). Poppler warned 21 times of a bad bounding box in a Type 3 glyph when it drew a probe page at 600 dpi (measured).
- **The static faces for a PDF of the server.** A document that a renderer on the server prints loads the static faces of the npm package `@fontsource/inter`, the weights 400, 500 and 600. It does not load `@fontsource-variable/inter`, and both packages take the same version, such as 5.3.0. Chromium embeds each static face as a CID TrueType font with the encoding Identity-H, as a subset with a Unicode map (measured with `pdffonts`). The CSS of the document then names the family `'Inter'` in place of `'Inter Variable'`, in `@page` and in `.document`. With both forms, each word of a probe page stood in the same place within 0.023 pt along the line, with the `wght` faces of `@fontsource-variable/inter`, in which `"opsz" 32` changes nothing. The static faces have no optical size axis, and `@fontsource/inter` has no display face, so in such a PDF the `title` and `figure` steps take the text optical size (not measured against `opsz.css`). Its PDF was 19 KB with the static faces and 94 KB with the variable face (measured).
- **A screen page keeps the variable face.** A screen page that a person prints loads the faces of its screen, so its PDF holds Type 3 fonts, and its text stays searchable. The static faces under `@media print` do not change that: `page.pdf()` printed the variable face, because the static faces had not loaded (measured). The print dialog was not measured. A renderer that first sets the print media with `emulateMedia()` and waits for `document.fonts.ready` printed the static faces of `@media print` (measured).
- **PDF/A and a print shop.** PDF/A allows a Type 3 font, because the rule on the embedded font programs passes each Type 3 font. That is rule 6.3.4 of PDF/A-1, and rule 6.2.11.4.1 of PDF/A-2 and PDF/A-3, in the published rules of a PDF/A validator (read on 2026-10-25). A PDF of Chromium fails at least two other rules of PDF/A-2. Its metadata holds no PDF/A identification (rule 6.6.4 of PDF/A-2), and it paints in DeviceRGB with no output intent (rule 6.2.4.3 of PDF/A-2). STACK found both with a search of the probe PDFs, and it ran no validator. So a product that keeps a document as PDF/A converts the PDF with a tool that writes PDF/A, and it checks the result with a validator. STACK did not measure a print shop, and a print shop checks a file against a profile of its own. The probe PDF with the static faces held no Type 3 font (measured with `pdffonts`). STACK did not check the file against a profile, and a glyph that Inter does not hold prints in the next face of the stack (not measured).
- Inter is under the SIL Open Font License 1.1. Its preamble says that the licence does not apply to a document that is made with the font. So a PDF that embeds Inter names no licence.

| Step | Size / line height | Weight | Use for |
|---|---|---|---|
| `title` | 18 / 24 pt, -0.01em | 600 | The title of the document, the one `<h1>` |
| `figure` | 16 / 20 pt | 600 | A key figure |
| `section` | 11 / 15 pt | 600 | The heading of a part of the document, an `<h2>` |
| `body` | 10 / 14 pt | 400; 500 for an identifier; 600 for the name of the issuer and for the total | Running text, the value of a fact, and a cell of a table |
| `small` | 8 / 11 pt | 400; 500 for the head of a column and for a value on a chart | A label, a caption, the head and the foot of a page, a note, and the text of a chart |

**Rules.**

- The smallest text is 8 pt, the size of `small`.
- Weights 400, 500 and 600 only, no italic, and sentence case, as `## Typography` asks. The title is in sentence case too, and never in capitals.
- Tabular figures on each amount, number, date, time and count. An identifier takes them only in a column of identifiers, and there only on its number part, as `### Type steps` asks.
- An amount, a count and a percent align at the end of their column.
- The `title` and `figure` steps take the display optical size, `"opsz" 32`, and the other steps take the text optical size, as `## Typography` asks. The exception is a PDF with the static faces, which have no optical size axis, so there the `title` and `figure` steps take the text optical size.
- A date, a time, a number and an amount follow `### Language, dates and times` of `## Email`. A document takes the locale of the person who gets it, and it names the zone of each time. It never shows a time by its age, because a person reads it later, as an email.

### Head and foot of a page

- **The head of the document.** The first page starts with the issuer, the title and the facts, in the flow of the page. The issuer is the company that issues the document: its name in `body` at 600, then its address and its tax number in `small` and `ink-muted`. The title stands 16 pt under the issuer, in `title`, as the one `<h1>`. The facts stand 12 pt under the title. Each label is in `small` and `ink-muted`, in a column as wide as the widest label. Each value is in `body`, 12 pt after the column. An invoice gives its number, its dates, the client and the terms there. A report gives its period and the time of issue.
- **The head of each further page.** From page 2, the top margin holds the title of the document at the start, and its number at the end, in `small` and `ink-muted`. So a loose page names its document. The first page has no such head, because the head of the document stands there.
- **The foot of each page.** The bottom margin holds the name and the address of the issuer at the start, and the page number at the end, such as Page 2 of 3. Both are in `small` and `ink-muted`, with tabular figures. Every page has the foot.
- **Margin boxes.** The head and the foot are the margin boxes of `@page`: `@top-left`, `@top-right`, `@bottom-left` and `@bottom-right`, with `counter(page)` and `counter(pages)` for the number. A margin box takes the font, the size and the color of the `@page` rule (measured). A margin box names a side of the paper, so a document in a right-to-left language puts its start in the boxes on the right. Chromium draws margin boxes from version 131 (not measured in other browsers). A renderer that draws no margin box prints no head and no foot of a page. The document stays whole without them, because the head of the document holds each fact.
- **The words of the locale.** The text of a margin box is a CSS string. So the product writes it into the CSS of each document, in the locale of the document, such as `"Page " counter(page) " of " counter(pages)` in English and `"Strana " counter(page) " od " counter(pages)` in Serbian. The product escapes each value that it puts into the string: `\` as `\\`, `"` as `\"`, a line break as `\A `, and `<` as `\3C `. So a value never ends the string or the `<style>` element.
- **The head and the foot of the browser.** The print dialog of a browser can print its own head and foot, with the date, the title and the address of the page. The person turns them off in the dialog (not measured). `page.pdf()` prints none unless the call sets `displayHeaderFooter`.

### Tables across pages

A table of a document can run over many pages, such as the lines of a long invoice or a list.

- The head of the columns is a `<thead>`. Chromium repeats it at the top of each page that the table reaches (measured).
- Never put the totals into a `<tfoot>`. Chromium repeats a `<tfoot>` at the foot of each page, so a total would stand on every page (measured). The totals go into a last `<tbody>` of the same table, with `break-inside: avoid`. Without that rule, Chromium split three total rows over two pages, and with it, the three rows moved to the next page together (measured). Do not add `break-before: avoid` to the same `<tbody>`: with both rules, Chromium split the totals again (measured).
- Each row takes `break-inside: avoid` (measured in Chromium only), so that a row of two lines never splits. Chromium kept each row whole also without the rule (measured), and the rule keeps a row whole in another renderer.
- The sample report prints on 3 pages of A4 and on 4 pages of Letter, with the column head on each page. On Letter its four total rows moved to page 4 together, under the column head. Without `break-inside: avoid`, one total row stayed on page 3 (measured).
- A heading takes `break-after: avoid`, so that it never stands alone at the foot of a page. Without the rule, Chromium left a heading at the foot of a page and put its table on the next page (measured).
- The facts, the key figures and a chart take `break-inside: avoid`.
- The head of a column is in `small` at 500 in `ink-muted`, with a 1 px `ink` rule under it. Each row has a 1 px `rule` line under it, 4 pt of padding at the top and at the bottom, and 8 pt between two cells. A column of identifiers takes weight 500, and a column of amounts aligns at the end. The last row has a 1 px `ink` rule under it, which closes the rows and stands over the totals.

### Figures and totals

- **Key figures.** A report may start with up to four key figures in one row, under a heading such as Summary. A band of key figures prints in the same row (see `### figure-band`). The row stands between two 1 px `rule` lines, as the figures of a digest do (see `### Content kinds`). Each figure has its label in `small` and `ink-muted`, then the figure in `figure` with tabular figures. Its change follows in words, in `small` and `ink-muted`, such as 12 more than September. A change is never an arrow or a color alone.
- **Totals.** The totals stand in the last rows of the table, in the column of the amounts. Each total row has its label in a `<th scope="row">` that spans the other columns and aligns at the end, and its amount in the column of the amounts. A subtotal, a tax and the parts of a total take `body` at 400. The total that the person pays, or the total of a report, takes `body` at 600 and stands last. The label says what the row sums, such as Total of 64 invoices or VAT 20% of €1,033.33.
- **Amounts.** An amount takes the currency of the document through `Intl.NumberFormat` with `style: 'currency'`, as `architecture/web-app.md` says. Every amount of one column has the same number of decimals.

### Charts in print

A chart in a document, a printed chart and a chart in a PDF always take the pattern style, whatever the style on the screen (see `### Styles`). A printer may print in black only, and the patterns tell the categories apart in gray.

- The marks and the lines are in `ink` on `paper`, with the widths of `chart-patterns`.
- **Marks as shapes.** Chromium writes a `<pattern>` fill into a PDF as an image of 72 dpi, one pixel for each pt (measured). In such an image, the strongest pixel of the 1 px lines of patterns 1 and 5 is 3.54:1, and no pixel of a line is at full ink. So a chart in a document draws the marks of each pattern as shapes: lines, circles or rects in a clip path of its shape. A group with `rotate(<angle>)` turns the marks about the origin of the chart. Tile (i, j) of a pattern starts at (i × pitch, j × the height of the tile) before the turn, as the tiles of a `<pattern>` with `patternUnits="userSpaceOnUse"` start. A line runs across the whole shape, and a dot or a square stands at the center of `### Patterns` in each tile, with the second row of a staggered tile. The shapes are paths in the PDF, so a printer draws each mark at its own resolution. Each mark takes `currentColor`, and the document is in `ink`.
- The labels on the chart follow `### Labels on the chart`, in `small`: a value in `ink` at 500, and the name of a value on an axis in `ink-muted`. A grid line is a 1 px `rule` line, and the base line is a 1 px `ink` line. Each shape has a 1 px edge in `paper`, as `### Edges` asks.
- A printed chart has no tooltip, no button and no legend that a person clicks. A stacked chart prints its legend as a list. Each value stands on the chart or in a table of the document, because a person cannot point at a shape on paper.
- A chart is at most 174 mm wide, the width of the text box of A4, and it takes `break-inside: avoid`.
- **A chart with no script.** A renderer that runs no script cannot draw a chart of LayerChart, because such a chart renders on the client only (see `### Rendering`). So the product draws the chart as plain SVG on its server: the rectangles, the lines and the text, and the marks as shapes. The sample report of the preview draws its chart in this way.
- **A chart in a headless browser.** A renderer that runs the scripts of the page, such as a headless browser, can draw a chart of the app in light mode. It prints after the chart has drawn. Such a chart fills its shapes with `<pattern>` elements, and `page.pdf()` fires `beforeprint`, so the listener of `### A screen page in print` draws the marks as shapes. Without that listener the chart page of the preview printed 140 images of 72 dpi, and with it none (measured). A document that the product keeps or sends draws its chart with the marks as shapes.

The sample report prints its chart in the pattern style with the marks as shapes. At 300 dpi in gray, each of its six patterns has pixels at full ink, 21.00:1, and the patterns cover 6.3% to 36.0% of their bars at full ink. Patterns 1 and 5 cover 19.4% and 19.5%, so they differ by the angle of their lines, and not by their gray. On the screen, the shapes of patterns 2, 4 and 6 draw the same pixels as the patterns of `chart-patterns.svg`. The turned lines of patterns 1, 3 and 5 differ in their smoothing only, by at most 46 of 255 (measured at 1x and 2x).

### A screen page in print

A person can print any screen of the app, such as a list or a dashboard, with the print dialog of the browser. The page then prints its content, and not its controls. A screen page is no document, so it keeps the tokens of its theme, and it sets no `@page` size: the person picks the paper in the dialog.

- **The shell does not print.** The sidebar, the top bar, a toolbar, the filter row, the pagination, a button, a toast and the controls of a pane take `print:hidden`, the `print` variant of Tailwind. The content then takes the whole width of the paper. The tab bar and the foot bar of band 1 take `print:hidden` too. The CSS takes band 1 on a column of paper narrower than 768 px. The header of band 1 prints its title, and its buttons take `print:hidden`. An inner screen hides its back button in print, so its title takes `print:pl-3`. The title then prints where the title of a top-level screen prints (measured). A control that holds content still prints, such as a row that opens an item, a field of a form or the head of a group. The header also takes `print:h-auto print:border-b-0`, so a header with only buttons takes no room. A `checkbox`, a `radio-group-item`, a `switch` and a `slider-thumb` each have an invisible area past the box, so they take `print:after:hidden`. On A4 of 794 px, the area of a `switch` at the end of a row made the page 805 px wide (measured). So the whole page printed at 98.6% of its size. To check the width, read `document.documentElement.clientWidth` in the listener of the `matchMedia('print')` query: on A4 with no margin it is 794 px.
- **A detail pane.** On paper narrower than 1280 px, the CSS takes a band in which the pane stands over the list. So the pane takes `print:hidden`, and the list prints whole. A person prints an item from its full page. The item column of the work form follows the same rule in band 2 and below: it takes `print:max-lg:hidden`, and the list prints whole.
- **A shell of the height of the screen.** A shell that takes the height of the screen, such as with `h-svh`, takes `print:h-auto print:overflow-visible print:[&_[class*=overflow-y-auto]]:overflow-visible`. In print, `100svh` is one column of the paper, 974 px high on A4 with the margins of `### Paper`. Without the rule, the shell cuts its list at the end of the first page. Three example lists printed 15 of 22 rows, 16 of 20 and 7 of 13. With `print:h-auto` alone, the `overflow-hidden` of the shell cut the last row of the list of 22. The third class gives each scroll box in the shell `overflow: visible`, so a scroll box needs no class of its own. A scroll box with `min-h-0 flex-1` takes its height from the flex layout before Chromium breaks the pages. Content that a page break pushes down, such as a card that moves to the next page, then falls past the end of the box. Without the third class, 8 example screens lost text, such as the lower lines of a board card that moved to page 2 (measured). With the three classes, no scroll box cut a word on 175 example screens and states, from 1920 and 390 px (measured). The third class finds a scroll box by `overflow-y-auto` in its classes, so a scroll box in the shell takes `overflow-y-auto` and not `overflow-auto`.
- **A card on one page.** A card prints on one page, so a card that does not fit at the end of a page moves to the next page. `stack.css` gives each `card` `break-inside: avoid` in print, so a product adds no class to `Card.Root`. The rule is in the base layer, so a utility class of a product, such as `print:break-inside-auto`, wins. Without the rule, 14 of 128 example PDFs cut a `card` at the end of a page. With the rule, none did, and each PDF kept its page count (measured). A board card takes `print:break-inside-avoid`, and `preview/src/lib/stack/Board.svelte` holds the class. Without the class, the end of page 1 cut the card with the most lines on an example board. Page 1 printed 120 px of the card, and page 2 printed the other 106 px. With the class, the card printed whole at the top of page 2. No card of that board crossed a page end in any of its seven states, from 1920 and 390 px (measured).
- **A layout that a script picks.** A component that reads a width query in script takes `ScreenQuery` in place of `MediaQuery` of `svelte/reactivity`. A STACK part reads the line of its band from `bands`, such as `bands.md.current`, and builds no width query of its own. From `beforeprint` to `afterprint`, `ScreenQuery` answers as the query does at a viewport of 658 px. That is the column of A4 portrait with the margins of `### Paper`, as Chromium lays it out (measured). On the screen, it answers as `MediaQuery`. Chromium counts the pages before the change listeners of the print layout run. So a `MediaQuery` swapped in the phone layout after the count, and Chromium kept the page count of the desktop layout. The board of an example printed 3 pages, and 2 of them were blank. With `ScreenQuery`, it printed 2 pages with the same words and no blank page (measured). The listener of `beforeprint` sets the print answer and calls `flushSync()`, so the layout changes before Chromium lays out the print. `ScreenQuery` reads one width feature in px or rem, such as `min-width: 768px` or `(width < 48rem)`, and it throws for another query. A band of widths takes two `ScreenQuery`, such as `md.current && !lg.current`. A query that is not about the width, such as `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce`, stays a `MediaQuery`. The CSS takes the real width of the paper. So on paper whose column reaches 768 px, such as A4 landscape or A4 with no margin, the CSS can take `md:`. The script then takes the phone layout. A phone layout can show one part of its content at a time, such as one column of a board. Paper has no switch, so in print that layout shows every part, and the switch does not print (see `### board`). The module adds its listeners when it loads, and Chromium runs the listeners of the window in the order that they were added. The capture phase does not change that order (measured). So the listener of `beforeprint` in the example below calls `setPrinting(true)` first, and the listener of `afterprint` calls `setPrinting(false)` first. Then the layout changes before the fills and the fit of the bullets below, whatever the order of the imports. A state that the person can change, such as an open sidebar, is a writable `$derived` of another `$derived`. The inner `$derived` reads the `ScreenQuery`, and it keeps its last screen answer while `isPrinting()` is true. A single `$derived` that reads `isPrinting()` resets the choice after each print (measured). The comment of `isPrinting()` in `preview/src/lib/stack/screen-query.svelte.js` gives the code. A product imports `ScreenQuery`, `bands` and `isPrinting` from `@stack-kit/stack/behaviour/width`. It calls `listenForPrint()` of `@stack-kit/stack/behaviour/print` once, in `onMount` of its root layout, and that function adds the two listeners of the example below.
- **Light mode.** A page in dark mode prints badly. Chromium drops the dark ground and darkens the light text. But it keeps the light marks, lines and labels of a chart, and they vanish on the white paper (measured). So the page changes to light mode for the print: a listener of `beforeprint` removes the class `dark` from `<html>`, and a listener of `afterprint` adds it again. `page.pdf()` fires both events too, and with the two listeners a dark page printed the same colors as a light page (measured). The screen shows the page in light mode while the print dialog is open, and afterprint restores the mode when the person prints or cancels.
- **Charts in the pattern style.** A page whose charts take a color style changes them to the pattern style in the same listener, and back after the print. Chromium prints a change that the listener makes at once or in a microtask, and not a change in `requestAnimationFrame` or in a timer (measured). A Svelte state changes the page in a microtask, and `flushSync()` of `svelte` applies it before the listener returns.
- **Marks as shapes.** After the mode and the style, the same listener draws the marks of each chart pattern as shapes, because Chromium prints a `<pattern>` fill as an image of 72 dpi (see `### Charts in print`). The chart library draws the shapes, so the listener finds each shape in the page. It takes each shape whose computed fill points to a `<pattern>` with `data-pattern` (see `### Patterns`). Read the computed fill and not the attribute: the top line of an area keeps the `url()` of its series in its attribute, and a class clears it (see `### Edges`). In front of the shape, the listener puts the marks of `### Charts in print` in a clip path of a copy of the shape. The marks stand in the user space of the shape, with its transform, where the tiles of its pattern stand. Then the listener sets the fill of the shape to `none`, so that the edge of the shape stays over the marks. The listener of `afterprint` removes the marks and gives each shape its fill back. A second `beforeprint` with no `afterprint` gives the fills back first, so each mark draws once. The lines of one direction are one path, and the dots or the squares of a shape are one path. With both directions of pattern 3 in one path, Chromium drew dark seams on the screen where the lines crossed (measured). The listener is in `preview/src/lib/print/pattern-fills.ts` and the marks are in `preview/src/lib/print/marks.ts`. Both files import `preview/src/lib/stack/patterns.ts`, which reads the values of the front matter from `preview/src/lib/stack/design-values.ts`, the file that `npm run theme` writes. The package ships the four files, and `listenForPrint()` adds the listener, so a product copies none of them. On the chart page of the preview it took 69 ms at 1x (measured).
- **The width of a chart.** A chart in print is at most as wide as its column on the paper, and never wider than on the screen. The chart library draws each chart svg at the size in px that it measured on the screen. It measures again only in a `ResizeObserver`, and no observer runs in the print layout. So on the chart page of the preview, on A4 with no margin, the bar chart of a pair kept its 480 px in a column of 365 px. Its right edge stood at 893 px, 99 px past the paper of 794 px, and bar Q4 and its value were not on the page (measured). A listener of `beforeprint` cannot draw the chart again at the width of its column, because the column gets its width only in the print layout. So the listener of `beforeprint` fits each chart into its column, and the listener of the next bullet then draws it again at that width. The fit gives each chart svg a `viewBox` of its size on the screen, with `preserveAspectRatio="xMinYMin meet"`. It sets the svg and the boxes of LayerChart around it to the size of the box of the chart. It sets `max-width` and `max-height` to the size of the svg on the screen. It finds each chart by the classes `lc-root-container` and `lc-layout-svg` of LayerChart 2.7.0. A version that renames them leaves each chart at its width on the screen, with no error, so a product that changes the version of LayerChart prints a chart page again. A chart that is wider than its column then shrinks to the width of the column, with its marks and its text. On a chart that draws again, the fit changes nothing. So the fit holds only where the chart does not draw again, and there its text shrinks by the same factor. Text of 12 px prints at 8 pt or more only at a factor of 0.89 or more. Without the redraw, a chart of 1,278 px on an example screen of 1,920 px printed 689 px wide on A4 with no margin. Its text of 12 px printed at 6.5 px, 4.9 pt (measured). The box of the chart keeps its height on the screen. A chart that draws again fills the box, and a chart that shrinks leaves an empty band under it. The listener of `afterprint` removes each `viewBox` and gives each box its size back. `preview/src/lib/print/chart-width.ts` holds the functions of both bullets, and it imports only `flushSync` from `svelte`. The package ships it, and `listenForPrint()` calls its functions, so a product copies nothing.
- **The text of a chart.** A chart in print draws its text at the size of its type step, and it does not scale the text. So the text of 12 px prints at 9 pt. In the listener of `beforeprint`, the page makes a `matchMedia('print')` query and adds a `change` listener to it. That listener runs in the print layout and reads the width of each column, and Chromium prints a change that it makes (measured with `page.pdf()`). A query made in `beforeprint` is the newest of the page, so its listener runs after the listeners of the other queries. Those listeners can change the layout first. On an example screen, the query `min-width: 1024px` of the shell turned the sidebar into its rail of icons in print. A listener of an older query read the column of the chart as 505 px, while the sidebar was still open. The final column was 689 px (measured). The chart library does not draw the chart again by itself there, because its `ResizeObserver` does not run in the print layout (measured). So the listener calls `measureCharts()`. It writes the size of each chart root into the state of its chart, as the binding of the root does after a resize. Then it calls `flushSync()`, and each chart draws again at the width of its column before the listener returns. A root reads at most the width of its chart on the screen. Without that limit, a full-width chart of the chart page grew from 556 px to the width of the paper on A4 with no margin. The last label of its axis then printed past the paper (measured). After the redraw, the listener draws the marks of the patterns again on the new shapes. Each chart gives its state to `chart-width.ts`. The charts of STACK, `HeatMap`, `PatternChart`, `Sparkline` and `TimeChart`, do it themselves. An own chart of a product binds `context`, and an `$effect` calls `registerChart(context)` of `@stack-kit/stack/behaviour/print`: `$effect(() => (context ? registerChart(context) : undefined));`. A chart that does not register keeps the fit of the bullet above, and `measureCharts()` writes a warning in the console. The redraw writes `_containerWidth` and `_containerHeight` of the state of LayerChart 2.7.0. So a product that changes the version of LayerChart prints a chart page again. The listener of `afterprint` gives each chart the size that it had on the screen before the print. An observer reports a size only when it differs from its last report, and that report is from the screen before the print. A chart that read its width in `afterprint` kept 878 px in a column of 638 px (measured). The sidebar of the shell had not come back yet. The chart page and the usage screen of the example printed from screens of 1,280 and 1,920 px. Each printed on A4 with no margin and with the margins of `### Paper`. The smallest text of a chart printed at 8.25 pt: the number of 11 px in the disc of an annotation. Without the redraw, it printed at 4.8 pt (measured with `pdftotext`). No chart passed the edge of the paper, and each chart had its width on the screen again after the print (measured). The print dialog was not measured.
- **No ground.** The dialog drops each ground by default (see `### Print colors`). So a selected row, a hover and a tinted badge print with no ground, and a state keeps its word.
- **A checked box.** A checked `checkbox` draws its check in the color of the text on its fill, and the print drops the fill. So in print `stack.css` draws the check in `foreground`, and a product adds no class. Without the rule, each checked box of the table showcase printed as an empty box from 1440 px. With the rule, each printed its check, from 1440 and 390 px (measured on A4).
- **A text area.** A text area prints its whole text. A text area of the kit grows with its text (`field-sizing-content`) up to the cap of a `max-h` class, such as `max-h-80`, and then scrolls. Paper cannot scroll, so `stack.css` removes the cap in print from each text area of the kit, and a product adds no class. The selector is `textarea[data-slot]`, so it also takes the text area of an `input-group`, whose `data-slot` is `input-group-control`. The rule is in the utilities layer, and its selector is more specific than a class, so it beats the `max-h` class of the product. A product that needs a cap in print adds `!` to its class, such as `print:max-h-96!`. Without the rule, the instruction of a role printed in a box of 480 px that held 544 px of text. The PDF lost its last line (measured). With the rule, no text area cut its text in the print layout, on 177 example screens and states from 1920 and 390 px (measured).
- **A code block.** A code block prints its whole text. The rule of the text area also removes the cap of each code block of the kit, `div[data-slot=code-block]`, so a product adds no class. A product that needs a cap in print adds `!` to its class, as for a text area. Without the rule, the role text of an example ran 110 px past its box of 120 px, over the next part. Nine pairs of words printed over each other. On the showcase, a box of 320 px printed 16 of its 57 lines (measured). With the rule, the PDFs of 6 pages and states of the preview lost no line of their code blocks, from 1920 and 390 px. No code block printed over other text, and a long block broke between two lines at the end of a page (measured).
- **A foot that sticks.** A foot with `sticky bottom-0`, such as the save foot of a settings section, takes `print:static`, so it ends its column in print. In print, Chromium moved such a foot up from its place, over the content or above the first page. It did so in 18 of 354 example PDFs, and the comment box of a ticket did not print (measured). A head with `sticky top-0`, such as the head of a group, kept its place in every example PDF (measured).
- **A column that a page break lengthens.** A box that cannot break, such as a text area, moves to the next page when it does not fit. The flex item that holds the box grows by that move. When the item takes `flex-1`, its flex column keeps the height that it had before the page breaks (measured in Chromium). The item then runs past the end of the column and over the content after it. On the roles screen of an example, the editor ran 271 px past its column. The save foot covered a line of the instruction (measured). So an item with `flex-1` in a column takes `print:basis-auto` when content follows the column. With the class, the foot printed under the instruction (measured). A flex column that only stacks its items can take `print:block` in place of the class, as the columns of a board do (see `### board`). With the rules of the text area, the foot and the column, the PDF held each line of each text area on 177 example screens and states, from 1920 and 390 px (measured).
- **An open dialog.** An open `dialog`, `alert-dialog`, `sheet`, `drawer`, `dropdown-menu`, `context-menu` or `popover` holds a question or a task, and not the content of the page. A person prints the page, so the page under the layer prints, and the layer does not. In print, `stack.css` hides the content of each, and the scrim of each one that has a scrim. A product adds no class. The rule comes after the utilities of Tailwind, so it beats the `grid` or `flex` class of the content. A product that prints a layer on purpose gives its content `print:grid!`. Without the rule, the PDF held the buttons of the layer, and a fixed layer printed over the page on each page (measured). With the rule, the PDF of each of 7 example states with an open layer held the text of its page with no layer. Each state printed from 1920 and 390 px (measured). An open sheet of the showcase printed on 40 of 44 pages, and an open drawer on 41 of 44. An open dropdown menu printed on 8 of 44 pages, and an open context menu on 6. With the rule, each PDF held the text of the page with no layer (measured). The content of a `select` stays outside the rule: Bits UI sets `display: flex` in its style attribute, which no rule of a layer beats, and an open select printed its list (measured). The scroll lock of a modal dialog, `overflow: hidden` on `<body>`, cut nothing in print (measured).
- A table of a screen page keeps its `<thead>`, so its head repeats on each page.

```js
import { drawFillsAsShapes, restoreFills } from './print/pattern-fills';
import { fitChartsToColumns, measureCharts, restoreChartSizes, restoreChartWidths } from './print/chart-width';
import { setPrinting } from './stack/screen-query.svelte.js';

const root = document.documentElement;
let wasDark = false;
let prev = '';
let printQuery;
function inPrintLayout(event) {
  if (!event.matches) return;
  restoreFills();
  restoreChartSizes();
  measureCharts();
  fitChartsToColumns();
  drawFillsAsShapes();
}
addEventListener('beforeprint', () => {
  setPrinting(true);
  wasDark = root.classList.contains('dark');
  prev = root.style.colorScheme;
  root.classList.remove('dark');
  root.style.colorScheme = 'light';
  drawFillsAsShapes();
  fitChartsToColumns();
  printQuery?.removeEventListener('change', inPrintLayout);
  printQuery = matchMedia('print');
  printQuery.addEventListener('change', inPrintLayout);
});
addEventListener('afterprint', () => {
  setPrinting(false);
  printQuery?.removeEventListener('change', inPrintLayout);
  printQuery = undefined;
  restoreFills();
  restoreChartSizes();
  restoreChartWidths();
  root.style.colorScheme = prev;
  if (wasDark) {
    root.classList.add('dark');
    root.style.colorScheme = 'dark';
  }
});
```

The chart page of the preview printed on 11 pages of A4 through `page.pdf()`. Without the listener of the marks, the PDF held 140 tiling patterns, each with an image of 72 dpi, and it was 13.2 MB. With the listener, it held no pattern and no image, and it was 2.5 MB. At 300 dpi in gray, the strongest pixel of each of the eight patterns was gray 10, the `chart-ink` of default light, at 19.80:1 on paper. Without the listener, that pixel was 3.28:1 for patterns 1 and 5, 4.54:1 for pattern 6 and 2.03:1 for pattern 7, in the 12 px tile of each pattern. The dots of pattern 2 printed as squares of pixels, like the squares of pattern 4. On the screen at 1x and 2x, the shapes of the pattern style differed from the patterns in their smoothing only, by at most 61 of 255 (measured on 2026-10-25).

### Make the PDF

A product makes a PDF in one of two ways. STACK names no library, because the right renderer depends on the platform of the product.

- **The print dialog of the browser.** The person opens the document as a page and saves it with Save as PDF. The product runs nothing on its server. The result depends on the browser and on the settings of the dialog: the paper, the margins, the head and the foot of the browser, and the grounds. A head and a foot of `@page` print only in a browser that draws margin boxes. The product never holds the file, so it cannot store it, attach it to an email or sign it.
- **A renderer on the server.** The product renders the same page on its server and stores the PDF, such as in R2. Every person gets the same file, and the product can send it, archive it and sign it. The renderer costs memory and time for each document.
  - A headless browser reads the same HTML and CSS as the page, and it runs the scripts, so a chart of the app draws. It needs much memory, so on Cloudflare it runs in a separate Worker (see `architecture/cloudflare.md`).
  - A renderer with no browser builds the PDF from HTML and CSS, or from code. It runs no script, so a chart is plain SVG with the marks as shapes (see `### Charts in print`). Its support of CSS differs from a browser, such as for a margin box, `oklch()` and a grid. So the product renders the sample report of the preview with it, and compares each page with the PDF of Chromium.
- **Which way.** A document that the product keeps or sends, such as an invoice, comes from a renderer on the server, because it is the same file each time. A page that a person prints for their own use, such as a list, takes the print dialog.
- **The title and the language.** `<title>` holds the title of the document, and Chromium writes it into the PDF as the title of the file (measured). `<html lang>` holds the language of the document.
- **A tagged PDF.** A renderer on the server writes a tagged PDF, which holds the structure of the document, so that a screen reader reads the PDF in order. `page.pdf()` writes one with `tagged: true`, with the language of `<html lang>`, and `outline: true` adds the `<h1>` and the `<h2>` headings as bookmarks (measured).

### Print template

The CSS of a document, with the strings of the sample report in its margin boxes. The preview renders the sample report on `/print` with this block as it stands, so the block and the preview never differ. A product copies the block, writes its own strings into the margin boxes, and changes `size: A4` to `size: letter` for Letter. For a PDF of its server, it also changes `'Inter Variable'` to `'Inter'` in both `font-family` lines, and it loads the static faces (see `### Print type`). The preview loads the variable face only, so its sample report holds Type 3 fonts. The document is a `<main class="document">` in a page with no other content. A chart in it draws its marks as shapes (see `### Charts in print`).

| Class | Element | Use |
|---|---|---|
| `document` | `<main>` | The document: `paper`, `ink`, the face and the `body` step |
| `doc-issuer` | `<p>` | The name of the issuer, at 600 |
| `doc-note` | `<p>`, `<span>`, `<figcaption>` | `small` in `ink-muted`: the address of the issuer, the label and the change of a key figure, a caption and a note |
| `doc-title` | `<h1>` | The title of the document |
| `doc-facts` | `<dl>` | The facts, with a `<dt>` and a `<dd>` for each fact |
| `doc-section` | `<h2>` | The heading of a part |
| `doc-figures` | `<div>` | The row of the key figures, with a `<div>` for each figure and `doc-figure` on the figure |
| `doc-chart` | `<figure>` | A chart. The parts of its svg take `doc-chart-bar`, `doc-chart-value`, `doc-chart-label`, `doc-chart-grid` and `doc-chart-base`. |
| `doc-table` | `<table>` | A table. A cell of a number takes `doc-number`, a cell of an identifier takes `doc-id`, the last `<tbody>` takes `doc-totals`, and the row of the total takes `doc-total`. |
| `doc-tabular` | Any element | Tabular figures, such as on a date or on the number part of an identifier |

```css
@page {
  size: A4;
  margin: 20mm 18mm;
  font-family: 'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif;
  font-size: 8pt;
  line-height: 11pt;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
  color: #595959;
  @top-left { content: "Invoices of October 2026"; }
  @top-right { content: "REP-2026-10"; }
  @bottom-left { content: "Northfield Ltd · 4 Grain Square · Riverton 4100"; }
  @bottom-right { content: "Page " counter(page) " of " counter(pages); }
}
@page :first {
  @top-left { content: none; }
  @top-right { content: none; }
}
.document {
  background: #ffffff;
  color: #000000;
  font-family: 'Inter Variable', ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif;
  font-size: 10pt;
  line-height: 14pt;
  font-weight: 400;
  font-optical-sizing: none;
}
.document :where(h1, h2, p, dl, dd, figure, table) {
  margin: 0;
}
.doc-issuer {
  font-weight: 600;
}
.doc-note {
  font-size: 8pt;
  line-height: 11pt;
  color: #595959;
}
.doc-title {
  margin-top: 16pt;
  font-size: 18pt;
  line-height: 24pt;
  font-weight: 600;
  letter-spacing: -0.01em;
  font-variation-settings: "opsz" 32;
}
.doc-facts {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: max-content 1fr;
  column-gap: 12pt;
  margin-top: 12pt;
  break-inside: avoid;
}
.doc-facts dt {
  font-size: 8pt;
  line-height: 14pt;
  color: #595959;
}
.doc-facts dd:not(.doc-id) {
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
}
.doc-section {
  margin: 20pt 0 6pt;
  font-size: 11pt;
  line-height: 15pt;
  font-weight: 600;
  break-after: avoid;
}
.doc-figures {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: repeat(4, 1fr);
  column-gap: 12pt;
  padding: 8pt 0;
  border-block: 1px solid #b7b7b7;
  break-inside: avoid;
}
.doc-figure {
  font-size: 16pt;
  line-height: 20pt;
  font-weight: 600;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
  font-variation-settings: "opsz" 32;
}
.doc-chart {
  break-inside: avoid;
}
.doc-chart figcaption {
  margin-bottom: 4pt;
}
.doc-chart svg {
  display: block;
}
.doc-chart-bar {
  stroke: #ffffff;
  stroke-width: 1px;
}
.doc-chart-value {
  font-size: 8pt;
  font-weight: 500;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
  fill: #000000;
}
.doc-chart-label {
  font-size: 8pt;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
  fill: #595959;
}
.doc-chart-grid {
  stroke: #b7b7b7;
  stroke-width: 1px;
}
.doc-chart-base {
  stroke: #000000;
  stroke-width: 1px;
}
.doc-table {
  width: 100%;
  border-collapse: collapse;
}
.doc-table tr {
  break-inside: avoid;
}
.doc-table :is(th, td) {
  padding: 4pt 8pt 4pt 0;
  text-align: start;
  vertical-align: top;
}
.doc-table :is(th, td):last-child {
  padding-inline-end: 0;
}
.doc-table thead th {
  padding-top: 0;
  font-size: 8pt;
  line-height: 11pt;
  font-weight: 500;
  color: #595959;
  vertical-align: bottom;
  border-bottom: 1px solid #000000;
}
.doc-table tbody:not(.doc-totals) td {
  border-bottom: 1px solid #b7b7b7;
}
.doc-table tbody:not(.doc-totals) tr:last-child td {
  border-bottom-color: #000000;
}
.doc-table .doc-number {
  text-align: end;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
}
.doc-id {
  font-weight: 500;
}
.doc-tabular {
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
}
.doc-totals {
  break-inside: avoid;
}
.doc-totals th {
  font-weight: 400;
  text-align: end;
}
.doc-totals .doc-total > * {
  font-weight: 600;
}
.doc-table + .doc-note {
  margin-top: 8pt;
}
```
